<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> Let <i>A</i><sub><i>n</i></sub> be a symmetric random matrix whose entries on and above the diagonal are independent signs, equal to 1 with fixed probability $`p\in(0,1)\setminus\{1/2\}`$. We prove that $`\mathbb P(\det A_n=0)=(p^2+(1-p)^2+o(1))^n`$. The rate is attained by the event that two rows agree.

- **C1** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): For every fixed $p\in(0,1)\setminus\{1/2\}$, $$\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb P\bigl(\det A_n^{(p)}=0\bigr)^{1/n} =p^2+(1-p)^2.$$
  - Explanation: The singularity probability has the collision rate of biased signs at exponential scale. The bias is fixed, and the error may depend on it.
- **C2** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-corank-reduction)): For the symmetric sign matrix $A=A_n^{(p)}$, $$\mathbb P(\det A=0) \le 2^{o(n)}\mathbb P(\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}A=n-1)+2^{-\Omega_p(n^{6/5})}.$$
  - Explanation: Diagonal flips reduce singularity to corank one with subexponential loss; large corank is negligible at the relevant scale.
- **C3** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-compressible)): For every $\epsilon>0$, there are fixed $\lambda,\delta\in(0,1/4)$ such that, for all sufficiently large $n$, the following event has probability at most $2^{(-d_*+\epsilon)n}$: the matrix $A_n^{(p)}$ has a nonzero kernel vector whose normalized tail, for some anchor, lies within Euclidean distance $\delta$ of a vector supported on at most $\lambda n$ coordinates.
  - Explanation: Kernel vectors with normalized off-anchor tails close to sparse vectors have probability controlled at the target exponential scale.
- **C4** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-anchor-congruence)): Fix an index $a_0$ and condition on its entire row in $A_n^{(p)}$. There is a symmetric bit matrix $M$ of size $N=n-1$ with the following properties:* 1. *the entries on and above its diagonal are independent, and each has success probability $p$ or $b$;* 2. *$\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(\mathbb EM)\le2$, and the same holds for every principal minor;* 3. *$\dim\ker M=\dim\ker A$; whenever $a_0$ is an anchor of a kernel vector of $A$, its normalized tail, with some coordinate signs changed, is a unit vector in $\ker M$.
  - Explanation: Conditioning on the anchor row gives a symmetric bit Schur complement with independent upper-triangular entries, a low-rank mean, and preserved nullity.
- **C5** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-column-bound)): For every fixed $L_0\ge1$ and every sufficiently small fixed $e_0>0$, a matrix $B$ in the bit model of size $m=N-1$ satisfies, for all sufficiently large $N$, $$\mathbb P\left( B\text{ is nonsingular and } \|\mathcal S_{L_0}(B)\|>2^{e_0N} \right) \le 2^{-N(2a-10e_0)}.$$
  - Explanation: An excessively large set of compatible columns occurs with a probability cost exceeding the target singularity rate.
- **C6** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-row-counts)): For every fixed $\rho,w>0$, with probability $1-\exp(-\Omega_{\rho,w}(n^2))$, fewer than $\rho n$ rows of $A_n^{(p)}$ have a number of positive entries outside $[pn-wn,pn+wn]$.
  - Explanation: Except for a very small event, only a small proportion of original rows have atypical positive-entry counts.
- **C7** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-prime-corank)): Typically, every nonsingular bit matrix $B$ of size $m\in\mathcal I_N$ satisfies $$\operatorname{corank}_{\mathbb F_q}(B\bmod q)\le N^{3/5} \quad\text{for every prime }q.$$ Consequently, its finite abelian group $$G_B=\mathbb Z^m/B\mathbb Z^m$$ can be generated by at most $\lceil N^{3/5}\rceil$ elements. The probability of nonsingularity together with failure is at most $2^{-\Omega_p(N^{6/5})}$.
  - Explanation: Corank is controlled simultaneously over prime fields, bounding the number of generators of the discriminant group.
- **C8** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-height-cover)): Fix $D,P>0$. For all sufficiently large $N$ and every integer $0\le m\le N$, there is a list $\mathcal C_{m,N}$ of rational subspaces of $\mathbb R^m$, depending only on $m,N,D,P$, such that $$\|\mathcal C_{m,N}\|\le 2^{N^2\ell^{-P}}.$$ Every rational $F\subseteq\mathbb R^m$ with $H(F)\le\exp(N\ell^D)$ satisfies $$\min_{U\in\mathcal C_{m,N}}d(F,U)\le N\ell^{-P}.$$
  - Explanation: A fixed finite list approximates every rational subspace in the specified height range using rank distance, which does not control angles.
- **C9** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-minimizer)): For every lattice $L$ and $T>0$, the quantity $$\frac{\det M}{T^{\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}M}},\qquad M\subseteq L\text{ a sublattice},$$ attains its minimum at a saturated sublattice $L_{\min}$. It satisfies $$\det L_{\min}\le T^{\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}L_{\min}},\qquad \det M\ge T^{\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}M} \quad\text{for every sublattice }M\subseteq L/L_{\min}.$$
  - Explanation: A saturated determinant-minimizing sublattice removes directions of small normalized covolume and leaves a quotient with determinant lower bounds.
- **C10** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-projected-count)): Let $T>0$. Suppose a lattice $\Lambda$ of rank $j\ge1$ satisfies $\det M\ge T^{\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}M}$ for every sublattice $M\subseteq\Lambda$. Then, with $s_0=T/(10(\log j+2))$, $$\#\{x\in\Lambda:\lVert x\rVert_2\le R\} \le \frac32\exp(\pi R^2/s_0^2),\qquad R\ge0.$$ In particular, for $T=(\log N)^4$, $j\le N$, and $R=\sqrt N$, this count is at most $$2^{O(N/(\log N)^6)}=2^{o(N)}.$$ The latter bound also holds in rank zero, where the count equals one.
  - Explanation: Sublattice determinant lower bounds control the number of short vectors; the rank-zero convention is included.
- **C11** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-small-intersection)): Fix $D>0$ and $0<\alpha<1$, and put $\tau=\ell^{-D}$. Typically, a nonsingular bit matrix $B$ of size $m\in\mathcal I_N$ has the following property. For every lattice $K$ with $$B\mathbb Z^m\subseteq K\subseteq\mathbb Z^m, \qquad [\mathbb Z^m:K]\ge N^{\tau N},$$ and every $x\in\mathbb R^m$, $$\|(x+K)\cap\{0,1\}^m\|<2^{\alpha N}.$$ The exceptional probability is uniform over the bit models specified in Section <a href="#sec:reductions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:reductions">2</a>.
  - Explanation: With overwhelming probability, all intermediate lattices of sufficiently large index have small cube intersections in every translate, even when selected after the matrix.
- **C12** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-separated-subsets)): For each integer $u$ with $1\le \|u\|\le N^C$, there is a set $I_u\subseteq[m]$, of size at least $c_1\tau N$, such that the subset sums of $$\{uS\pi_E e_i:i\in I_u\}$$ have pairwise distance greater than $R_0\sqrt N$ modulo $S\Lambda$. The constant $c_1>0$ is fixed, independently of $u$ and $N$.
  - Explanation: A large quotient supplies many separated subset sums after the specified anisotropic scaling.
- **C13** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-dual-fourier-average)): There is a fixed $c_3>0$ such that, for every integer $1\le\|u\|\le N^C$ and every collection of real numbers $(s_i)_{i\in J}$, $$\label{eq:lattice-fourier-average} \mathbb E_y\prod_{i\in J}\|\varphi(uy_i+s_i)\|^2 \le e^{-c_3\tau N}.$$
  - Explanation: Dual Gaussian averaging forces decay of the biased characteristic-function product, uniformly over shifts and the specified integer multipliers.
- **C14** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-static-column)): For every fixed $L_0\ge1$ and $e_0>0$, there is a fixed $\zeta>0$ such that, typically for nonsingular bit matrices $B$ of size $m\in\mathcal I_N$, $$W_2(B)\le\zeta \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \|\mathcal S_{L_0}(B)\|\le2^{e_0N}.$$
  - Explanation: A small layer tail potential bounds the number of admissible columns outside a typical exception.
- **C15** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-pairing-colors)): Let $B$ be a nonsingular symmetric integer matrix. For each prime $l$, put $f_l=b_{l,3}$. The perfect pairing on $G_{B,l}$ admits an orthogonal decomposition $$G_{B,l}=U_l\oplus V_l,$$ where $U_l$ consists of the blocks with cyclic exponents strictly greater than $f_l$, and the exponent of $V_l$ divides $l^{f_l}$. There are at most two cyclic factors in $U_l$. The isotropic elements of $G_{B,l}$ can be colored with $C_l$ colors satisfying $$\label{eq:primary-color-count} C_l\le1+8b_{l,1}, \qquad C_l\le2^{b_{l,2}-b_{l,3}}\quad\text{if \(l\) is odd},$$ such that two elements of one color satisfy $$\label{eq:primary-color-pairing} l^{f_l}\lambda_B(x_{U_l},y_{U_l})=0 \quad\text{in }\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z.$$ If $X\subset G_B$ consists of isotropic elements with one fixed color at every prime, put $H=\langle x-y:x,y\in X\rangle$. Then the image of the *restricted* pairing map $$\label{eq:restricted-pairing-map} H\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(H,\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z), \qquad u\longmapsto\lambda_B(u,\cdot)\|_H,$$ has order at most $N^{NJ_B}$.
  - Explanation: Primary colors control mutual pairings and the image of the pairing restricted to the subgroup of monochromatic differences.
- **C16** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-color-subgroup)): For every prime $l$, each primary color in Lemma <a href="#lem:pairing-colors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pairing-colors">15</a> is contained in a subgroup of $G_{B,l}$ whose index is at least $l^{b_{l,2}-b_{l,3}}$.
  - Explanation: Each primary color lies in a subgroup whose index reflects the gap between the second and third cyclic exponents.
- **C17** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-color-cost)): Uniformly over nonsingular symmetric bit matrices of size at most $N$, the following hold as $N\to\infty$.* 1. *If $T_2(B)\ge\sigma$, there is a set of primes $\mathcal P$ such that $$\sum_{l\in\mathcal P}(b_{l,2}-b_{l,3})\nu_l\ge\sigma, \qquad \sum_{l\in\mathcal P}\log_2 C_l=o(N).$$* 2. *If $T_2(B)<\sigma$, then $\sum_l\log_2 C_l=o(N)$.
  - Explanation: The entropy needed to specify colors is subexponential in both layer-weight regimes.
- **C18** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-robust-extraction)): If $\varnothing\ne\mathcal A\subset\{0,1\}^m$ and $\rho>0$, there is an affine subspace $E$ for which the nonempty set $\mathcal V=\mathcal A\cap E$ is $\rho$-robust on its affine span and satisfies $$\log_2\|\mathcal V\|\ge\log_2\|\mathcal A\|-\rho m.$$
  - Explanation: Affine restriction extracts a robust subset with a controlled loss of logarithmic cardinality.
- **C19** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-robust-image)): Fix $D,C_0>0$ and put $\rho=\ell^{-D}$. Let $\varnothing\ne\mathcal V\subset\{0,1\}^m$, $m\le N$, be $\rho$-robust on its affine span $F$, of dimension $r$. Let $\Phi:F\to\mathbb R^d$ be an injective rational affine map such that $$\lVert\Phi(v)-\Phi(w)\rVert_2\le C_0\sqrt N \qquad(v,w\in\mathcal V).$$ Then the integer span of its image differences is a rank-$r$ lattice of covolume at most $\exp(O_{D,C_0}(N\ell))$. Also $H(\mathop{\mathrm{span}}(\mathcal V-\mathcal V))\le\exp(O_D(N\ell))$. The covolumes in rank zero are one.
  - Explanation: A rational affine embedding of a robust cube subset with bounded differences generates a lattice of controlled covolume and a low-height span.
- **C20** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-pairing-determinant)): Let $L$ be a lattice spanning a Euclidean space $V$, and let $q$ be a symmetric bilinear form with $q(L,L)\subset\mathbb Q$. Set $$K=L\cap\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}q, \qquad S=V\cap(\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}q)^\perp.$$ If the image of $L\to\operatorname{Hom}(L,\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z)$, $x\mapsto q(x,\cdot)\bmod\mathbb Z$, has order $t$, then $$\label{eq:pairing-det} \|\det_{\mathrm{orth}}(q\|_S)\| \ge\frac{(\det K)^2}{t(\det L)^2}.$$ The determinant on the left is in an orthonormal basis of $S$.
  - Explanation: The image order of a rational pairing gives a lower bound for its determinant on the orthogonal complement of its radical.
- **C21** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-graph-comparison)): Fix $L_0\ge1$ and $\xi,w>0$. Suppose that $B$ is a nonsingular symmetric bit matrix of size $m\in\mathcal I_N$, with $T_2(B)<\sigma$, and that the following hold:* 1. *for every rational $F\subseteq\mathbb R^m$ of height at most $\exp(N\ell^2)$, $\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(B^{-1}\|_F)\ge\dim F-\xi N$;* 2. *at most $\xi N$ eigenvalues of $B$ lie in $[-w\sqrt N,w\sqrt N]$.* *Then $$\|\mathcal S_{L_0}(B)\|\le 2^{(2J_B+2\xi+o(1))N},$$ where the error tends to zero uniformly over these matrices, for fixed $L_0,\xi,w$.
  - Explanation: An arithmetic determinant lower bound and spectral compression bound the dimension of a large monochromatic column subset.
- **C22** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-inverse-rank)): For every fixed $D>0$ and $\xi>0$, typically every nonsingular bit matrix $B$ satisfies, simultaneously for all rational subspaces $F\subseteq\mathbb R^m$ with $H(F)\le\exp(N\ell^D)$, $$\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(B^{-1}\|_F)\ge\dim F-\xi N.$$
  - Explanation: Inverse bilinear forms retain nearly full rank on all rational subspaces in the specified height range simultaneously.
- **C23** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-small-spectrum)): For every fixed $\xi>0$, there is $w_\xi>0$, depending also on $p$, such that typically a bit matrix $B$ has at most $\xi N$ eigenvalues, counted with multiplicity, in $$[-w_\xi\sqrt N,w_\xi\sqrt N].$$ Nonsingularity is not required. The exceptional probability is at most $\exp(-c_{p,\xi}N^2)$.
  - Explanation: A low-rank mean and common entry variance allow a small-spectrum estimate without requiring nonsingularity.
- **C24** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-path-deletion)): Let $B$ be a nonsingular symmetric bit matrix of positive size. The empty principal minor has determinant one, and its admissible column set consists of the unique empty column. Either some one-coordinate principal deletion is nonsingular, or a two-coordinate principal deletion gives a nonsingular matrix $C$ for which both deleted cross columns satisfy $$v_i^{\mathsf T}C^{-1}v_i\in\{0,1\}, \qquad \lVert C^{-1}v_i\rVert_\infty\le1 \quad(i=1,2).$$ In particular, whenever the sizes belong to $\mathcal I_N$, the two-coordinate move satisfies Equation <a href="#eq:path-gate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-gate">[eq:path-gate]</a>.
  - Explanation: A nonsingular symmetric bit matrix admits a nonsingular principal deletion; when a paired deletion is needed, the deleted columns satisfy the quadratic and norm gates.
- **C25** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-path-algebra)): Let $C$ be a nonsingular symmetric bit matrix of size $m\le N$.* 1. *Let $\pi:\mathbb Z^m\twoheadrightarrow G$ be a quotient map with $C\mathbb Z^m\subseteq\ker\pi$. For any set $\mathcal D$ of positive layers of $G$, there is a lattice $K_{\mathcal D}$, fixed by $\pi,G,\mathcal D$, such that $$\label{eq:path-retention-lattice} C\mathbb Z^m\subseteq K_{\mathcal D}\subseteq\mathbb Z^m, \qquad [\mathbb Z^m:K_{\mathcal D}]=N^{Nq(\mathcal D)}, \quad q(\mathcal D)=\sum_{(l,j)\in\mathcal D}k_G(l,j)\nu_l.$$ If every layer in $\mathcal D$ is retained on quotienting by $\pi(v)$, then $v\in K_{\mathcal D}$.* 2. *Suppose the extension in Equation <a href="#eq:path-extension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-extension">[eq:path-extension]</a> also has nonsingular endpoint $B$. Then $k_B(l,j)\le k_C(l,j)+h$ for every layer. Writing $\mathcal R_i$ for the retained layers in $G_{i-1}\to G_i$, for every integer $s\ge h$ we have $$\label{eq:path-tail-recursion} W_s(B)\le W_{s+h}(C) +2\sum_{i=1}^h \sum_{\substack{(l,j)\in\mathcal R_i\\ k_{G_{i-1}}(l,j)\ge s+1-h}}\nu_l.$$
  - Explanation: Retention constraints define intermediate lattices, while principal extensions satisfy layer-height interlacing and a tail recursion.
- **C26** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-path-retention-cost)): Typically for nonsingular $C$ with size in $\mathcal I_N$, conditional on $C$, the probability that an independent principal extension of either width is costly is at most $$\label{eq:path-retention-cost} p_N:=2^{-N(a-2e_0)}.$$ For one specified column, the probability that its retained weight is at least $\sigma$ obeys the same bound, also after exposing the preceding column of a width-two extension. Also, typically, when $W_2(C)\le\zeta$, the width-two gate has conditional probability at most $p_N^2$.
  - Explanation: Retaining substantial layer weight has nearly the full point-probability cost of a column; a gated paired extension from a small-tail start incurs the product cost.
- **C27** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-path-dissociation)): Let $C$ be a nonsingular bit matrix of size $m\le N$, and let $C\mathbb Z^m\subseteq K\subseteq\mathbb Z^m$ have index $N^{qN}$. If $v$ has independent bit coordinates, each with maximum atom at most $b$, then $$\label{eq:path-fixed-mask-cost} \mathbb P(v\in K)\le b^{2qN/3}.$$
  - Explanation: Dissociated coordinate classes convert a lattice index into an upper bound on the probability that an independent bit column belongs to the lattice.
- **C28** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-path-heavy-cost)): Fix $0<\eta<1$, and put $q_{\min}=\eta/(100\log N)$. There is a constant $c_p>0$ such that, uniformly for $q_{\min}\le q\le1/2$, $$\label{eq:path-heavy-cost} \mathbb P\bigl(Q_{\rm hi}\in[q,2q)\mid\text{previous exposure}\bigr) \le2^{-c_pqN}.$$ This holds whenever the previous exposure fixes a nonsingular start $C$ of size in $\mathcal I_N$, and a quotient map to $G$ whose kernel contains $C\mathbb Z^m$, while leaving the tested bits independent with success probabilities $p$ or $b$.
  - Explanation: Heavy retained layers have an exponential probability cost uniformly over the specified weight range and admissible previous exposures.
- **C29** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-path-high-tail)): For every fixed $0<\eta<1$, each fixed path satisfies $$\label{eq:path-high-tail} \mathbb P\!\left(\mathcal A, \max_{H_0-5R\le t\le H_0} W_R(B_t)>\eta\right) \le2^{-\Omega_{p,\eta}(NR)}.$$
  - Explanation: An exponential-moment argument removes paths carrying a large high-layer tail anywhere in the terminal window.
- **C30** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-path-window)): Consider $5R$ consecutive nonsingular principal extensions of widths one or two, satisfying their gates. Suppose all their nodes have $W_R\le\eta$ and the terminal node has $W_2>\zeta$. For all sufficiently large $N$, either at least two distinct steps are costly, or a width-two step starts at a node with $W_2\le\zeta$.
  - Explanation: A terminal large tail with small high-layer tails forces distinct costly steps or a gated paired extension from a small-tail start.

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-minimizer",
        "exact_quote": "For every lattice $L$ and $T>0$, the quantity $$\\frac{\\det M}{T^{\\mathop{\\mathrm{rank}}M}},\\qquad M\\subseteq L\\text{ a sublattice},$$ attains its minimum at a saturated sublattice $L_{\\min}$.         "anchor": "stmt-lem-projected-count",
        "exact_quote": "Let $T>0$. Suppose a lattice $\\Lambda$ of rank $j\\ge1$ satisfies $\\det M\\ge T^{\\mathop{\\mathrm{rank}}M}$ for every sublattice $M\\subseteq\\Lambda$.     {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-pairing-colors",
        "exact_quote": "Let $B$ be a nonsingular symmetric integer matrix. For each prime $l$, put $f_l=b_{l,3}$. The perfect pairing on $G_{B,l}$ admits an orthogonal decomposition $$G_{B,l}=U_l\\oplus V_l,$$ where $U_l$ consists of the blocks with cyclic exponents strictly greater than $f_l$, and the exponent of $V_l$ divides $l^{f_l}$. There are at most two cyclic factors in $U_l$. The isotropic elements of $G_{B,l}$ can be colored with $C_l$ colors satisfying $$\\label{eq:primary-color-count}\n C_l\\le1+8b_{l,1},\n \\qquad C_l\\le2^{b_{l,2}-b_{l,3}}\\quad\\text{if \\(l\\) is odd},$$ such that two elements of one color satisfy $$\\label{eq:primary-color-pairing}\n l^{f_l}\\lambda_B(x_{U_l},y_{U_l})=0\n \\quad\\text{in }\\mathbb Q/\\mathbb Z.$$ If $X\\subset G_B$ consists of isotropic elements with one fixed color at every prime, put $H=\\langle x-y:x,y\\in X\\rangle$. Then the image of the *restricted* pairing map $$\\label{eq:restricted-pairing-map}\n H\\longrightarrow\\operatorname{Hom}(H,\\mathbb Q/\\mathbb Z),\n \\qquad u\\longmapsto\\lambda_B(u,\\cdot)|_H,$$ has order at most $N^{NJ_B}$.",
        "source_id": "canonical:PM-239.2"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-pairing-colors",
          "exact_quote": "Let $B$ be a nonsingular symmetric integer matrix. For each prime $l$, put $f_l=b_{l,3}$. Then the image of the *restricted* pairing map $$\\label{eq:restricted-pairing-map}\n H\\longrightarrow\\operatorname{Hom}(H,\\mathbb Q/\\mathbb Z),\n \\qquad u\\longmapsto\\lambda_B(u,\\cdot)|_H,$$ has order at most $N^{NJ_B}$.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-color-cost",
        "exact_quote": "Uniformly over nonsingular symmetric bit matrices of size at most $N$, the following hold as $N\\to\\infty$.*\n\n1.  *If $T_2(B)\\ge\\sigma$, there is a set of primes $\\mathcal P$ such that $$\\sum_{l\\in\\mathcal P}(b_{l,2}-b_{l,3})\\nu_l\\ge\\sigma,\n      \\qquad \\sum_{l\\in\\mathcal P}\\log_2 C_l=o(N).$$*\n\n2.  *If $T_2(B)<\\sigma$, then $\\sum_l\\log_2 C_l=o(N)$.",
        "source_id": "canonical:PM-239.2"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-color-cost",

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-robust-image",
        "exact_quote": "Fix $D,C_0>0$ and put $\\rho=\\ell^{-D}$. Let $\\varnothing\\ne\\mathcal V\\subset\\{0,1\\}^m$, $m\\le N$, be $\\rho$-robust on its affine span $F$, of dimension $r$.         "exact_quote": "Let $L$ be a lattice spanning a Euclidean space $V$, and let $q$ be a symmetric bilinear form with $q(L,L)\\subset\\mathbb Q$.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-path-deletion",
        "exact_quote": "Let $B$ be a nonsingular symmetric bit matrix of positive size. The empty principal minor has determinant one, and its admissible column set consists of the unique empty column. Either some one-coordinate principal deletion is nonsingular, or a two-coordinate principal deletion gives a nonsingular matrix $C$ for which both deleted cross columns satisfy $$v_i^{\\mathsf T}C^{-1}v_i\\in\\{0,1\\},\n \\qquad \\lVert C^{-1}v_i\\rVert_\\infty\\le1\n \\quad(i=1,2).$$ In particular, whenever the sizes belong to $\\mathcal I_N$, the two-coordinate move satisfies Equation <a href=\"#eq:path-gate\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:path-gate\">[eq:path-gate]</a>.",
        "source_id": "canonical:PM-239.2"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-path-deletion",
          "exact_quote": "Let $B$ be a nonsingular symmetric bit matrix of positive size.           "source_id": "canonical:PM-239.2"
        }
      ],

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-path-algebra",
        "exact_quote": "Let $C$ be a nonsingular symmetric bit matrix of size $m\\le N$.*\n\n\n1.  *Let $\\pi:\\mathbb Z^m\\twoheadrightarrow G$ be a quotient map with $C\\mathbb Z^m\\subseteq\\ker\\pi$. For any set $\\mathcal D$ of positive layers of $G$, there is a lattice $K_{\\mathcal D}$, fixed by $\\pi,G,\\mathcal D$, such that $$\\label{eq:path-retention-lattice}\n     C\\mathbb Z^m\\subseteq K_{\\mathcal D}\\subseteq\\mathbb Z^m,\n     \\qquad [\\mathbb Z^m:K_{\\mathcal D}]=N^{Nq(\\mathcal D)},\n     \\quad q(\\mathcal D)=\\sum_{(l,j)\\in\\mathcal D}k_G(l,j)\\nu_l.$$ If every layer in $\\mathcal D$ is retained on quotienting by $\\pi(v)$, then $v\\in K_{\\mathcal D}$.*\n\n\n2.  *Suppose the extension in Equation <a href=\"#eq:path-extension\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:path-extension\">[eq:path-extension]</a> also has nonsingular endpoint $B$. Then $k_B(l,j)\\le k_C(l,j)+h$ for every layer. Writing $\\mathcal R_i$ for the retained layers in $G_{i-1}\\to G_i$, for every integer $s\\ge h$ we have $$\\label{eq:path-tail-recursion}\n     W_s(B)\\le W_{s+h}(C)\n       +2\\sum_{i=1}^h\n           \\sum_{\\substack{(l,j)\\in\\mathcal R_i\\\\\n                            k_{G_{i-1}}(l,j)\\ge s+1-h}}\\nu_l.$$",
        "source_id": "canonical:PM-239.2"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-path-algebra",
          "exact_quote": "Let $C$ be a nonsingular symmetric bit matrix of size $m\\le N$.*\n\n\n1.  *Let $\\pi:\\mathbb Z^m\\twoheadrightarrow G$ be a quotient map with $C\\mathbb Z^m\\subseteq\\ker\\pi$. <a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Symmetric biased sign matrix** ([source](#sec:introduction)): Fix $p\in(0,1)$. Let $A_n^{(p)}$ be the real symmetric $n\times n$ matrix whose entries on and above the diagonal are independent and satisfy $$\mathbb P(A_{ij}^{(p)}=1)=p,\qquad \mathbb P(A_{ij}^{(p)}=-1)=1-p \qquad (i\le j).$$ The entries below the diagonal are determined by symmetry.
- **Rate and entropy constants** ([source](#sec:reductions)): Negating the entire matrix replaces $p$ by $1-p$ and preserves singularity. We therefore assume throughout the proof that $0<p<1/2$, and set $$\label{eq:rate-constants} b=1-p,\qquad Q=p^2+b^2,\qquad a=-\log_2 b,\qquad d_*=-\log_2 Q,\qquad h_*=-p\log_2 p-b\log_2 b.$$
- **Anchor** ([source](#eq:anchor-size)): Given a nonzero kernel vector $v$, call an index $a_0$ with $\|v_{a_0}\|=\max_i\|v_i\|$ an *anchor*.
- **Normalized tail** ([source](#eq:anchor-size)): We normalize it by $$\lVert v_{[n]\setminus\{a_0\}}\rVert_2=1$$ and call this normalized off-anchor vector the *tail*.
- **Bit model** ([source](#eq:reference-range)): By the *bit model* we mean a symmetric matrix $B$ of size $m\in\mathcal I_N$, with independent entries on and above the diagonal, whose fixed success probabilities belong to $\{p,b\}$, and with $\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(\mathbb EB)\le2$.
- **Typical property** ([source](#eq:reference-range)): A property *holds typically* if its failure probability is $2^{-\omega(N)}$ uniformly in this sense.
- **Admissible columns** ([source](#pm-h-7)): For $L_0\ge1$ fixed and a nonsingular matrix $B$ of size $m$, define $$\label{eq:admissible-columns} \mathcal S_{L_0}(B)= \left\{z\in\{0,1\}^m: z^{\mathsf T}B^{-1}z\in\{0,1\},\ \lVert B^{-1}z\rVert_2\le L_0\sqrt N\right\}.$$
- **Lattice height** ([source](#pm-h-10)): A subspace $F\subseteq\mathbb R^m$ is *rational* if it is spanned by integer vectors; its *height* is $$H(F)=\det(F\cap\mathbb Z^m).$$
- **Rank distance** ([source](#pm-h-10)): We measure the difference between two subspaces by their *rank distance* $$d(U,V)=\dim U+\dim V-2\dim(U\cap V).$$
- **Saturated sublattice** ([source](#pm-h-11)): A sublattice $L_0\subseteq L$ is *saturated*, or *primitive*, if $L_0=L\cap\mathop{\mathrm{span}}L_0$.
- **Layer tail potential** ([source](#pm-h-17)): For $s\ge0$, define $$\label{eq:tail-potential} W_s(B)=\sum_{l,j}(k_B(l,j)-s)_+\nu_l, \qquad (x)_+=\max\{x,0\}.$$
- **Discriminant pairing** ([source](#pm-h-18)): The *discriminant pairing* of $B$ is $$\label{eq:discriminant-pairing} \lambda_B(\bar x,\bar y) =x^{\mathsf T}B^{-1}y\bmod\mathbb Z, \qquad \bar x,\bar y\in G_B.$$
- **Robust set** ([source](#pm-h-19)): A nonempty finite set $\mathcal A$ is *$\rho$-robust* on its affine span $F$ if every affine subspace $E\subset F$ of codimension $t$ satisfies $$\|\mathcal A\cap E\|\le2^{-\rho t}\|\mathcal A\|.$$
- **Costly extension** ([source](#eq:path-costly-definition)): Call an extension *costly* if, in at least one of its quotient operations, $$\label{eq:path-costly-definition} \sum_{(l,j)\in\mathcal R_i}\nu_l\ge\sigma.$$

          "anchor": "stmt-lem-graph-comparison",
          "exact_quote": "Fix $L_0\\ge1$ and $\\xi,w>0$.           "anchor": "stmt-lem-path-retention-cost",
          "exact_quote": "Typically for nonsingular $C$ with size in $\\mathcal I_N$, conditional on $C$, the probability that an independent principal extension of either width is costly is at most $$\\label{eq:path-retention-cost}\n p_N:=2^{-N(a-2e_0)}.$$ For one specified column, the probability that its retained weight is at least $\\sigma$ obeys the same bound, also after exposing the preceding column of a width-two extension. Also, typically, when $W_2(C)\\le\\zeta$, the width-two gate has conditional probability at most $p_N^2$.",
          "source_id": "canonical:PM-239.2"
        }

          "anchor": "eq:group-order",
          "exact_quote": "Hadamard’s inequality gives $$1\\le |\\det B|\\le m^{m/2}\\le N^{N/2}.$$ A union bound over all primes at most $N^{N/2}$ therefore costs at most $2^{O(N\\log N)}$, which is negligible compared with $2^{-\\Omega_p(N^{6/5})}$. Finally, $$\\dim_{\\mathbb F_q}(G_B/qG_B)\n =\\operatorname{corank}_{\\mathbb F_q}(B\\bmod q).$$ The structure theorem for finite abelian groups identifies the minimum number of generators with the maximum of these dimensions. ◻\n\n</details>\n\n\n#### Lattice height and a cover of rational subspaces\n\nA lattice is a discrete subgroup of a finite-dimensional Euclidean space, considered in its real span. Its rank is the dimension of that span, and its determinant is its Euclidean covolume. Thus, for a lattice basis $v_1,\\ldots,v_r$, $$\\det L=\\det\\bigl(\\langle v_i,v_j\\rangle_{i,j=1}^r\\bigr)^{1/2}.$$ The rank-zero lattice has determinant one. A subspace $F\\subseteq\\mathbb R^m$ is *rational* if it is spanned by integer vectors; its *height* is $$H(F)=\\det(F\\cap\\mathbb Z^m).$$ In particular, $H(0)=H(\\mathbb R^m)=1$. Every such height is at least one: the wedge of an integer lattice basis is a nonzero integer vector whose Euclidean norm is the determinant.\n\nWe measure the difference between two subspaces by their *rank distance* $$d(U,V)=\\dim U+\\dim V-2\\dim(U\\cap V).$$ This distance counts dimensions lost upon passing to their intersection; it does not measure angles. The dimension formula gives $d(U^\\perp,V^\\perp)=d(U,V)$. The next theorem supplies a fixed finite list approximating every subspace in the required height range.\n\n\n<div id=\"thm:height-cover\" class=\"theorem\">\n\n**Theorem 8** (Low-height cover, [[SymmetricRate](#ref-SymmetricRate), Theorem 4.1]). *Fix $D,P>0$. For all sufficiently large $N$ and every integer $0\\le m\\le N$, there is a list $\\mathcal C_{m,N}$ of rational subspaces of $\\mathbb R^m$, depending only on $m,N,D,P$, such that $$|\\mathcal C_{m,N}|\\le 2^{N^2\\ell^{-P}}.$$ Every rational $F\\subseteq\\mathbb R^m$ with $H(F)\\le\\exp(N\\ell^D)$ satisfies $$\\min_{U\\in\\mathcal C_{m,N}}d(F,U)\\le N\\ell^{-P}.$$*\n\n</div>\n\n\n#### Removing directions of small determinant\n\nThe next decomposition separates a lattice from the directions in which its determinant is small. A sublattice $L_0\\subseteq L$ is *saturated*, or *primitive*, if $L_0=L\\cap\\mathop{\\mathrm{span}}L_0$. A basis of a saturated sublattice extends to a basis of $L$. If $F=\\mathop{\\mathrm{span}}L_0$, orthogonal projection onto $F^\\perp$ therefore realizes the quotient as a lattice $L/L_0=\\pi_{F^\\perp}L$, and volume factorization gives $$\\label{eq:orthogonal-quotient}\n \\det L=\\det L_0\\,\\det(\\pi_{F^\\perp}L).$$ The same factorization holds for the full preimage in $L$ of any sublattice of the quotient. These facts hold in every rank, with the rank-zero conventions already specified; see [[SymmetricRate](#ref-SymmetricRate), Lemma 3.1].\n\nExtremal sublattices and their canonical filtrations also occur in Grayson’s reduction theory of Euclidean lattices [[Grayson](#ref-Grayson), Section 1]. We need only the following elementary minimization statement.\n\n\n<div id=\"lem:minimizer\" class=\"lemma\">\n\n**Lemma 9** (Determinant minimizer, [[SymmetricRate](#ref-SymmetricRate), Lemma 3.6]). *For every lattice $L$ and $T>0$, the quantity $$\\frac{\\det M}{T^{\\mathop{\\mathrm{rank}}M}},\\qquad M\\subseteq L\\text{ a sublattice},$$ attains its minimum at a saturated sublattice $L_{\\min}$. It satisfies $$\\det L_{\\min}\\le T^{\\mathop{\\mathrm{rank}}L_{\\min}},\\qquad\n \\det M\\ge T^{\\mathop{\\mathrm{rank}}M}\n \\quad\\text{for every sublattice }M\\subseteq L/L_{\\min}.$$*\n\n</div>\n\n<details id=\"proof-stmt-lem-minimizer\"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-minimizer</summary>\n\n*Proof.* The zero sublattice gives value one. For each positive rank $r$, the wedge of a basis belongs to the discrete lattice $\\bigwedge^r L$, so there are only finitely many such wedge vectors of norm at most $T^r$. Thus the normalized determinants no greater than one have only finitely many possible values, and the minimum is attained. Saturation preserves rank and can only lower the determinant. Comparing with the zero sublattice gives the first bound. For a sublattice $M$ of the quotient, its full preimage $\\widetilde M\\subseteq L$ has $$\\det\\widetilde M=\\det L_{\\min}\\det M,\n \\qquad\n \\mathop{\\mathrm{rank}}\\widetilde M=\\mathop{\\mathrm{rank}}L_{\\min}+\\mathop{\\mathrm{rank}}M.$$ Minimality applied to $\\widetilde M$ gives the second bound. ◻\n\n</details>\n\nThe point of removing $L_{\\min}$ is that lower bounds on all sublattice determinants give an upper bound on the number of short vectors in the remaining quotient.\n\n\n<div id=\"lem:projected-count\" class=\"lemma\">\n\n**Lemma 10** (Projected point count). *Let $T>0$. Suppose a lattice $\\Lambda$ of rank $j\\ge1$ satisfies $\\det M\\ge T^{\\mathop{\\mathrm{rank}}M}$ for every sublattice $M\\subseteq\\Lambda$. Then, with $s_0=T/(10(\\log j+2))$, $$\\#\\{x\\in\\Lambda:\\lVert x\\rVert_2\\le R\\}\n \\le \\frac32\\exp(\\pi R^2/s_0^2),\\qquad R\\ge0.$$ In particular, for $T=(\\log N)^4$, $j\\le N$, and $R=\\sqrt N$, this count is at most $$2^{O(N/(\\log N)^6)}=2^{o(N)}.$$ The latter bound also holds in rank zero, where the count equals one.*\n\n</div>\n\n<details id=\"proof-stmt-lem-projected-count\"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-projected-count</summary>\n\n*Proof.* For $s>0$, write $\\rho_s(\\Lambda)=\\sum_{x\\in\\Lambda}\n\\exp(-\\pi\\lVert x\\rVert_2^2/s^2)$. The Reverse Minkowski Theorem of Regev and Stephens-Davidowitz [[ReverseMinkowski](#ref-ReverseMinkowski), Theorem 1.2], in the scaled form of [[SymmetricRate](#ref-SymmetricRate), Theorem 3.4], gives $\\rho_{s_0}(\\Lambda)\\le3/2$ under precisely the stated sublattice hypotheses. Every vector of norm at most $R$ contributes at least $\\exp(-\\pi R^2/s_0^2)$, yielding the displayed count; this is [[SymmetricRate](#ref-SymmetricRate), Lemma 3.5]. Finally, $s_0\\ge c(\\log N)^3$ for $1\\le j\\le N$, which gives the claimed exponent. ◻\n\n</details>\n\n\n### A simultaneous bound for lattice intersections\n\nA lattice of large index can still contain many vertices of the cube. The constraint that it contain the columns of a random matrix changes this picture. We now show that, with overwhelmingly high probability, every such lattice has small intersection with every translate of the cube. The simultaneous quantifier is essential: the lattices used later will themselves depend on the matrix.\n\n\n<div id=\"thm:small-intersection\" class=\"theorem\">\n\n**Theorem 11** (Simultaneous lattice intersections). *Fix $D>0$ and $0<\\alpha<1$, and put $\\tau=\\ell^{-D}$. Typically, a nonsingular bit matrix $B$ of size $m\\in\\mathcal I_N$ has the following property. For every lattice $K$ with $$B\\mathbb Z^m\\subseteq K\\subseteq\\mathbb Z^m,\n \\qquad [\\mathbb Z^m:K]\\ge N^{\\tau N},$$ and every $x\\in\\mathbb R^m$, $$|(x+K)\\cap\\{0,1\\}^m|<2^{\\alpha N}.$$ The exceptional probability is uniform over the bit models specified in Section <a href=\"#sec:reductions\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"sec:reductions\">2</a>.*\n\n</div>\n\nThe proof constructs a small collection of vectors $y$ for which $By$ is integral. A large cube intersection forces these vectors to lie near a subspace of dimension strictly smaller than $N$. Their possible values can therefore be covered by a sufficiently small fixed net. For fixed representatives, Fourier estimates show that their scalar products with a random bit row are unlikely to lie simultaneously near integers. We test this on disjoint groups of entries in different rows of $B$; comparing the resulting probability with the net size proves the theorem without enumerating the lattices $K$.\n\nWe first work deterministically. Assume that $B$ is nonsingular and satisfies Lemma <a href=\"#lem:prime-corank\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:prime-corank\">7</a>, and suppose that an admissible $K$ has a coset containing at least $2^{\\alpha N}$ cube points. All constants in the construction are fixed before $N$ tends to infinity.\n\n\n#### From a large intersection to separated quotient sums\n\n\nApply Lemma <a href=\"#lem:minimizer\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:minimizer\">9</a> to $K$ with $T=(\\log N)^4$. Write $L_{\\min}$ for the resulting primitive sublattice, and set $$F=\\mathop{\\mathrm{span}}L_{\\min},\\qquad E=F^\\perp,\\qquad\n \\Lambda=\\pi_E K,\\qquad \\Pi=\\pi_E\\mathbb Z^m,$$ where $\\pi_E$ denotes orthogonal projection. Both projected groups are lattices in $E$, and $\\Lambda\\subseteq\\Pi$ has full rank there. Orthogonal factorization of covolumes gives $$\\label{eq:lattice-height-index}\n H(F)\\le\\det L_{\\min}\\le e^{4N\\ell},\n \\qquad\n \\det\\Lambda\\ge N^{\\tau N}e^{-4N\\ell}.$$ For the first inequality, $L_{\\min}$ is a full-rank sublattice of $F\\cap\\mathbb Z^m$. The second bound uses $\\det K=\\det L_{\\min}\\det\\Lambda$.\n\nDifferences of cube points in the chosen coset belong to $K$ and have norm at most $\\sqrt N$. Lemma <a href=\"#lem:projected-count\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:projected-count\">10</a> therefore bounds the number of their distinct projections into $\\Lambda$ by $2^{o(N)}$. Some fiber contains $2^{\\alpha N-o(N)}$ cube points. Every affine translate of $F$ contains at most $2^{\\dim F}$ cube points: project injectively onto a set of $\\dim F$ independent coordinate functionals. Thus $$\\label{eq:lattice-large-fiber}\n \\dim F\\ge\\alpha N/2$$ for sufficiently large $N$. This is the dimension saving that will make the eventual net smaller than the collection of row constraints.\n\nChoose a fixed $P>D+5$. Since $H(F)\\le e^{N\\ell^2}$ for large $N$, Theorem <a href=\"#thm:height-cover\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"thm:height-cover\">8</a>, with height exponent $2$, gives a list member $V$ with $d(F,V)\\le N\\ell^{-P}$. Put $U=V^\\perp$. Orthocomplementation preserves rank distance, so $d(E,U)\\le N\\ell^{-P}$, and $U$ belongs to a fixed list of at most $2^{N^2\\ell^{-P}}$ subspaces. In particular, $$\\label{eq:lattice-cover-dimension}\n \\dim U\\le(1-\\alpha/3)N,\n \\qquad W_0=E\\cap U,\n \\qquad r=\\dim E-\\dim W_0\\le N\\ell^{-P}.$$ Rank distance controls the dimension of an exact intersection; it does not assert that the two subspaces have small Euclidean angle. To obtain vectors close to $U$, we will suppress the remaining $r$ directions by a linear change of scale.\n\n\n\nFix $0<\\gamma<\\alpha/20$, and choose $C>10$ so large that $$\\label{eq:lattice-entropy-margin}\n \\Delta:=(C-3)(1-2\\gamma)-(C+4)(1-\\alpha/3)>0.$$ This is possible because $\\alpha/3-2\\gamma>0$. Define a positive self-adjoint map $S:E\\to E$ by $$\\label{eq:lattice-scale}\n S=N^2 I\\quad\\hbox{on }W_0,\n \\qquad\n S=N^{-C-5}I\\quad\\hbox{on }E\\cap W_0^\\perp.$$ The large scale in $W_0$ will separate quotient sums. The small scale on its complement will later force the dual vectors close to $U$.\n\n\n\nWe need a covolume bound that applies even to sublattices of deficient rank. Since $F\\cap\\mathbb Z^m$ is primitive in $\\mathbb Z^m$, $$\\label{eq:lattice-projection-covolume}\n \\det\\Pi=H(F)^{-1}.$$ More generally, every sublattice $L\\subseteq\\mathbb Z^j\\oplus\\Pi$, for any $j\\ge0$, satisfies $$\\label{eq:lattice-lift-bound}\n \\det L\\ge H(F)^{-1}.$$ To see this, take the full inverse image of $L$ in $\\mathbb Z^j\\oplus\\mathbb Z^m$ under $(v,z)\\mapsto(v,\\pi_Ez)$. Its primitive kernel is $\\{0\\}\\oplus(F\\cap\\mathbb Z^m)$. Orthogonal quotient factorization gives covolume $H(F)\\det L$ for the inverse image, which is at least one because it is an integer lattice. This argument uses the actual rank of $L$ and includes rank zero.\n\n\nThe finite group $\\Pi/\\Lambda$ is a quotient of $G_B=\\mathbb Z^m/B\\mathbb Z^m$, and hence has at most $g_N=\\lceil N^{3/5}\\rceil$ generators. Its order is at least the lower bound for $\\det\\Lambda$ in Equation <a href=\"#eq:lattice-height-index\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:lattice-height-index\">[eq:lattice-height-index]</a>, by Equation <a href=\"#eq:lattice-projection-covolume\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:lattice-projection-covolume\">[eq:lattice-projection-covolume]</a> and $H(F)\\ge1$. Multiplication by a nonzero integer $u$ has a kernel of size at most $|u|^{g_N}$ on any group with $g_N$ generators. Consequently, uniformly for $1\\le |u|\\le N^C$, $$\\label{eq:lattice-multiple-index}\n |u(\\Pi/\\Lambda)|\\ge N^{\\tau N-o(\\tau N)}.$$ Here $N\\ell/\\log N=o(\\tau N)$ and $N^{3/5}=o(\\tau N)$.\n\nFor a lattice $L$ in a Euclidean space, write $\\operatorname{dist}(z,L)=\\inf_{v\\in L}\\lVert z-v\\rVert_2$. The next lemma turns the large finite quotient in Equation <a href=\"#eq:lattice-multiple-index\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:lattice-multiple-index\">[eq:lattice-multiple-index]</a> into many separated subset sums. Fix an absolute constant $R_0\\ge100$.\n\n\n<div id=\"lem:separated-subsets\" class=\"lemma\">\n\n**Lemma 12**. *For each integer $u$ with $1\\le |u|\\le N^C$, there is a set $I_u\\subseteq[m]$, of size at least $c_1\\tau N$, such that the subset sums of $$\\{uS\\pi_E e_i:i\\in I_u\\}$$ have pairwise distance greater than $R_0\\sqrt N$ modulo $S\\Lambda$. The constant $c_1>0$ is fixed, independently of $u$ and $N$.*\n\n</div>\n\n<details id=\"proof-stmt-lem-separated-subsets\"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-separated-subsets</summary>\n\n\n*Proof.* Choose a maximal such set of indices, say $i_1,\\ldots,i_j$. Adding an unselected index creates a pair of subset sums at distance at most $R_0\\sqrt N$ modulo $S\\Lambda$. The added index occurs in exactly one of them, since the old subset sums were separated. Undoing $S$, we obtain, for every $i\\in[m]$, $$\\label{eq:lattice-representations}\n u\\pi_Ee_i\\equiv\n \\sum_{h=1}^j a_{hi}u\\pi_Ee_{i_h}+\\varepsilon_i\n       \\pmod\\Lambda,\n \\qquad a_{hi}\\in\\{-1,0,1\\},\n \\qquad\\lVert S\\varepsilon_i\\rVert_2\\le R_0\\sqrt N,$$ where $\\varepsilon_i\\in\\Pi$. For the selected indices use the exact representations, with coefficient columns the standard basis and zero errors.\n\n\nLet $t$ be the dimension spanned by the errors, and choose $t$ independent ones. By Equation <a href=\"#eq:lattice-lift-bound\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:lattice-lift-bound\">[eq:lattice-lift-bound]</a>, their volume is at least $1/H(F)$. On every $t$-dimensional subspace, $S$ expands volume by at least $N^{2t-(C+7)r}$: at most $r$ singular directions have the smaller scale in Equation <a href=\"#eq:lattice-scale\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:lattice-scale\">[eq:lattice-scale]</a>. Hadamard’s inequality for the scaled errors gives $$H(F)^{-1}N^{2t-(C+7)r}\\le(R_0\\sqrt N)^t.$$ Using Equation <a href=\"#eq:lattice-height-index\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:lattice-height-index\">[eq:lattice-height-index]</a>, we conclude that $$\\label{eq:lattice-error-rank}\n t=O_C\\left(r+\\frac{N\\ell}{\\log N}\\right)=o(\\tau N).$$\n\nDefine a real linear map $M:\\mathbb R^m\\to\\mathbb R^j\\oplus E$ whose $i$th column is $(a_{1i},\\ldots,a_{ji},\\varepsilon_i)$. Its integer image $M\\mathbb Z^m$ is a sublattice of $\\mathbb Z^j\\oplus\\Pi$, of rank $s\\le j+t$. Evaluation of a representation defines the homomorphism $$\\Psi:\\mathbb Z^j\\oplus\\Pi\\longrightarrow\\Pi/\\Lambda,\n \\qquad\n \\Psi(a,v)=\\sum_{h=1}^j a_hu\\pi_Ee_{i_h}+v\\pmod\\Lambda.$$ By Equation <a href=\"#eq:lattice-representations\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:lattice-representations\">[eq:lattice-representations]</a>, $\\Psi(M\\mathbb Z^m)=u(\\Pi/\\Lambda)$. Every column of $MB$ lies in $\\ker\\Psi$, because its evaluation is $u\\pi_EBe_i\\in\\Lambda$. Moreover, $B$ is nonsingular, so $\\mathop{\\mathrm{rank}}(MB)=s$. Choose $s$ independent columns of $MB$, and let $L'$ be their integer span. Then $$|u(\\Pi/\\Lambda)|\\le[M\\mathbb Z^m:L']\n       =\\frac{\\det L'}{\\det(M\\mathbb Z^m)}.$$ Each error has ordinary norm at most $R_0N^{C+5}\\sqrt N$. Since the entries of $B$ are bounded by one, the chosen columns have norm at most $N^{C+9}$ for large $N$. Equation <a href=\"#eq:lattice-lift-bound\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:lattice-lift-bound\">[eq:lattice-lift-bound]</a> and Hadamard’s inequality therefore imply $$N^{\\tau N-o(\\tau N)}\n \\le H(F)N^{(C+9)s}\n \\le H(F)N^{(C+9)(j+t)}.$$ Together with Equation <a href=\"#eq:lattice-error-rank\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:lattice-error-rank\">[eq:lattice-error-rank]</a>, this yields $j\\ge c_1\\tau N$, for example with $c_1=1/[2(C+9)]$ and sufficiently large $N$. ◻\n\n</details>\n\nThere are only polynomially many integers $u$ under consideration. Select each coordinate independently with probability $\\gamma/2$. Binomial concentration, applied to $[m]$ and to the sets $I_u$, shows that some set $J\\subseteq[m]$ satisfies $$\\label{eq:lattice-small-coordinate-set}\n |J|\\le\\gamma N,\n \\qquad |J\\cap I_u|\\ge c_2\\tau N\n       \\quad(1\\le|u|\\le N^C),$$ with a fixed $c_2>0$. Indeed the failure probability for each $I_u$ is $\\exp(-\\Omega(\\tau N))$, which absorbs the polynomial union.\n\n\n#### Dual vectors and Fourier bounds\n\nWe have obtained separated quotient sums using only the coordinates in $J$. We now convert that separation into vectors whose scalar products with random bit columns are spread around the circle. The dual lattice is $$\\Lambda^*=\\{y\\in E:\\langle y,\\Lambda\\rangle\\subseteq\\mathbb Z\\}.$$ For every $y\\in\\Lambda^*$, symmetry of $B$ and $\\pi_E B\\mathbb Z^m\\subseteq\\Lambda$ give $$\\label{eq:lattice-dual-integrality}\n By\\in\\mathbb Z^m.$$ Sample $y\\in\\Lambda^*$ with probability proportional to $\\exp(-\\pi\\lVert S^{-1}y\\rVert_2^2)$. The factor $S^{-1}$ strongly suppresses components in $E\\cap W_0^\\perp$; Gaussian tails will therefore place the sampled vectors close to $W_0\\subseteq U$. Write $$\\varphi(t)=b+p e^{2\\pi i t}.$$\n\n\n<div id=\"lem:dual-fourier-average\" class=\"lemma\">\n\n\n**Lemma 13**. *There is a fixed $c_3>0$ such that, for every integer $1\\le|u|\\le N^C$ and every collection of real numbers $(s_i)_{i\\in J}$, $$\\label{eq:lattice-fourier-average}\n \\mathbb E_y\\prod_{i\\in J}|\\varphi(uy_i+s_i)|^2\n       \\le e^{-c_3\\tau N}.$$*\n\n</div>\n\n<details id=\"proof-stmt-lem-dual-fourier-average\"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-dual-fourier-average</summary>\n\n\n*Proof.* Let $X=\\xi-\\xi'$ where $\\xi,\\xi'$ are independent vectors of Bernoulli $p$ bits on $J$, extended by zero outside $J$. Expanding the product in Equation <a href=\"#eq:lattice-fourier-average\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:lattice-fourier-average\">[eq:lattice-fourier-average]</a> expresses its expectation as $$\\mathbb E_X\\left[e^{2\\pi i\\sum_{i\\in J}s_iX_i}\n                  \\mathbb E_y e^{2\\pi i u\\langle y,X\\rangle}\\right].$$ Poisson summation in $E$ gives $$\\label{eq:lattice-poisson-ratio}\n \\mathbb E_y e^{2\\pi i u\\langle y,X\\rangle}=f(uX),\\qquad\n f(uX)=\n \\frac{\\sum_{\\lambda\\in\\Lambda}\n       e^{-\\pi\\lVert S(\\lambda-u\\pi_EX)\\rVert_2^2}}\n      {\\sum_{\\lambda\\in\\Lambda}e^{-\\pi\\lVert S\\lambda\\rVert_2^2}}.$$ The ratio is nonnegative and at most one. Positivity follows from the displayed formula; the upper bound follows either from its characteristic-function interpretation or from the Gaussian translate inequality. Thus taking absolute values permits us to discard the phases involving $s_i$ and bound the original expectation by $\\mathbb E_X f(uX)$.\n\nWe record explicitly the Gaussian estimates used here. For every lattice $L$ of rank $d$ and every $z$ in its span, Poisson summation and positivity of the Fourier transform of a Gaussian imply $$\\sum_{v\\in L}e^{-\\pi\\lVert v-z\\rVert_2^2/2}\n \\le\\sum_{v\\in L}e^{-\\pi\\lVert v\\rVert_2^2/2},\n \\qquad\n \\frac{\\sum_{v\\in L}e^{-\\pi\\lVert v\\rVert_2^2/2}}\n      {\\sum_{v\\in L}e^{-\\pi\\lVert v\\rVert_2^2}}\n \\le 2^{d/2}.$$ For the second inequality, Poisson summation expresses the ratio as $2^{d/2}$ times a ratio of dual Gaussian masses at decreasing scales. These estimates hold for arbitrary lattices, with no additional regularity assumption. For these lattice Gaussian comparisons, see Banaszczyk [[Banaszczyk](#ref-Banaszczyk)] and [[ReverseMinkowski](#ref-ReverseMinkowski), Equation (5) and Claim 2.2].\n\n\nApply them to $L=S\\Lambda$. If $\\operatorname{dist}(uS\\pi_EX,S\\Lambda)\\ge R_0\\sqrt N/3$, retaining half of each Gaussian exponent in the numerator of Equation <a href=\"#eq:lattice-poisson-ratio\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:lattice-poisson-ratio\">[eq:lattice-poisson-ratio]</a> gives $$\\label{eq:lattice-gaussian-distance}\n f(uX)\\le e^{-\\pi R_0^2N/18}2^{\\dim E/2}\n          \\le e^{-c_4N}$$ for an absolute $c_4>0$.\n\nTo bound the complementary event, condition on $\\xi'$ and on all coordinates of $\\xi$ except those in $J\\cap I_u$. By Lemma <a href=\"#lem:separated-subsets\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:separated-subsets\">12</a>, at most one of the remaining subset sums lies within distance $R_0\\sqrt N/3$ of the prescribed translate of $S\\Lambda$. Otherwise the distance between two such sums would be at most $2R_0\\sqrt N/3$. The probability of any one bit pattern is at most $b^{|J\\cap I_u|}\\le b^{c_2\\tau N}$. Combining this bound with Equation <a href=\"#eq:lattice-gaussian-distance\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:lattice-gaussian-distance\">[eq:lattice-gaussian-distance]</a> proves the result, after decreasing $c_3$. ◻\n\n</details>\n\n\n\nWe next choose several dual vectors at once. Let $$\\label{eq:lattice-number-testers}\n k=\\left\\lfloor\\kappa\\frac{\\tau N}{\\log N}\\right\\rfloor,$$ where $\\kappa>0$ will be fixed sufficiently small. For fixed $c_5>0$ small enough, there exist $y^1,\\ldots,y^k\\in\\Lambda^*$ such that $$\\label{eq:lattice-tester-geometry}\n \\lVert y^g\\rVert_2\\le R_0N^2\\sqrt N,\n \\qquad\\operatorname{dist}(y^g,U)\\le R_0N^{-C-5}\\sqrt N\n \\qquad(1\\le g\\le k),$$ $$\\label{eq:lattice-tester-fourier}\n \\prod_{i\\in J}\\left|\\varphi\\left(\\sum_{g=1}^k w_gy_i^g\\right)\\right|\n \\le e^{-c_5\\tau N}\n \\quad\\bigl(w\\in\\mathbb Z^k,\\ 1\\le\\lVert w\\rVert_\\infty\\le N^C\\bigr).$$ Here and below a distance to a subspace is Euclidean distance.\n\n\nFor completeness, sample the $k$ vectors independently according to the dual Gaussian. The Gaussian scale inequality just proved, applied on $S^{-1}\\Lambda^*$, gives $$\\mathbb P\\bigl(\\lVert S^{-1}y^g\\rVert_2>R_0\\sqrt N\\bigr)\n \\le 2^{\\dim E/2}e^{-\\pi R_0^2N/2}.$$ Outside this event, the two scales of $S$ give Equation <a href=\"#eq:lattice-tester-geometry\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:lattice-tester-geometry\">[eq:lattice-tester-geometry]</a>; for the second bound use $W_0\\subseteq U$. For a fixed nonzero $w$ in the stated range, condition on every vector except one whose coefficient is nonzero. The remaining vectors produce arbitrary shifts in Lemma <a href=\"#lem:dual-fourier-average\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:dual-fourier-average\">13</a>. Markov’s inequality bounds failure of Equation <a href=\"#eq:lattice-tester-fourier\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:lattice-tester-fourier\">[eq:lattice-tester-fourier]</a> by $$\\exp\\bigl(-(c_3-2c_5)\\tau N\\bigr).$$ There are at most $(3N^C)^k$ possible $w$. Choose $c_5<c_3/4$ and then $\\kappa$ so small that $$\\label{eq:lattice-kappa-choice}\n (C+2)\\kappa<c_5/2.$$ The union of these failures and the norm failures has probability less than one for large $N$, establishing the asserted existence. Notice the order of choices: $\\gamma,C$ were fixed first, the constants $c_1,c_2,c_3,c_5$ follow, and only then is $\\kappa$ chosen. In particular, the dependence of the Fourier constants on $C$ does not affect the positive margin in Equation <a href=\"#eq:lattice-entropy-margin\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:lattice-entropy-margin\">[eq:lattice-entropy-margin]</a>.\n\nWe have now associated to every violating lattice a set $J$ of at most $\\gamma N$ coordinates and $k$ vectors satisfying Equations <a href=\"#eq:lattice-tester-geometry\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:lattice-tester-geometry\">[eq:lattice-tester-geometry]</a>–<a href=\"#eq:lattice-tester-fourier\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:lattice-tester-fourier\">[eq:lattice-tester-fourier]</a>, together with $By^g\\in\\mathbb Z^m$. The first two conditions will allow a fixed finite description; the last condition will make such a description unlikely for a random matrix.\n\n\n#### Counting fixed descriptions and testing rows\n\n\nFix a covering-list member $U$ with $\\dim U\\le(1-\\alpha/3)N$. The region for one vector in Equation <a href=\"#eq:lattice-tester-geometry\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:lattice-tester-geometry\">[eq:lattice-tester-geometry]</a> can be covered by at most $$\\label{eq:lattice-net-size}\n N^{(C+4)\\dim U}$$ sets of diameter $4N^{-C}$. Indeed cover its projection to $U$, a ball of radius at most $R_0N^{5/2}$, at scale $N^{-C}$. The entire perpendicular thickness is $2R_0N^{-C-9/2}=o(N^{-C})$, so it requires no additional covering directions. This also proves the assertion when $\\dim U=0$, with one set.\n\n\nFix $J$ and one such cell for each of the $k$ vectors. If the cells admit a tuple satisfying Equations <a href=\"#eq:lattice-tester-geometry\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:lattice-tester-geometry\">[eq:lattice-tester-geometry]</a>–<a href=\"#eq:lattice-tester-fourier\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:lattice-tester-fourier\">[eq:lattice-tester-fourier]</a>, choose one such tuple and denote it by $\\widehat y^1,\\ldots,\n\\widehat y^k$. These choices are made independently of $B$: the conditions just imposed involve only the fixed $U,J$ and the vector coordinates. The representatives need not belong to any dual lattice. Nevertheless, existence of an actual witness in the same cells implies, for every row index $v$ and every $g$, $$\\label{eq:lattice-approximate-congruences}\n \\operatorname{dist}\\bigl((B\\widehat y^g)_v,\\mathbb Z\\bigr)\n       \\le 4N^{-C}\\sqrt N,$$ because $By^g$ is integral and $\\lVert B_{v,*}\\rVert_2\\le\\sqrt N$. This passage is what removes the dependence on the unknown lattice from the probability estimate.\n\nCondition on every entry of $B$ except those in the cross block between $J$ and $J^c$. For a row $v\\in J^c$, the remaining variables are independent bits $(B_{vi})_{i\\in J}$. For different $v\\in J^c$ these are disjoint collections of upper-triangular variables, so the row events in Equation <a href=\"#eq:lattice-approximate-congruences\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:lattice-approximate-congruences\">[eq:lattice-approximate-congruences]</a> are conditionally independent.\n\nWe bound the probability of one row event by a finite Fourier expansion. Put $M_0=\\lfloor N^{C-2}\\rfloor$, and define the nonnegative periodic kernel $$F_{M_0}(t)=\\left|\\frac1{M_0}\n                   \\sum_{h=0}^{M_0-1}e^{2\\pi i ht}\\right|^2.$$ On the interval of radius $4N^{-C}\\sqrt N$ around an integer this kernel is bounded below by a fixed positive constant, since the product of that radius and $M_0$ tends to zero. Its constant Fourier coefficient is $M_0^{-1}$, its other coefficients have magnitude at most $M_0^{-1}$, and its frequencies satisfy $|w|<M_0$. Apply the product of $k$ such kernels to the row vector $((B\\widehat y^g)_v)_{g=1}^k$.\n\n\nFor every nonzero frequency vector $w$, the absolute value of its conditional expectation is bounded by $$\\prod_{i\\in J}\n \\left|\\varphi\\left(\\sum_{g=1}^k w_g\\widehat y_i^g\\right)\\right|\n \\le e^{-c_5\\tau N}.$$ The deterministic contribution from $J^c$ has modulus one. A bit with success probability $b$ has the same characteristic-function modulus as one with success probability $p$, since $|p+b e^{it}|^2=|b+p e^{it}|^2$. All frequencies in the product kernel fall within Equation <a href=\"#eq:lattice-tester-fourier\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:lattice-tester-fourier\">[eq:lattice-tester-fourier]</a>. It follows, for an absolute constant $C_2$, that the row probability is at most $$\\begin{aligned}\n C_2^kM_0^{-k}\\left(1+(3M_0)^k e^{-c_5\\tau N}\\right)\n &\\le N^{-(C-3)k}.\n \\label{eq:lattice-row-cost}\n\\end{aligned}$$ The last inequality uses Equation <a href=\"#eq:lattice-kappa-choice\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:lattice-kappa-choice\">[eq:lattice-kappa-choice]</a>; the quantity inside parentheses is bounded, and fixed factors per coordinate are absorbed by one power of $N$.\n\nThere are $m-|J|\\ge(1-2\\gamma)N$ tested rows for large $N$. The conditional independence just noted and Equation <a href=\"#eq:lattice-row-cost\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:lattice-row-cost\">[eq:lattice-row-cost]</a> therefore bound the probability for a fixed $U,J$, and list of cells by $N^{-(C-3)(1-2\\gamma)kN}$. The cell choices cost at most $N^{(C+4)(1-\\alpha/3)kN}$ by Equation <a href=\"#eq:lattice-net-size\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:lattice-net-size\">[eq:lattice-net-size]</a>. There are at most $2^m$ choices of $J$, and at most $2^{N^2\\ell^{-P}}$ choices of $U$. Consequently the probability of any witness, together with nonsingularity and the prime-corank bound, is at most $$2^{m+N^2\\ell^{-P}}\n       \\exp(-\\Delta kN\\log N)\n \\le \\exp(-c_6\\tau N^2)$$ for a fixed $c_6>0$. Here $k\\sim\\kappa\\tau N/\\log N$, $\\Delta>0$, and $P>D$. Adding the exceptional probability from Lemma <a href=\"#lem:prime-corank\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"lem:prime-corank\">7</a> still gives $2^{-\\omega(N)}$. Every violating lattice supplied a witness in the preceding construction, so this proves Theorem <a href=\"#thm:small-intersection\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"thm:small-intersection\">11</a>.\n\n\n### Discriminant pairings and the number of admissible columns\n\nWe now relate the size of $\\mathcal S_{L_0}(B)$ to the cyclic factors of the finite group $G_B=\\mathbb Z^m/B\\mathbb Z^m$. The estimate will apply when the factors beyond the two largest in each primary component have small total logarithmic size. Layers provide convenient notation for this condition.\n\n\n#### Layers and the static estimate\n\nFor a finite abelian group $G$, write its $l$-primary part as $$G_l\\cong\\bigoplus_i\\mathbb Z/l^{b_{l,i}}\\mathbb Z,\n \\qquad b_{l,1}\\ge b_{l,2}\\ge\\cdots\\ge0,$$ where $l$ always denotes a prime and zero exponents are appended. For $j\\ge1$, define the *layer height* and its weight by $$k_G(l,j)=\\#\\{i:b_{l,i}\\ge j\\},\n \\qquad \\nu_l=\\frac{\\log_N l}{N}.$$ A layer is a pair $(l,j)$ of positive height. Write $k_B$ for $k_{G_B}$. For $G_B$, and for every quotient of $G_B$, Hadamard’s inequality gives $$\\label{eq:group-order}\n \\sum_{l,j}k_G(l,j)\\nu_l\n =\\frac{\\log_N|G|}{N}\\le\\frac12,\n \\qquad m\\le N.$$",
          "source_id": "canonical:PM-239.2"
        }

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **R1** [claim](#stmt-lem-corank-reduction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-corank-reduction): Principal minors and diagonal flips -> Reduction to corank one
- **R2** [claim](#stmt-lem-compressible) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-compressible): Nets and independent row concentration -> Sparse-tail probability bound
- **R3** [claim](#stmt-lem-anchor-congruence) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-anchor-congruence): Conditioned diagonal congruence and Schur complement -> Bit model with preserved nullity
- **R4** [claim](#stmt-thm-small-intersection) / [proof](#sec:lattice): Low-height covers, anisotropic scaling and dual Fourier tests -> Simultaneous intermediate-lattice cube bound
- **R5** [claim](#stmt-prop-static-column) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-small-spectrum-2): Primary coloring and graph determinant comparison -> Static admissible-column estimate
- **R6** [claim](#stmt-prop-column-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-path-window-2): Gated principal-minor paths and successive conditioning -> Exceptional large column-set bound
- **R7** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-row-counts-2): Typical original-row entropy and equal-row lower bound -> Matching upper and lower exponential rates

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **gated-deletion**: A nonsingular symmetric bit matrix admits a nonsingular principal deletion; when a pair is required, its deleted cross columns satisfy the quadratic and inverse norm gates.
- **fixed-bias-rate**: The singularity rate equals the collision probability for each fixed nonuniform bias.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **finite-rate**: Treat the exponential rate as an exact finite-dimension singularity probability. -> The asymptotic rate does not supply a finite-dimension error bound.
- **generic-matrices**: Apply the gated deletion to arbitrary real matrices. -> The stated quadratic gate depends on a nonsingular symmetric bit matrix.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The gated deletion proof selects a coordinate from a nonzero inverse diagonal, or a pair from a largest inverse entry, yielding a finite exact construction. This code checks that construction, not the asymptotic singularity rate.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON list of square symmetric bit matrices, size at most 4.
# PRECONDITIONS: Each matrix is nonsingular; the empty matrix is admitted.
# OUTPUTS: JSON list of deletion index lists, one per input matrix.
# TIME: O(batch_size * m^3) exact rational arithmetic operations.
# SPACE: O(batch_size * m^2) rational entries, excluding coefficient bit lengths.
# ARITHMETIC: Certified exact rational matrices from python-flint; no floating point.
# FAILURE: Invalid input raises ValueError; singular matrices are rejected.
import sys, json
from flint import fmpq_mat

def deletion(B):
    m = len(B)
    if m > 4 or any(len(r) != m for r in B):
        raise ValueError('shape')
    if any(type(B[i][j]) is not int or B[i][j] not in (0, 1) or B[i][j] != B[j][i] for i in range(m) for j in range(m)):
        raise ValueError('symmetric bits')
    if m == 0:
        return []
    Q = fmpq_mat(B)
    if Q.det() == 0:
        raise ValueError('singular')
    T = Q.inv()
    for i in range(m):
        if T[i, i] != 0:
            return [i]
    i, j = max(((i, j) for i in range(m) for j in range(i + 1, m)), key=lambda ij: abs(T[ij[0], ij[1]]))
    return [i, j]

print(json.dumps([deletion(B) for B in json.loads(sys.stdin.read())]))
```

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | Conditional use in analysis of software that generates the stated symmetric biased-sign model and regards exact singularity as failure. The theorem supplies an asymptotic rate; a concrete software implementation is ABSENT. |
| games_simulation | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | Conditional use as a mathematical reference for a simulation of the stated matrix ensemble. It supplies a limiting singularity rate, while a game mechanic, sampling implementation, and finite-dimension calibration are ABSENT. |
| tools | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | Conditional use in reviewing whether a matrix model meets the singularity theorem hypotheses. The source supplies the mathematical contract; a packaged diagnostic tool is ABSENT. |
| algorithms | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | Gated principal deletion provides a deterministic mathematical rule for reducing a nonsingular symmetric bit matrix to a nonsingular principal minor. Transfer to an algorithm requires exact arithmetic and an implementation; complexity bounds are ABSENT. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Finite abelian group layers organize the proof, but an executable storage representation with operation costs is ABSENT. The paper does not establish a data-structure contract. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | An accelerator design, hardware error model, and physical failure measurements are ABSENT. Exact singularity under the specified sign ensemble does not establish an accelerator reliability or throughput contract. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Lattice intersections and discriminant pairings are used to count admissible matrix columns. A cryptographic construction, adversary model, hardness reduction, and security guarantee are ABSENT. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | A learning architecture, training law, and generalisation claim are ABSENT. The prescribed independent biased-sign ensemble does not establish a model for learned weights. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | An optimisation objective, operational constraints, and an algorithmic performance contract are ABSENT. The lattice minimisation result is a mathematical proof input, rather than a stated optimisation solver. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | A distributed protocol, communication model, and consensus failure event are ABSENT. The source analyses singularity of a symmetric sign matrix. |
| other (Random matrix probability) | True | strong | adaptable | stated | Direct applicability to asymptotic analysis of random symmetric matrices: the main theorem determines the exponential singularity rate for every fixed nonuniform sign bias. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **L01** (hypothesis): The main rate concerns a fixed nonuniform bias in the stated open interval. Blocked use: ['Using the main theorem for unbiased signs or endpoint biases.', 'Allowing the bias to vary with dimension without a separate bound.']
- **L02** (hypothesis): The sign model requires symmetry and independent entries on and above the diagonal; the lower entries are fixed by symmetry. Blocked use: ['Transferring the rate to arbitrary entry laws.', 'Transferring the rate to dependent upper-triangular entries or independent full matrices.']
- **L03** (approximation): The theorem identifies a limiting exponential rate, rather than an exact finite-dimensional singularity probability or a multiplicative asymptotic prefactor. Blocked use: ['Substituting the limiting rate as an exact finite-dimensional failure probability.', 'Claiming a finite error tolerance or a uniform onset dimension.']
- **L04** (hypothesis): The asymptotic error may depend on the fixed bias. Blocked use: ['Claiming uniform convergence as the bias approaches the excluded unbiased value or the endpoints.']
- **L05** (hypothesis): The bit model requires the reference size range, independent upper-triangular bits with fixed permitted probabilities, and the stated bound on mean rank. Blocked use: ['Applying typical bit-model conclusions to arbitrary symmetric bit matrices or arbitrary mean arrays.']
- **L06** (approximation): Typicality is a uniform asymptotic failure-probability statement for the specified models, rather than a deterministic certificate for each matrix. Blocked use: ['Declaring an observed matrix typical from its size alone.', 'Treating a typical conclusion as a universal finite-dimensional assertion.']
- **L07** (hypothesis): Admissible columns require a nonsingular start, the exact quadratic completion condition and the inverse-image norm constraint. Blocked use: ['Applying inverse-based column tests to a singular start.', 'Counting every singular completion as admissible without the norm constraint.']
- **L08** (hypothesis): The simultaneous cube-intersection conclusion concerns intermediate lattices containing the matrix column lattice with the stated index lower bound. Blocked use: ['Applying the cube-intersection estimate to a freely chosen lattice without the containment and index hypotheses.']
- **L09** (edge_case): Rank distance measures lost dimensions at an exact intersection and does not control Euclidean angles. Blocked use: ['Using the low-height cover as an angular or metric approximation of subspaces.']
- **L10** (formal_scope): The inverse restriction is a bilinear-form restriction, so its rank is not the rank of the inverse linear map on the subspace. Blocked use: ['Replacing restricted-form rank by injectivity of the inverse operator.']
- **L11** (counterexample_scope): The rate is attained by a repeated-row event, but the upper bound controls all linear dependencies; it does not assert that every singular matrix has repeated rows. Blocked use: ['Replacing determinant singularity by repeated-row detection.']
- **L12** (unknown): ABSENT: an empirical production failure rate or a numerical solver guarantee for an application is not supplied by the stated matrix model. Blocked use: ['Publishing the theoretical singularity rate as a measured hardware or software failure rate.', 'Claiming numerical stability or a solver guarantee from exact singularity alone.']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


Let <i>A</i><sub><i>n</i></sub> be a symmetric random matrix whose entries on and above the diagonal are independent signs, equal to 1 with fixed probability $`p\in(0,1)\setminus\{1/2\}`$. We prove that $`\mathbb P(\det A_n=0)=(p^2+(1-p)^2+o(1))^n`$. The rate is attained by the event that two rows agree.
