<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

Fix $p\in(0,1)$. Let $A_n^{(p)}$ be the real symmetric $n\times n$ matrix whose entries on and above the diagonal are independent and satisfy $$\mathbb P(A_{ij}^{(p)}=1)=p,\qquad
 \mathbb P(A_{ij}^{(p)}=-1)=1-p \qquad (i\le j).$$ The entries below the diagonal are determined by symmetry. We study the exponential rate at which this matrix is singular.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *For every fixed $p\in(0,1)\setminus\{1/2\}$, $$\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb P\bigl(\det A_n^{(p)}=0\bigr)^{1/n}
   =p^2+(1-p)^2.$$*

</div>

The quantity on the right is the probability that two independent signs with this bias agree. Requiring two rows of the matrix to agree gives the same exponential rate: symmetry changes only the probability of the fixed corner where those rows meet. The upper bound shows that all linear dependencies together have this rate. The assertion concerns each fixed $p$; its error term may depend on $p$.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Historical context

The singularity problem for discrete random matrices goes back to Komlós, who proved asymptotic nonsingularity for matrices with independent uniform $0/1$ entries <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Komlos](#ref-Komlos)]. For independent uniform signs, Kahn, Komlós, and Szemerédi obtained an exponential upper bound <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[KahnKomlosSzemeredi](#ref-KahnKomlosSzemeredi)], and Tikhomirov identified the sharp exponential rate $(1/2+o(1))^n$ <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Tikhomirov](#ref-Tikhomirov)]. Jain, Sah, and Sawhney determined the singularity asymptotics for every fixed nonuniform finitely supported entry law <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[JainSahSawhneyDiscrete](#ref-JainSahSawhneyDiscrete)]. In particular, their result gives the exponential rate $p^2+(1-p)^2$ for independent biased signs. A recent preprint of Hu, Song, and Wu establishes the full asymptotic $(2+o(1))n^2 2^{-n}$ for independent uniform signs <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[HuSongWu](#ref-HuSongWu)]. All these models have independent rows; symmetry removes that independence.

Costello, Tao, and Vu proved asymptotic nonsingularity for symmetric Bernoulli matrices by developing quadratic Littlewood–Offord estimates <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[CTV](#ref-CTV)]. Their general maximum-atom theorem also covers each fixed biased sign law. In the unbiased symmetric sign model, Nguyen obtained arbitrary polynomial decay <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Nguyen](#ref-Nguyen)], Vershynin obtained a stretched exponential bound <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Vershynin](#ref-Vershynin)], and further combinatorial progress included the work of Ferber and Jain <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[FerberJain](#ref-FerberJain)]. Campos, Jenssen, Michelen, and Sahasrabudhe proved the exponential bound $\exp(-cn)$ <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[SymmetricExponential](#ref-SymmetricExponential)]. The companion manuscript <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[SymmetricRate](#ref-SymmetricRate)] proves the sharp unbiased rate $(1/2+o(1))^n$. The present paper establishes its fixed-bias analogue, at the level of the exponential rate.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The proof and its main ingredients

The main difficulty is to combine symmetry with the unequal probabilities of individual columns. Multiplying the whole matrix by $-1$ allows us to assume $p<1/2$. A prescribed column can then have probability as large as $(1-p)^{n-1}$. That bound alone is too large for the rate in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. We obtain two different gains: a structural exception requires two rare column extensions, and a nonexceptional singular matrix admits a deletion whose column has nearly its full Shannon entropy.

Section <a href="#sec:reductions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:reductions">2</a> first reduces to corank one. For a nonzero kernel vector, choose a coordinate of largest absolute value as its anchor and normalize the remaining coordinates, called its tail, to have Euclidean norm one. Tails close to sparse vectors are controlled directly at the claimed rate. For the remaining vectors, we multiply by a global sign and change coordinate signs to make the anchor row all ones. Its Schur complement is $-2$ times a symmetric matrix with entries in $\{0,1\}$. Conditional on the anchor row, these entries are independent on and above the diagonal, their probabilities belong to $\{p,1-p\}$, and their mean matrix has rank at most two. Deleting a suitable coordinate gives a nonsingular principal minor $B$. The omitted column $z$ must satisfy $$z^{\mathsf T}B^{-1}z\in\{0,1\},
 \qquad \lVert B^{-1}z\rVert_2\le L_0\sqrt n$$ for a fixed constant $L_0$. The coordinate is chosen so that the original sign column also has its typical number of positive entries. This use of the gap between entropy and collision probability follows the independent-entry strategy of Jain, Sah, and Sawhney <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[JainSahSawhneyDiscrete](#ref-JainSahSawhneyDiscrete), Section 6]. Thus each compatible column has probability governed by the binary entropy of $p$. Proposition <a href="#prop:column-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:column-bound">5</a> is the central remaining assertion: there are few such columns except on an event with a probability close to $(1-p)^{2n}$. Both bounds leave a strict margin beyond the required rate when $p\ne1/2$.

The arithmetic part follows the principal-minor and discriminant-group method of the companion paper <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[SymmetricRate](#ref-SymmetricRate)]. We use its low-height subspace cover, robust lattice bounds, and deterministic pairing and minor identities; the precise statements needed are given where they enter the proof. We strengthen the companion’s cube-intersection estimate to accommodate the biased law and to obtain nearly complete loss of cube entropy, simultaneously over intermediate lattices. For a nonsingular bit matrix $B$ of size $m$ close to a reference dimension $N$, consider $$B\mathbb Z^m\subseteq K\subseteq\mathbb Z^m.$$ Theorem <a href="#thm:small-intersection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:small-intersection">11</a> shows that, outside an event of probability smaller than every fixed exponential in $N$, an index as large as $N^{\tau N}$ forces every coset of $K$ to meet the cube in fewer than $2^{\alpha N}$ points. Here $\alpha>0$ can be any fixed constant, and $\tau$ may decrease as a fixed inverse power of $\log\log N$. The simultaneous conclusion permits the lattice to be chosen after the matrix has been observed.

The proof of this estimate, in Section <a href="#sec:lattice" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:lattice">4</a>, converts a large cube intersection into many effective linear tests from a dual lattice. A low-height cover, combined with a change of scale, confines those tests near a subspace of dimension strictly less than $N$. Their approximate locations can therefore be counted more cheaply than independent groups of entries can satisfy their integrality conditions. Gaussian sampling on the dual lattice makes this comparison effective for the biased distribution.

Sections <a href="#sec:pairings" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:pairings">5</a> and <a href="#sec:paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:paths">6</a> use the estimate in two ways. First, the finite pairing on $\mathbb Z^m/B\mathbb Z^m$ bounds the number of admissible columns when all but the two largest invariant factors of the group have small total logarithmic size. Second, paths of nonsingular principal minors control the remaining matrices. In a short terminal portion of a path, a matrix with many admissible columns forces either two rare extensions or one extension by two rare columns. Both alternatives have the required probability cost. This proves Proposition <a href="#prop:column-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:column-bound">5</a> and completes the reduction to Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

<a id="sec:reductions"></a>
### From kernel vectors to possible columns

We reduce Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> to one estimate on the number of columns that can complete a nonsingular bit matrix to a singular one. We first treat kernel vectors whose coordinates away from a largest one, after normalization, are close to a vector supported on a small set. For every remaining kernel vector, we will delete a coordinate that is large enough to control the inverse matrix and whose original row has the expected proportion of positive signs. The latter condition supplies the entropy needed for the biased law.

<a id="eq:rate-margins"></a>
Negating the entire matrix replaces $p$ by $1-p$ and preserves singularity. We therefore assume throughout the proof that $0<p<1/2$, and set <a id="eq:rate-constants"></a>$$\label{eq:rate-constants}
 b=1-p,\qquad Q=p^2+b^2,\qquad
 a=-\log_2 b,\qquad d_*=-\log_2 Q,\qquad
 h_*=-p\log_2 p-b\log_2 b.$$ Two strict inequalities will be used: $$\label{eq:rate-margins}
 2a>d_*,\qquad h_*>d_*.$$ The first follows from $b^2<Q$. For the second, strict concavity of the logarithm gives $p\log p+b\log b<\log(p^2+b^2)$. The use of column counts to exploit this entropy gap has precedents in independent-entry models <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[LitvakTikhomirov](#ref-LitvakTikhomirov), Section 2.1]; the comparison itself is explicit in Jain, Sah, and Sawhney <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[JainSahSawhneyDiscrete](#ref-JainSahSawhneyDiscrete), Section 6]. Here it will be applied to an original row selected from the kernel coordinates of a symmetric matrix. Constants may depend on $p$ and on any other parameters fixed before the dimension tends to infinity. In $O_p(\cdot)$, the implicit constant depends only on $p$. The logarithm $\log$ is natural, unless a different base is displayed, and $[m]=\{1,\ldots,m\}$.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### Corank one and tails close to sparse vectors

The diagonal-flip reduction of <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[SymmetricRate](#ref-SymmetricRate), Lemma 2.4] remains valid under the biased law, with a subexponential change in probability.

<a id="stmt-lem-corank-reduction"></a>
<div id="lem:corank-reduction" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2**. *For the symmetric sign matrix $A=A_n^{(p)}$, $$\mathbb P(\det A=0)
 \le 2^{o(n)}\mathbb P(\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}A=n-1)+2^{-\Omega_p(n^{6/5})}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-corank-reduction"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-corank-reduction</summary>

*Proof.* A symmetric matrix of rank $r$ has a nonsingular principal submatrix of size $r$. Indeed, write it as $XSX^{\mathsf T}$, where $X$ has $r$ independent columns and $S$ is nonsingular, and choose $r$ independent rows of $X$. The principal submatrix on those rows is a product of three nonsingular square matrices. For a fixed candidate principal submatrix, reveal it and its cross block. If the full matrix has the same rank, the Schur complement forces all entries in the remaining principal block. Each forced entry has conditional probability at most $b$. Consequently, with $t=\lceil n^{3/5}\rceil$, $$\mathbb P(n-\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}A\ge t)
 \le \sum_{k=t}^n\binom nk b^{k(k+1)/2}
 \le 2^{-\Omega_p(n^{6/5})}.$$

Now let $K=\ker A$ have dimension $1\le k<t$. Choose a set $I$ of $k-1$ coordinates whose restrictions to $K$ are independent linear functionals, and flip the diagonal signs on $I$. Write $D$ for the diagonal matrix of these changes. If $(A+D)y=0$, then $$x^{\mathsf T}Dy=0\qquad(x\in K).$$ The map $x\mapsto x_I$ maps $K$ onto $\mathbb R^I$, and each entry of $D$ on $I$ is nonzero. Hence $y_I=0$, and then $Ay=0$. Conversely, every vector in $K$ vanishing on $I$ is in $\ker(A+D)$. The new kernel is exactly that one-dimensional subspace.

Fix a choice of $I$ for each such matrix. The probability of a matrix divided by that of its image is at most $(b/p)^t$. Each image has at most $$\sum_{j\le t}\binom nj=2^{O(n^{3/5}\log n)}$$ preimages, since specifying the flipped coordinates recovers the original matrix. Both factors are $2^{o(n)}$, proving the lemma. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:anchor-size"></a>
Given a nonzero kernel vector $v$, call an index $a_0$ with $|v_{a_0}|=\max_i|v_i|$ an *anchor*. The vector off the anchor cannot vanish, since no column of $A$ has a zero entry. We normalize it by $$\lVert v_{[n]\setminus\{a_0\}}\rVert_2=1$$ and call this normalized off-anchor vector the *tail*. Every row equation $Av=0$ gives $$\label{eq:anchor-size}
 |v_{a_0}|
 \le \sum_{j\ne a_0}|v_j|
 \le \sqrt{n-1}.$$

<a id="stmt-lem-compressible"></a>
<div id="lem:compressible" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3** (Tails close to sparse vectors). *For every $\epsilon>0$, there are fixed $\lambda,\delta\in(0,1/4)$ such that, for all sufficiently large $n$, the following event has probability at most $2^{(-d_*+\epsilon)n}$: the matrix $A_n^{(p)}$ has a nonzero kernel vector whose normalized tail, for some anchor, lies within Euclidean distance $\delta$ of a vector supported on at most $\lambda n$ coordinates.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-compressible"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-compressible</summary>

<a id="eq:centered-norm"></a>
*Proof.* Put $\mu=2p-1$ and $W=A-\mu\mathbf1\mathbf1^{\mathsf T}$. For a sufficiently large fixed $L$, $$\label{eq:centered-norm}
 \mathbb P(\lVert W\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}>L\sqrt n)\le 2^{-4n}.$$ To see this, for a fixed unit vector $x$, write $x^{\mathsf T}Wx$ as a sum over the independent upper-triangular entries. Its coefficients have squared sum $$\sum_i x_i^4+4\sum_{i<j}x_i^2x_j^2\le2.$$ Hoeffding’s inequality <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Hoeffding](#ref-Hoeffding), Theorem 2] therefore gives a bound $2\exp(-cL^2n)$ at a constant multiple of $L\sqrt n$. A $1/4$-net of the unit sphere has at most $9^n$ points, and the usual quadratic-form net inequality bounds the operator norm by twice the maximum over this net. Taking $L$ large proves <a href="#eq:centered-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:centered-norm">[eq:centered-norm]</a>.

Fix an anchor $a_0$ and a tail support $T$, with $j=|T|\le\lambda n$. If a tail is within $\delta$ of a vector on $T$, its truncation to $T$ has error at most $\delta$. Approximate this truncation by a $\delta$-net of the unit ball in $\mathbb R^T$, and approximate the anchor coefficient on $[-\sqrt n,\sqrt n]$ with mesh at most $\delta$. The resulting vector $x$, supported on $S=T\cup\{a_0\}$, satisfies $$\lVert v-x\rVert_2\le3\delta,\qquad
 \lVert x_T\rVert_2\ge1-2\delta,\qquad
 |x_{a_0}|\ge\max_{i\in T}|x_i|-2\delta.$$ It suffices to retain net points with these last two properties. Also approximate $\sum_i v_i$ by a number $u$ with error at most $\delta$. By <a href="#eq:anchor-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:anchor-size">[eq:anchor-size]</a>, this sum lies in $[-2\sqrt n,2\sqrt n]$. For each $T$, the number of pairs $(x,u)$ is at most $(C/\delta)^j$ times a polynomial in $n$, with fixed parameters. The separate scalar approximation prevents the mean matrix from amplifying the error in the coordinate sum.

<a id="eq:net-residual"></a>
<a id="eq:two-sign-concentration"></a>
On the event $\lVert W\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\le L\sqrt n$, the equation $Av=0$ implies that one of these pairs satisfies $$\label{eq:net-residual}
 \lVert Wx+\mu u\mathbf1\rVert_2
 \le C_1L\delta\sqrt n.$$ We next show that every fixed pair has small probability of satisfying this inequality. There are fixed $w>0$ and $\delta_0>0$, depending on $p$, such that, whenever $\delta<\delta_0$, each row outside $S$ satisfies $$\label{eq:two-sign-concentration}
 \mathbb P\bigl(|(Wx)_i+\mu u|\le w\bigr)\le Q.$$ All row shifts here may be arbitrary.

Choose a small fixed coefficient threshold $u_0>0$. If some $|x_j|\ge u_0$, $j\in T$, then $|x_{a_0}|\ge u_0/2$ when $\delta$ is small enough. Condition on all signs in this row except those at $j,a_0$. For $w<u_0/4$, the interval of length $2w$ cannot contain two outcomes that differ in only one of these two signs. The allowed vertices of the two-bit square thus consist of at most one vertex or two opposite vertices. Their total probability is at most $$\max\{b^2,p^2,2pb,p^2+b^2\}=Q.$$ If instead every tail coefficient has absolute value below $u_0$, condition on the anchor sign and use the tail alone. Its centered sum has variance $$\sigma_T^2=4pb\sum_{j\in T}x_j^2,$$ bounded below by a positive constant depending on $p$. The sum of its third absolute centered moments is at most $C_pu_0\sum_{j\in T}x_j^2$. The Berry–Esseen inequality <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Berry](#ref-Berry), [Esseen](#ref-Esseen)], in its unequal-summand form <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Tyurin](#ref-Tyurin), Theorem, p. 586], bounds the uniform distribution-function error by an absolute constant times the sum of these third moments divided by $\sigma_T^3$. The error from the centered normal law with variance $\sigma_T^2$ is therefore at most $C_pu_0$. The probability of any interval of length $2w$ is therefore at most $C_p(w+u_0)$. Choose $u_0$, then $w$, small enough to make this quantity less than $Q$, and finally decrease $\delta_0$. This proves <a href="#eq:two-sign-concentration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:two-sign-concentration">[eq:two-sign-concentration]</a> in both cases.

For a fixed pair $(x,u)$, the rows outside $S$ depend on disjoint sets of upper-triangular entries, so these tests are independent. Choose a small fixed $\gamma>0$, and then $\delta<\delta_0$ so that $C_1^2L^2\delta^2/w^2<\gamma$. Inequality <a href="#eq:net-residual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:net-residual">[eq:net-residual]</a> allows at most $\gamma n$ rows to fail the interval test. Thus its probability, for fixed data, is at most $$\left(\sum_{i\le\gamma n}\binom ni\right)
 Q^{\,n-j-1-\gamma n}.$$ The norm event was used only to obtain <a href="#eq:net-residual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:net-residual">[eq:net-residual]</a>; the independent tests in this bound are not conditioned on that event.

For $t\in(0,1)$, let $H_2(t)=-t\log_2t-(1-t)\log_2(1-t)$. Union over anchors, supports, and net points gives a base-two logarithm at most $$-d_*n+
 \bigl[d_*(\lambda+\gamma)+H_2(\gamma)+H_2(\lambda)
       +\lambda\log_2(C/\delta)\bigr]n+o(n).$$ Choose $\gamma$ sufficiently small, then $\delta$ as above, then $\lambda$ sufficiently small, to make the bracket less than $\epsilon/2$. Adding <a href="#eq:centered-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:centered-norm">[eq:centered-norm]</a> and taking $n$ large proves the lemma. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### The conditioned bit model and the column estimate

For tails outside Lemma <a href="#lem:compressible" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:compressible">3</a>, we will use Schur complementation to identify the possible deleted columns. The sign-to-bit congruence is the one in <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[SymmetricRate](#ref-SymmetricRate), Lemma 2.1]; conditioning must now retain its effect on the entry probabilities.

<a id="stmt-lem-anchor-congruence"></a>
<div id="lem:anchor-congruence" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4**. *Fix an index $a_0$ and condition on its entire row in $A_n^{(p)}$. There is a symmetric bit matrix $M$ of size $N=n-1$ with the following properties:*

1.  *the entries on and above its diagonal are independent, and each has success probability $p$ or $b$;*

2.  *$\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(\mathbb EM)\le2$, and the same holds for every principal minor;*

3.  *$\dim\ker M=\dim\ker A$; whenever $a_0$ is an anchor of a kernel vector of $A$, its normalized tail, with some coordinate signs changed, is a unit vector in $\ker M$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-anchor-congruence"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-anchor-congruence</summary>

*Proof.* Relabel the fixed index as the first coordinate, put $\mu=2p-1$, $t=A_{11}$ and $u_i=A_{1i}$ for $i>1$, and set $$D=\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(1,tu_2,\ldots,tu_n).$$ The matrix $tDAD$ has first row and column equal to one. Its lower principal block is the sign matrix $C_{ij}=tu_i u_j A_{ij}$, $i,j>1$. Hence its Schur complement at the first entry is $$C-\mathbf1\mathbf1^{\mathsf T}=-2M,\qquad
 M_{ij}=\frac{1-tu_i u_j A_{ij}}2.$$ After the anchor row is fixed, the remaining original signs are independent. The displayed formula proves <span class="upright">(i)</span> and gives $$\mathbb EM=\frac12\mathbf1\mathbf1^{\mathsf T}
       -\frac{\mu t}{2}uu^{\mathsf T}.$$ This includes the diagonal, since $u_i^2=1$, and proves <span class="upright">(ii)</span>. Multiplication by $t$, diagonal congruence, and taking the Schur complement of the nonzero first entry preserve nullity. More explicitly, $Dv$ is a kernel vector of $tDAD$, and its lower coordinates are in $\ker M$. They differ from the original tail only by coordinate signs, which proves <span class="upright">(iii)</span>. ◻

</details>

For the rest of the paper, let $N$ be a reference dimension and put <a id="eq:reference-range"></a>$$\label{eq:reference-range}
 \ell=\log\log N,\qquad
 \mathcal I_N=\{m\in\mathbb Z:N-N^{7/10}\le m\le N\}.$$ By the *bit model* we mean a symmetric matrix $B$ of size $m\in\mathcal I_N$, with independent entries on and above the diagonal, whose fixed success probabilities belong to $\{p,b\}$, and with $\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(\mathbb EB)\le2$. All estimates for this model are uniform over these sizes and arrays of probabilities. A property *holds typically* if its failure probability is $2^{-\omega(N)}$ uniformly in this sense. For a property stated only for nonsingular matrices, failure means nonsingularity together with failure of the conclusion. Any fixed parameters in the property are chosen before $N$ tends to infinity.

For $L_0\ge1$ fixed and a nonsingular matrix $B$ of size $m$, define <a id="eq:admissible-columns"></a>$$\label{eq:admissible-columns}
 \mathcal S_{L_0}(B)=
 \left\{z\in\{0,1\}^m:
 z^{\mathsf T}B^{-1}z\in\{0,1\},\
 \lVert B^{-1}z\rVert_2\le L_0\sqrt N\right\}.$$ The quadratic condition is exactly the Schur-complement condition for a bit diagonal to complete $B$ to a singular matrix. The norm bound will follow from the choice of deleted coordinate. The only deferred input in the proof of the main theorem is the following estimate.

<a id="stmt-prop-column-bound"></a>
<div id="prop:column-bound" class="proposition">

**Proposition 5** (Column estimate). *For every fixed $L_0\ge1$ and every sufficiently small fixed $e_0>0$, a matrix $B$ in the bit model of size $m=N-1$ satisfies, for all sufficiently large $N$, $$\mathbb P\left(
 B\text{ is nonsingular and }
 |\mathcal S_{L_0}(B)|>2^{e_0N}
 \right)
 \le 2^{-N(2a-10e_0)}.$$*

</div>

Proposition <a href="#prop:column-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:column-bound">5</a> will be proved at the end of Section <a href="#sec:paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:paths">6</a>. Its cardinality bound is useful here because a row with approximately $pn$ positive original signs has point probability close to $2^{-h_*n}$. We next ensure that such a row can be chosen among the large kernel coordinates.

<a id="stmt-lem-row-counts"></a>
<div id="lem:row-counts" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6** (Row counts). *For every fixed $\rho,w>0$, with probability $1-\exp(-\Omega_{\rho,w}(n^2))$, fewer than $\rho n$ rows of $A_n^{(p)}$ have a number of positive entries outside $[pn-wn,pn+wn]$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-row-counts"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-row-counts</summary>

*Proof.* If at least $\rho n$ rows fail, at least half fail in the same direction. Choose a set $I$ of $k=\lceil\rho n/3\rceil$ such rows, for all sufficiently large $n$; it suffices to consider $\rho<1$. The sum of their positive-entry counts is a sum of independent upper-triangular Bernoulli variables with coefficients one or two. Its mean is $pkn$, and its coefficients have squared sum at most $2kn$. The common-direction deviation is at least $kwn$. Hoeffding’s inequality <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Hoeffding](#ref-Hoeffding), Theorem 2] bounds this probability by $2\exp(-kw^2n)$. There are at most $2^n$ sets $I$ and two directions, giving the assertion. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-row-counts-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-row-counts</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, assuming Proposition <a href="#prop:column-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:column-bound">5</a>.* Fix $\epsilon>0$, and choose $\lambda,\delta$ from Lemma <a href="#lem:compressible" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:compressible">3</a>. By Lemma <a href="#lem:corank-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:corank-reduction">2</a>, it suffices to bound the corank-one event; the matrices discarded in the corank reduction are already negligible. Discard also the event in Lemma <a href="#lem:compressible" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:compressible">3</a>.

Take any remaining normalized tail and choose a fixed $c\in(0,\delta)$. There are at least $\lambda n/2$ off-anchor coordinates with absolute value at least $c/\sqrt n$. Otherwise truncation to these coordinates would have support at most $\lambda n$ and discarded norm at most $c$, contrary to the choice of the tail. For a fixed tolerance $w_1>0$, Lemma <a href="#lem:row-counts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:row-counts">6</a> with $\rho=\lambda/4$ shows, apart from probability $\exp(-\Omega_{\lambda,w_1}(n^2))$, that some such coordinate has a row count within $w_1n$ of $pn$. Choose it as the deletion index.

Fix the anchor and deletion indices and condition on the anchor row. This does not condition on their having been chosen by a kernel vector; we will take a union bound over their indices. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:anchor-congruence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:anchor-congruence">4</a>, and denote the transformed unit tail by $v\in\ker M$, where $M$ has size $N=n-1$ and corank one. Its deletion coordinate has $|v_i|\ge c/\sqrt n$. The adjugate of a symmetric corank-one matrix is a nonzero scalar multiple of $vv^{\mathsf T}$. Thus deleting coordinate $i$ leaves a nonsingular principal minor $B$ of size $N-1$. Writing $$M=\begin{pmatrix}B&z\\z^{\mathsf T}&d\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad d\in\{0,1\},$$ the kernel equation and Schur complement give $$B^{-1}z=-\frac{v_{[N]\setminus\{i\}}}{v_i},
 \qquad z^{\mathsf T}B^{-1}z=d.$$ Consequently $z\in\mathcal S_{L_0}(B)$ for a fixed $L_0\ge\max(1,2/c)$.

<a id="eq:column-entropy"></a>
It remains to bound the probability of these possible columns under the biased law. Conditional on the anchor row, $B$ and the entries of $z$ are independent. A specified $z$ specifies the $n-2=N-1$ original off-diagonal signs of the deleted column outside the anchor. If $r$ of those signs are positive, its point probability is exactly $$p^r b^{N-1-r}.$$ The full original row has only two additional entries, its diagonal and its anchor entry. Compatibility with the selected row-count condition therefore requires $|r-pn|\le w_1n+2$. For every such $z$, $$\label{eq:column-entropy}
 p^r b^{N-1-r}
 \le 2^{-N(h_*-C_pw_1-o(1))}.$$ Indeed its negative base-two logarithm is $$(N-1)h_*+
 \bigl(r-p(N-1)\bigr)\log_2(b/p).$$ The count here concerns positive *original signs*; the success probabilities of the transformed bits need not be equal. We have not charged the deleted diagonal: dropping its requirement only enlarges the event.

On the event that the nonsingular $B$ satisfies the cardinality conclusion of Proposition <a href="#prop:column-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:column-bound">5</a>, independence and <a href="#eq:column-entropy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:column-entropy">[eq:column-entropy]</a> bound the conditional probability by $$2^{-N(h_*-e_0-C_pw_1-o(1))}.$$ The exceptional nonsingular minors have conditional probability at most $2^{-N(2a-10e_0)}$. Both bounds are uniform in the anchor row, so averaging over that row introduces no additional factor. The choices of anchor and deletion indices cost at most $n^2$.

After $L_0$ has been fixed, choose $e_0,w_1>0$ sufficiently small that $$2a-10e_0>d_*,
 \qquad h_*-e_0-C_pw_1>d_*,$$ using <a href="#eq:rate-margins" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rate-margins">[eq:rate-margins]</a>. Together with the discarded events and the subexponential corank reduction, this proves $$\limsup_{n\to\infty}\frac1n\log_2
       \mathbb P(\det A_n^{(p)}=0)\le-d_*+\epsilon.$$ Letting $\epsilon\downarrow0$ gives the required upper bound.

For the lower bound, require the first two rows to be equal. The entries in their first two coordinates must satisfy $A_{11}=A_{12}=A_{22}$, at cost $p^3+b^3$. For each $j\ge3$, the independent pair $(A_{1j},A_{2j})$ agrees with probability $Q$. These requirements involve disjoint upper-triangular entries, and hence $$\mathbb P(\det A_n^{(p)}=0)\ge(p^3+b^3)Q^{n-2}.$$ This gives the matching lower bound. Negation handles $p>1/2$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:typical"></a>
### Lattice tools and a prime-corank bound

The lattice-intersection argument of Section <a href="#sec:lattice" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:lattice">4</a> uses three inputs: a uniform bound on corank modulo primes, a finite cover of rational subspaces of low height, and a lattice decomposition that removes directions of small determinant. We prove the prime-corank bound for the biased bit model, adapting <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[SymmetricRate](#ref-SymmetricRate), Proposition 5.3], and recall the deterministic lattice results with their precise hypotheses.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Corank over all prime fields

<a id="eq:typical-corank-tail"></a>
The principal-block argument of <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[SymmetricRate](#ref-SymmetricRate), Lemmas 2.2 and 2.3] gives the following estimate over any field in which the two bit values remain distinct. If $A$ is a symmetric $d\times d$ matrix whose upper-triangular entries are independent and have atoms at most $b$, and $H$ is a fixed symmetric matrix over the same field, then $$\label{eq:typical-corank-tail}
 \mathbb P\bigl(\operatorname{corank}(A+H)\ge k\bigr)
 \le 2^d b^{k(k+1)/2},\qquad 1\le k\le d.$$ Indeed, a symmetric matrix of rank $r$ has a nonsingular principal submatrix of size $r$, over any field, including characteristic two. One way to see this is to write it as $Y C Y^{\mathsf T}$, where $Y$ has full column rank $r$ and $C$ is symmetric and nonsingular, and then choose $r$ independent rows of $Y$. For a fixed such principal block, reveal that block and all entries joining it to its complement. If the full matrix has rank $r$, Schur complementation fixes all $(d-r)(d-r+1)/2$ unrevealed entries. Their independence bounds the conditional probability by $b^{(d-r)(d-r+1)/2}$. Summing over all principal sets of size at most $d-k$ proves Equation <a href="#eq:typical-corank-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:typical-corank-tail">[eq:typical-corank-tail]</a>; the empty principal set handles rank zero.

<a id="stmt-lem-prime-corank"></a>
<div id="lem:prime-corank" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7** (Uniform prime corank). *Typically, every nonsingular bit matrix $B$ of size $m\in\mathcal I_N$ satisfies $$\operatorname{corank}_{\mathbb F_q}(B\bmod q)\le N^{3/5}
 \quad\text{for every prime }q.$$ Consequently, its finite abelian group $$G_B=\mathbb Z^m/B\mathbb Z^m$$ can be generated by at most $\lceil N^{3/5}\rceil$ elements. The probability of nonsingularity together with failure is at most $2^{-\Omega_p(N^{6/5})}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-prime-corank"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-prime-corank</summary>

*Proof.* For a fixed prime, Equation <a href="#eq:typical-corank-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:typical-corank-tail">[eq:typical-corank-tail]</a> with $k=\lfloor N^{3/5}\rfloor+1$ gives the asserted probability bound. If $B$ is nonsingular over $\mathbb R$, every prime with positive corank divides the nonzero integer $\det B$. Hadamard’s inequality gives $$1\le |\det B|\le m^{m/2}\le N^{N/2}.$$ A union bound over all primes at most $N^{N/2}$ therefore costs at most $2^{O(N\log N)}$, which is negligible compared with $2^{-\Omega_p(N^{6/5})}$. Finally, $$\dim_{\mathbb F_q}(G_B/qG_B)
 =\operatorname{corank}_{\mathbb F_q}(B\bmod q).$$ The structure theorem for finite abelian groups identifies the minimum number of generators with the maximum of these dimensions. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Lattice height and a cover of rational subspaces

A lattice is a discrete subgroup of a finite-dimensional Euclidean space, considered in its real span. Its rank is the dimension of that span, and its determinant is its Euclidean covolume. Thus, for a lattice basis $v_1,\ldots,v_r$, $$\det L=\det\bigl(\langle v_i,v_j\rangle_{i,j=1}^r\bigr)^{1/2}.$$ The rank-zero lattice has determinant one. A subspace $F\subseteq\mathbb R^m$ is *rational* if it is spanned by integer vectors; its *height* is $$H(F)=\det(F\cap\mathbb Z^m).$$ In particular, $H(0)=H(\mathbb R^m)=1$. Every such height is at least one: the wedge of an integer lattice basis is a nonzero integer vector whose Euclidean norm is the determinant.

We measure the difference between two subspaces by their *rank distance* $$d(U,V)=\dim U+\dim V-2\dim(U\cap V).$$ This distance counts dimensions lost upon passing to their intersection; it does not measure angles. The dimension formula gives $d(U^\perp,V^\perp)=d(U,V)$. The next theorem supplies a fixed finite list approximating every subspace in the required height range.

<a id="stmt-thm-height-cover"></a>
<div id="thm:height-cover" class="theorem">

**Theorem 8** (Low-height cover, <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[SymmetricRate](#ref-SymmetricRate), Theorem 4.1]). *Fix $D,P>0$. For all sufficiently large $N$ and every integer $0\le m\le N$, there is a list $\mathcal C_{m,N}$ of rational subspaces of $\mathbb R^m$, depending only on $m,N,D,P$, such that $$|\mathcal C_{m,N}|\le 2^{N^2\ell^{-P}}.$$ Every rational $F\subseteq\mathbb R^m$ with $H(F)\le\exp(N\ell^D)$ satisfies $$\min_{U\in\mathcal C_{m,N}}d(F,U)\le N\ell^{-P}.$$*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Removing directions of small determinant

The next decomposition separates a lattice from the directions in which its determinant is small. A sublattice $L_0\subseteq L$ is *saturated*, or *primitive*, if $L_0=L\cap\mathop{\mathrm{span}}L_0$. A basis of a saturated sublattice extends to a basis of $L$. If $F=\mathop{\mathrm{span}}L_0$, orthogonal projection onto $F^\perp$ therefore realizes the quotient as a lattice $L/L_0=\pi_{F^\perp}L$, and volume factorization gives <a id="eq:orthogonal-quotient"></a>$$\label{eq:orthogonal-quotient}
 \det L=\det L_0\,\det(\pi_{F^\perp}L).$$ The same factorization holds for the full preimage in $L$ of any sublattice of the quotient. These facts hold in every rank, with the rank-zero conventions already specified; see <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[SymmetricRate](#ref-SymmetricRate), Lemma 3.1].

Extremal sublattices and their canonical filtrations also occur in Grayson’s reduction theory of Euclidean lattices <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Grayson](#ref-Grayson), Section 1]. We need only the following elementary minimization statement.

<a id="stmt-lem-minimizer"></a>
<div id="lem:minimizer" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9** (Determinant minimizer, <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[SymmetricRate](#ref-SymmetricRate), Lemma 3.6]). *For every lattice $L$ and $T>0$, the quantity $$\frac{\det M}{T^{\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}M}},\qquad M\subseteq L\text{ a sublattice},$$ attains its minimum at a saturated sublattice $L_{\min}$. It satisfies $$\det L_{\min}\le T^{\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}L_{\min}},\qquad
 \det M\ge T^{\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}M}
 \quad\text{for every sublattice }M\subseteq L/L_{\min}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-minimizer"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-minimizer</summary>

*Proof.* The zero sublattice gives value one. For each positive rank $r$, the wedge of a basis belongs to the discrete lattice $\bigwedge^r L$, so there are only finitely many such wedge vectors of norm at most $T^r$. Thus the normalized determinants no greater than one have only finitely many possible values, and the minimum is attained. Saturation preserves rank and can only lower the determinant. Comparing with the zero sublattice gives the first bound. For a sublattice $M$ of the quotient, its full preimage $\widetilde M\subseteq L$ has $$\det\widetilde M=\det L_{\min}\det M,
 \qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{rank}}\widetilde M=\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}L_{\min}+\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}M.$$ Minimality applied to $\widetilde M$ gives the second bound. ◻

</details>

The point of removing $L_{\min}$ is that lower bounds on all sublattice determinants give an upper bound on the number of short vectors in the remaining quotient.

<a id="stmt-lem-projected-count"></a>
<div id="lem:projected-count" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10** (Projected point count). *Let $T>0$. Suppose a lattice $\Lambda$ of rank $j\ge1$ satisfies $\det M\ge T^{\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}M}$ for every sublattice $M\subseteq\Lambda$. Then, with $s_0=T/(10(\log j+2))$, $$\#\{x\in\Lambda:\lVert x\rVert_2\le R\}
 \le \frac32\exp(\pi R^2/s_0^2),\qquad R\ge0.$$ In particular, for $T=(\log N)^4$, $j\le N$, and $R=\sqrt N$, this count is at most $$2^{O(N/(\log N)^6)}=2^{o(N)}.$$ The latter bound also holds in rank zero, where the count equals one.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-projected-count"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-projected-count</summary>

*Proof.* For $s>0$, write $\rho_s(\Lambda)=\sum_{x\in\Lambda}
\exp(-\pi\lVert x\rVert_2^2/s^2)$. The Reverse Minkowski Theorem of Regev and Stephens-Davidowitz <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[ReverseMinkowski](#ref-ReverseMinkowski), Theorem 1.2], in the scaled form of <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[SymmetricRate](#ref-SymmetricRate), Theorem 3.4], gives $\rho_{s_0}(\Lambda)\le3/2$ under precisely the stated sublattice hypotheses. Every vector of norm at most $R$ contributes at least $\exp(-\pi R^2/s_0^2)$, yielding the displayed count; this is <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[SymmetricRate](#ref-SymmetricRate), Lemma 3.5]. Finally, $s_0\ge c(\log N)^3$ for $1\le j\le N$, which gives the claimed exponent. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:lattice"></a>
### A simultaneous bound for lattice intersections

A lattice of large index can still contain many vertices of the cube. The constraint that it contain the columns of a random matrix changes this picture. We now show that, with overwhelmingly high probability, every such lattice has small intersection with every translate of the cube. The simultaneous quantifier is essential: the lattices used later will themselves depend on the matrix.

<a id="stmt-thm-small-intersection"></a>
<div id="thm:small-intersection" class="theorem">

**Theorem 11** (Simultaneous lattice intersections). *Fix $D>0$ and $0<\alpha<1$, and put $\tau=\ell^{-D}$. Typically, a nonsingular bit matrix $B$ of size $m\in\mathcal I_N$ has the following property. For every lattice $K$ with $$B\mathbb Z^m\subseteq K\subseteq\mathbb Z^m,
 \qquad [\mathbb Z^m:K]\ge N^{\tau N},$$ and every $x\in\mathbb R^m$, $$|(x+K)\cap\{0,1\}^m|<2^{\alpha N}.$$ The exceptional probability is uniform over the bit models specified in Section <a href="#sec:reductions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:reductions">2</a>.*

</div>

The proof constructs a small collection of vectors $y$ for which $By$ is integral. A large cube intersection forces these vectors to lie near a subspace of dimension strictly smaller than $N$. Their possible values can therefore be covered by a sufficiently small fixed net. For fixed representatives, Fourier estimates show that their scalar products with a random bit row are unlikely to lie simultaneously near integers. We test this on disjoint groups of entries in different rows of $B$; comparing the resulting probability with the net size proves the theorem without enumerating the lattices $K$.

We first work deterministically. Assume that $B$ is nonsingular and satisfies Lemma <a href="#lem:prime-corank" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:prime-corank">7</a>, and suppose that an admissible $K$ has a coset containing at least $2^{\alpha N}$ cube points. All constants in the construction are fixed before $N$ tends to infinity.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### From a large intersection to separated quotient sums

<a id="eq:lattice-height-index"></a>
Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:minimizer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:minimizer">9</a> to $K$ with $T=(\log N)^4$. Write $L_{\min}$ for the resulting primitive sublattice, and set $$F=\mathop{\mathrm{span}}L_{\min},\qquad E=F^\perp,\qquad
 \Lambda=\pi_E K,\qquad \Pi=\pi_E\mathbb Z^m,$$ where $\pi_E$ denotes orthogonal projection. Both projected groups are lattices in $E$, and $\Lambda\subseteq\Pi$ has full rank there. Orthogonal factorization of covolumes gives $$\label{eq:lattice-height-index}
 H(F)\le\det L_{\min}\le e^{4N\ell},
 \qquad
 \det\Lambda\ge N^{\tau N}e^{-4N\ell}.$$ For the first inequality, $L_{\min}$ is a full-rank sublattice of $F\cap\mathbb Z^m$. The second bound uses $\det K=\det L_{\min}\det\Lambda$.

Differences of cube points in the chosen coset belong to $K$ and have norm at most $\sqrt N$. Lemma <a href="#lem:projected-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:projected-count">10</a> therefore bounds the number of their distinct projections into $\Lambda$ by $2^{o(N)}$. Some fiber contains $2^{\alpha N-o(N)}$ cube points. Every affine translate of $F$ contains at most $2^{\dim F}$ cube points: project injectively onto a set of $\dim F$ independent coordinate functionals. Thus <a id="eq:lattice-large-fiber"></a>$$\label{eq:lattice-large-fiber}
 \dim F\ge\alpha N/2$$ for sufficiently large $N$. This is the dimension saving that will make the eventual net smaller than the collection of row constraints.

Choose a fixed $P>D+5$. Since $H(F)\le e^{N\ell^2}$ for large $N$, Theorem <a href="#thm:height-cover" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:height-cover">8</a>, with height exponent $2$, gives a list member $V$ with $d(F,V)\le N\ell^{-P}$. Put $U=V^\perp$. Orthocomplementation preserves rank distance, so $d(E,U)\le N\ell^{-P}$, and $U$ belongs to a fixed list of at most $2^{N^2\ell^{-P}}$ subspaces. In particular, <a id="eq:lattice-cover-dimension"></a>$$\label{eq:lattice-cover-dimension}
 \dim U\le(1-\alpha/3)N,
 \qquad W_0=E\cap U,
 \qquad r=\dim E-\dim W_0\le N\ell^{-P}.$$ Rank distance controls the dimension of an exact intersection; it does not assert that the two subspaces have small Euclidean angle. To obtain vectors close to $U$, we will suppress the remaining $r$ directions by a linear change of scale.

<a id="eq:lattice-entropy-margin"></a>
<a id="eq:lattice-scale"></a>
Fix $0<\gamma<\alpha/20$, and choose $C>10$ so large that $$\label{eq:lattice-entropy-margin}
 \Delta:=(C-3)(1-2\gamma)-(C+4)(1-\alpha/3)>0.$$ This is possible because $\alpha/3-2\gamma>0$. Define a positive self-adjoint map $S:E\to E$ by $$\label{eq:lattice-scale}
 S=N^2 I\quad\hbox{on }W_0,
 \qquad
 S=N^{-C-5}I\quad\hbox{on }E\cap W_0^\perp.$$ The large scale in $W_0$ will separate quotient sums. The small scale on its complement will later force the dual vectors close to $U$.

<a id="eq:lattice-projection-covolume"></a>
<a id="eq:lattice-lift-bound"></a>
We need a covolume bound that applies even to sublattices of deficient rank. Since $F\cap\mathbb Z^m$ is primitive in $\mathbb Z^m$, $$\label{eq:lattice-projection-covolume}
 \det\Pi=H(F)^{-1}.$$ More generally, every sublattice $L\subseteq\mathbb Z^j\oplus\Pi$, for any $j\ge0$, satisfies $$\label{eq:lattice-lift-bound}
 \det L\ge H(F)^{-1}.$$ To see this, take the full inverse image of $L$ in $\mathbb Z^j\oplus\mathbb Z^m$ under $(v,z)\mapsto(v,\pi_Ez)$. Its primitive kernel is $\{0\}\oplus(F\cap\mathbb Z^m)$. Orthogonal quotient factorization gives covolume $H(F)\det L$ for the inverse image, which is at least one because it is an integer lattice. This argument uses the actual rank of $L$ and includes rank zero.

<a id="eq:lattice-multiple-index"></a>
The finite group $\Pi/\Lambda$ is a quotient of $G_B=\mathbb Z^m/B\mathbb Z^m$, and hence has at most $g_N=\lceil N^{3/5}\rceil$ generators. Its order is at least the lower bound for $\det\Lambda$ in Equation <a href="#eq:lattice-height-index" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lattice-height-index">[eq:lattice-height-index]</a>, by Equation <a href="#eq:lattice-projection-covolume" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lattice-projection-covolume">[eq:lattice-projection-covolume]</a> and $H(F)\ge1$. Multiplication by a nonzero integer $u$ has a kernel of size at most $|u|^{g_N}$ on any group with $g_N$ generators. Consequently, uniformly for $1\le |u|\le N^C$, $$\label{eq:lattice-multiple-index}
 |u(\Pi/\Lambda)|\ge N^{\tau N-o(\tau N)}.$$ Here $N\ell/\log N=o(\tau N)$ and $N^{3/5}=o(\tau N)$.

For a lattice $L$ in a Euclidean space, write $\operatorname{dist}(z,L)=\inf_{v\in L}\lVert z-v\rVert_2$. The next lemma turns the large finite quotient in Equation <a href="#eq:lattice-multiple-index" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lattice-multiple-index">[eq:lattice-multiple-index]</a> into many separated subset sums. Fix an absolute constant $R_0\ge100$.

<a id="stmt-lem-separated-subsets"></a>
<div id="lem:separated-subsets" class="lemma">

**Lemma 12**. *For each integer $u$ with $1\le |u|\le N^C$, there is a set $I_u\subseteq[m]$, of size at least $c_1\tau N$, such that the subset sums of $$\{uS\pi_E e_i:i\in I_u\}$$ have pairwise distance greater than $R_0\sqrt N$ modulo $S\Lambda$. The constant $c_1>0$ is fixed, independently of $u$ and $N$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-separated-subsets"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-separated-subsets</summary>

<a id="eq:lattice-representations"></a>
*Proof.* Choose a maximal such set of indices, say $i_1,\ldots,i_j$. Adding an unselected index creates a pair of subset sums at distance at most $R_0\sqrt N$ modulo $S\Lambda$. The added index occurs in exactly one of them, since the old subset sums were separated. Undoing $S$, we obtain, for every $i\in[m]$, $$\label{eq:lattice-representations}
 u\pi_Ee_i\equiv
 \sum_{h=1}^j a_{hi}u\pi_Ee_{i_h}+\varepsilon_i
       \pmod\Lambda,
 \qquad a_{hi}\in\{-1,0,1\},
 \qquad\lVert S\varepsilon_i\rVert_2\le R_0\sqrt N,$$ where $\varepsilon_i\in\Pi$. For the selected indices use the exact representations, with coefficient columns the standard basis and zero errors.

<a id="eq:lattice-error-rank"></a>
Let $t$ be the dimension spanned by the errors, and choose $t$ independent ones. By Equation <a href="#eq:lattice-lift-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lattice-lift-bound">[eq:lattice-lift-bound]</a>, their volume is at least $1/H(F)$. On every $t$-dimensional subspace, $S$ expands volume by at least $N^{2t-(C+7)r}$: at most $r$ singular directions have the smaller scale in Equation <a href="#eq:lattice-scale" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lattice-scale">[eq:lattice-scale]</a>. Hadamard’s inequality for the scaled errors gives $$H(F)^{-1}N^{2t-(C+7)r}\le(R_0\sqrt N)^t.$$ Using Equation <a href="#eq:lattice-height-index" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lattice-height-index">[eq:lattice-height-index]</a>, we conclude that $$\label{eq:lattice-error-rank}
 t=O_C\left(r+\frac{N\ell}{\log N}\right)=o(\tau N).$$

Define a real linear map $M:\mathbb R^m\to\mathbb R^j\oplus E$ whose $i$th column is $(a_{1i},\ldots,a_{ji},\varepsilon_i)$. Its integer image $M\mathbb Z^m$ is a sublattice of $\mathbb Z^j\oplus\Pi$, of rank $s\le j+t$. Evaluation of a representation defines the homomorphism $$\Psi:\mathbb Z^j\oplus\Pi\longrightarrow\Pi/\Lambda,
 \qquad
 \Psi(a,v)=\sum_{h=1}^j a_hu\pi_Ee_{i_h}+v\pmod\Lambda.$$ By Equation <a href="#eq:lattice-representations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lattice-representations">[eq:lattice-representations]</a>, $\Psi(M\mathbb Z^m)=u(\Pi/\Lambda)$. Every column of $MB$ lies in $\ker\Psi$, because its evaluation is $u\pi_EBe_i\in\Lambda$. Moreover, $B$ is nonsingular, so $\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(MB)=s$. Choose $s$ independent columns of $MB$, and let $L'$ be their integer span. Then $$|u(\Pi/\Lambda)|\le[M\mathbb Z^m:L']
       =\frac{\det L'}{\det(M\mathbb Z^m)}.$$ Each error has ordinary norm at most $R_0N^{C+5}\sqrt N$. Since the entries of $B$ are bounded by one, the chosen columns have norm at most $N^{C+9}$ for large $N$. Equation <a href="#eq:lattice-lift-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lattice-lift-bound">[eq:lattice-lift-bound]</a> and Hadamard’s inequality therefore imply $$N^{\tau N-o(\tau N)}
 \le H(F)N^{(C+9)s}
 \le H(F)N^{(C+9)(j+t)}.$$ Together with Equation <a href="#eq:lattice-error-rank" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lattice-error-rank">[eq:lattice-error-rank]</a>, this yields $j\ge c_1\tau N$, for example with $c_1=1/[2(C+9)]$ and sufficiently large $N$. ◻

</details>

There are only polynomially many integers $u$ under consideration. Select each coordinate independently with probability $\gamma/2$. Binomial concentration, applied to $[m]$ and to the sets $I_u$, shows that some set $J\subseteq[m]$ satisfies <a id="eq:lattice-small-coordinate-set"></a>$$\label{eq:lattice-small-coordinate-set}
 |J|\le\gamma N,
 \qquad |J\cap I_u|\ge c_2\tau N
       \quad(1\le|u|\le N^C),$$ with a fixed $c_2>0$. Indeed the failure probability for each $I_u$ is $\exp(-\Omega(\tau N))$, which absorbs the polynomial union.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Dual vectors and Fourier bounds

We have obtained separated quotient sums using only the coordinates in $J$. We now convert that separation into vectors whose scalar products with random bit columns are spread around the circle. The dual lattice is $$\Lambda^*=\{y\in E:\langle y,\Lambda\rangle\subseteq\mathbb Z\}.$$ For every $y\in\Lambda^*$, symmetry of $B$ and $\pi_E B\mathbb Z^m\subseteq\Lambda$ give <a id="eq:lattice-dual-integrality"></a>$$\label{eq:lattice-dual-integrality}
 By\in\mathbb Z^m.$$ Sample $y\in\Lambda^*$ with probability proportional to $\exp(-\pi\lVert S^{-1}y\rVert_2^2)$. The factor $S^{-1}$ strongly suppresses components in $E\cap W_0^\perp$; Gaussian tails will therefore place the sampled vectors close to $W_0\subseteq U$. Write $$\varphi(t)=b+p e^{2\pi i t}.$$

<a id="stmt-lem-dual-fourier-average"></a>
<div id="lem:dual-fourier-average" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:lattice-fourier-average"></a>
**Lemma 13**. *There is a fixed $c_3>0$ such that, for every integer $1\le|u|\le N^C$ and every collection of real numbers $(s_i)_{i\in J}$, $$\label{eq:lattice-fourier-average}
 \mathbb E_y\prod_{i\in J}|\varphi(uy_i+s_i)|^2
       \le e^{-c_3\tau N}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-dual-fourier-average"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-dual-fourier-average</summary>

<a id="eq:lattice-poisson-ratio"></a>
*Proof.* Let $X=\xi-\xi'$ where $\xi,\xi'$ are independent vectors of Bernoulli $p$ bits on $J$, extended by zero outside $J$. Expanding the product in Equation <a href="#eq:lattice-fourier-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lattice-fourier-average">[eq:lattice-fourier-average]</a> expresses its expectation as $$\mathbb E_X\left[e^{2\pi i\sum_{i\in J}s_iX_i}
                  \mathbb E_y e^{2\pi i u\langle y,X\rangle}\right].$$ Poisson summation in $E$ gives $$\label{eq:lattice-poisson-ratio}
 \mathbb E_y e^{2\pi i u\langle y,X\rangle}=f(uX),\qquad
 f(uX)=
 \frac{\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}
       e^{-\pi\lVert S(\lambda-u\pi_EX)\rVert_2^2}}
      {\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}e^{-\pi\lVert S\lambda\rVert_2^2}}.$$ The ratio is nonnegative and at most one. Positivity follows from the displayed formula; the upper bound follows either from its characteristic-function interpretation or from the Gaussian translate inequality. Thus taking absolute values permits us to discard the phases involving $s_i$ and bound the original expectation by $\mathbb E_X f(uX)$.

We record explicitly the Gaussian estimates used here. For every lattice $L$ of rank $d$ and every $z$ in its span, Poisson summation and positivity of the Fourier transform of a Gaussian imply $$\sum_{v\in L}e^{-\pi\lVert v-z\rVert_2^2/2}
 \le\sum_{v\in L}e^{-\pi\lVert v\rVert_2^2/2},
 \qquad
 \frac{\sum_{v\in L}e^{-\pi\lVert v\rVert_2^2/2}}
      {\sum_{v\in L}e^{-\pi\lVert v\rVert_2^2}}
 \le 2^{d/2}.$$ For the second inequality, Poisson summation expresses the ratio as $2^{d/2}$ times a ratio of dual Gaussian masses at decreasing scales. These estimates hold for arbitrary lattices, with no additional regularity assumption. For these lattice Gaussian comparisons, see Banaszczyk <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[Banaszczyk](#ref-Banaszczyk)] and <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[ReverseMinkowski](#ref-ReverseMinkowski), Equation (5) and Claim 2.2].

<a id="eq:lattice-gaussian-distance"></a>
Apply them to $L=S\Lambda$. If $\operatorname{dist}(uS\pi_EX,S\Lambda)\ge R_0\sqrt N/3$, retaining half of each Gaussian exponent in the numerator of Equation <a href="#eq:lattice-poisson-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lattice-poisson-ratio">[eq:lattice-poisson-ratio]</a> gives $$\label{eq:lattice-gaussian-distance}
 f(uX)\le e^{-\pi R_0^2N/18}2^{\dim E/2}
          \le e^{-c_4N}$$ for an absolute $c_4>0$.

To bound the complementary event, condition on $\xi'$ and on all coordinates of $\xi$ except those in $J\cap I_u$. By Lemma <a href="#lem:separated-subsets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:separated-subsets">12</a>, at most one of the remaining subset sums lies within distance $R_0\sqrt N/3$ of the prescribed translate of $S\Lambda$. Otherwise the distance between two such sums would be at most $2R_0\sqrt N/3$. The probability of any one bit pattern is at most $b^{|J\cap I_u|}\le b^{c_2\tau N}$. Combining this bound with Equation <a href="#eq:lattice-gaussian-distance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lattice-gaussian-distance">[eq:lattice-gaussian-distance]</a> proves the result, after decreasing $c_3$. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:lattice-tester-geometry"></a>
<a id="eq:lattice-tester-fourier"></a>
We next choose several dual vectors at once. Let <a id="eq:lattice-number-testers"></a>$$\label{eq:lattice-number-testers}
 k=\left\lfloor\kappa\frac{\tau N}{\log N}\right\rfloor,$$ where $\kappa>0$ will be fixed sufficiently small. For fixed $c_5>0$ small enough, there exist $y^1,\ldots,y^k\in\Lambda^*$ such that $$\label{eq:lattice-tester-geometry}
 \lVert y^g\rVert_2\le R_0N^2\sqrt N,
 \qquad\operatorname{dist}(y^g,U)\le R_0N^{-C-5}\sqrt N
 \qquad(1\le g\le k),$$ $$\label{eq:lattice-tester-fourier}
 \prod_{i\in J}\left|\varphi\left(\sum_{g=1}^k w_gy_i^g\right)\right|
 \le e^{-c_5\tau N}
 \quad\bigl(w\in\mathbb Z^k,\ 1\le\lVert w\rVert_\infty\le N^C\bigr).$$ Here and below a distance to a subspace is Euclidean distance.

<a id="eq:lattice-kappa-choice"></a>
For completeness, sample the $k$ vectors independently according to the dual Gaussian. The Gaussian scale inequality just proved, applied on $S^{-1}\Lambda^*$, gives $$\mathbb P\bigl(\lVert S^{-1}y^g\rVert_2>R_0\sqrt N\bigr)
 \le 2^{\dim E/2}e^{-\pi R_0^2N/2}.$$ Outside this event, the two scales of $S$ give Equation <a href="#eq:lattice-tester-geometry" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lattice-tester-geometry">[eq:lattice-tester-geometry]</a>; for the second bound use $W_0\subseteq U$. For a fixed nonzero $w$ in the stated range, condition on every vector except one whose coefficient is nonzero. The remaining vectors produce arbitrary shifts in Lemma <a href="#lem:dual-fourier-average" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:dual-fourier-average">13</a>. Markov’s inequality bounds failure of Equation <a href="#eq:lattice-tester-fourier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lattice-tester-fourier">[eq:lattice-tester-fourier]</a> by $$\exp\bigl(-(c_3-2c_5)\tau N\bigr).$$ There are at most $(3N^C)^k$ possible $w$. Choose $c_5<c_3/4$ and then $\kappa$ so small that $$\label{eq:lattice-kappa-choice}
 (C+2)\kappa<c_5/2.$$ The union of these failures and the norm failures has probability less than one for large $N$, establishing the asserted existence. Notice the order of choices: $\gamma,C$ were fixed first, the constants $c_1,c_2,c_3,c_5$ follow, and only then is $\kappa$ chosen. In particular, the dependence of the Fourier constants on $C$ does not affect the positive margin in Equation <a href="#eq:lattice-entropy-margin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lattice-entropy-margin">[eq:lattice-entropy-margin]</a>.

We have now associated to every violating lattice a set $J$ of at most $\gamma N$ coordinates and $k$ vectors satisfying Equations <a href="#eq:lattice-tester-geometry" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lattice-tester-geometry">[eq:lattice-tester-geometry]</a>–<a href="#eq:lattice-tester-fourier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lattice-tester-fourier">[eq:lattice-tester-fourier]</a>, together with $By^g\in\mathbb Z^m$. The first two conditions will allow a fixed finite description; the last condition will make such a description unlikely for a random matrix.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Counting fixed descriptions and testing rows

<a id="eq:lattice-net-size"></a>
Fix a covering-list member $U$ with $\dim U\le(1-\alpha/3)N$. The region for one vector in Equation <a href="#eq:lattice-tester-geometry" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lattice-tester-geometry">[eq:lattice-tester-geometry]</a> can be covered by at most $$\label{eq:lattice-net-size}
 N^{(C+4)\dim U}$$ sets of diameter $4N^{-C}$. Indeed cover its projection to $U$, a ball of radius at most $R_0N^{5/2}$, at scale $N^{-C}$. The entire perpendicular thickness is $2R_0N^{-C-9/2}=o(N^{-C})$, so it requires no additional covering directions. This also proves the assertion when $\dim U=0$, with one set.

<a id="eq:lattice-approximate-congruences"></a>
Fix $J$ and one such cell for each of the $k$ vectors. If the cells admit a tuple satisfying Equations <a href="#eq:lattice-tester-geometry" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lattice-tester-geometry">[eq:lattice-tester-geometry]</a>–<a href="#eq:lattice-tester-fourier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lattice-tester-fourier">[eq:lattice-tester-fourier]</a>, choose one such tuple and denote it by $\widehat y^1,\ldots,
\widehat y^k$. These choices are made independently of $B$: the conditions just imposed involve only the fixed $U,J$ and the vector coordinates. The representatives need not belong to any dual lattice. Nevertheless, existence of an actual witness in the same cells implies, for every row index $v$ and every $g$, $$\label{eq:lattice-approximate-congruences}
 \operatorname{dist}\bigl((B\widehat y^g)_v,\mathbb Z\bigr)
       \le 4N^{-C}\sqrt N,$$ because $By^g$ is integral and $\lVert B_{v,*}\rVert_2\le\sqrt N$. This passage is what removes the dependence on the unknown lattice from the probability estimate.

Condition on every entry of $B$ except those in the cross block between $J$ and $J^c$. For a row $v\in J^c$, the remaining variables are independent bits $(B_{vi})_{i\in J}$. For different $v\in J^c$ these are disjoint collections of upper-triangular variables, so the row events in Equation <a href="#eq:lattice-approximate-congruences" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lattice-approximate-congruences">[eq:lattice-approximate-congruences]</a> are conditionally independent.

We bound the probability of one row event by a finite Fourier expansion. Put $M_0=\lfloor N^{C-2}\rfloor$, and define the nonnegative periodic kernel $$F_{M_0}(t)=\left|\frac1{M_0}
                   \sum_{h=0}^{M_0-1}e^{2\pi i ht}\right|^2.$$ On the interval of radius $4N^{-C}\sqrt N$ around an integer this kernel is bounded below by a fixed positive constant, since the product of that radius and $M_0$ tends to zero. Its constant Fourier coefficient is $M_0^{-1}$, its other coefficients have magnitude at most $M_0^{-1}$, and its frequencies satisfy $|w|<M_0$. Apply the product of $k$ such kernels to the row vector $((B\widehat y^g)_v)_{g=1}^k$.

<a id="eq:lattice-row-cost"></a>
For every nonzero frequency vector $w$, the absolute value of its conditional expectation is bounded by $$\prod_{i\in J}
 \left|\varphi\left(\sum_{g=1}^k w_g\widehat y_i^g\right)\right|
 \le e^{-c_5\tau N}.$$ The deterministic contribution from $J^c$ has modulus one. A bit with success probability $b$ has the same characteristic-function modulus as one with success probability $p$, since $|p+b e^{it}|^2=|b+p e^{it}|^2$. All frequencies in the product kernel fall within Equation <a href="#eq:lattice-tester-fourier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lattice-tester-fourier">[eq:lattice-tester-fourier]</a>. It follows, for an absolute constant $C_2$, that the row probability is at most $$\begin{aligned}
 C_2^kM_0^{-k}\left(1+(3M_0)^k e^{-c_5\tau N}\right)
 &\le N^{-(C-3)k}.
 \label{eq:lattice-row-cost}
\end{aligned}$$ The last inequality uses Equation <a href="#eq:lattice-kappa-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lattice-kappa-choice">[eq:lattice-kappa-choice]</a>; the quantity inside parentheses is bounded, and fixed factors per coordinate are absorbed by one power of $N$.

There are $m-|J|\ge(1-2\gamma)N$ tested rows for large $N$. The conditional independence just noted and Equation <a href="#eq:lattice-row-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lattice-row-cost">[eq:lattice-row-cost]</a> therefore bound the probability for a fixed $U,J$, and list of cells by $N^{-(C-3)(1-2\gamma)kN}$. The cell choices cost at most $N^{(C+4)(1-\alpha/3)kN}$ by Equation <a href="#eq:lattice-net-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lattice-net-size">[eq:lattice-net-size]</a>. There are at most $2^m$ choices of $J$, and at most $2^{N^2\ell^{-P}}$ choices of $U$. Consequently the probability of any witness, together with nonsingularity and the prime-corank bound, is at most $$2^{m+N^2\ell^{-P}}
       \exp(-\Delta kN\log N)
 \le \exp(-c_6\tau N^2)$$ for a fixed $c_6>0$. Here $k\sim\kappa\tau N/\log N$, $\Delta>0$, and $P>D$. Adding the exceptional probability from Lemma <a href="#lem:prime-corank" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:prime-corank">7</a> still gives $2^{-\omega(N)}$. Every violating lattice supplied a witness in the preceding construction, so this proves Theorem <a href="#thm:small-intersection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:small-intersection">11</a>.

<a id="sec:pairings"></a>
### Discriminant pairings and the number of admissible columns

We now relate the size of $\mathcal S_{L_0}(B)$ to the cyclic factors of the finite group $G_B=\mathbb Z^m/B\mathbb Z^m$. The estimate will apply when the factors beyond the two largest in each primary component have small total logarithmic size. Layers provide convenient notation for this condition.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Layers and the static estimate

For a finite abelian group $G$, write its $l$-primary part as $$G_l\cong\bigoplus_i\mathbb Z/l^{b_{l,i}}\mathbb Z,
 \qquad b_{l,1}\ge b_{l,2}\ge\cdots\ge0,$$ where $l$ always denotes a prime and zero exponents are appended. For $j\ge1$, define the *layer height* and its weight by $$k_G(l,j)=\#\{i:b_{l,i}\ge j\},
 \qquad \nu_l=\frac{\log_N l}{N}.$$ A layer is a pair $(l,j)$ of positive height. Write $k_B$ for $k_{G_B}$. For $G_B$, and for every quotient of $G_B$, Hadamard’s inequality gives <a id="eq:group-order"></a>$$\label{eq:group-order}
 \sum_{l,j}k_G(l,j)\nu_l
 =\frac{\log_N|G|}{N}\le\frac12,
 \qquad m\le N.$$ Indeed, $|G|\le|\det B|\le m^{m/2}\le N^{N/2}$. The trivial group has no layers, so its sums are zero.

<a id="eq:pairing-high-tail"></a>
For $s\ge0$, define <a id="eq:tail-potential"></a>$$\label{eq:tail-potential}
 W_s(B)=\sum_{l,j}(k_B(l,j)-s)_+\nu_l,
 \qquad (x)_+=\max\{x,0\}.$$ Two related quantities will distinguish the two cases of the proof: <a id="eq:pairing-weights"></a>$$\label{eq:pairing-weights}
 T_2(B)=\sum_{k_B(l,j)=2}\nu_l
       =\sum_l(b_{l,2}-b_{l,3})\nu_l,
 \qquad
 J_B=\sum_{k_B(l,j)\ge3}k_B(l,j)\nu_l.$$ Thus $T_2$ counts layers of height exactly two, whereas $J_B$ counts their full heights when the height is at least three. In particular, $$\label{eq:pairing-high-tail}
 0\le J_B\le\frac12,
 \qquad J_B\le3W_2(B),$$ since $k\le3(k-2)$ for every integer $k\ge3$. Figure <a href="#fig:layers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:layers">1</a> illustrates these distinctions at one prime.

<figure id="fig:layers">

<figcaption>Layers for a primary group with cyclic exponents $(5,4,3,1)$. The column heights are $(4,3,3,2,1)$. $W_{2}$ and $J_{B}$ count selected squares, each of weight $ν_{l}$, while $T_{2}$ counts each height-two column once. The four dark squares above the dashed cutoff contribute $4ν_{l}$ to $W_{2}$. The orange column is the unique layer of height exactly two, contributing $ν_{l}$ to $T_{2}$. The first three columns have total weight $10ν_{l}$ and contribute that amount to $J_{B}$.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-layers"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:layers`)

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=0.72cm,y=0.66cm,font=\small]
 \definecolor{layertail}{RGB}{37,111,122}
 \definecolor{layertwo}{RGB}{227,166,70}
 \foreach \j/\height in {1/4,2/3,3/3,4/2,5/1} {
  \foreach \i in {1,...,\height} {
   \fill[black!8] (\j-1,\i-1) rectangle (\j,\i);
   \ifnum\i>2
    \fill[layertail!75] (\j-1,\i-1) rectangle (\j,\i);
   \fi
   \ifnum\j=4
    \fill[layertwo!80] (\j-1,\i-1) rectangle (\j,\i);
   \fi
   \draw[white,line width=1pt] (\j-1,\i-1) rectangle (\j,\i);
  }
  \node[below] at (\j-0.5,0) {\(\j\)};
  \node[above] at (\j-0.5,\height+0.05) {\(\height\)};
 }
 \draw[densely dashed,black!65] (-0.3,2)--(5.3,2);
 \node[anchor=east] at (-0.4,2) {\(s=2\)};
 \node[anchor=east] at (-0.4,4.7) {height};
 \node at (2.5,-0.9) {layer index \(j\)};
 \draw[layertail,thick] (1.5,2.6)--(6.1,4.0);
 \node[anchor=west,align=left] at (6.25,4.0)
   {tail above two:\\ \(W_2=4\nu_l\)};
 \draw[layertwo!80!black,thick] (3.6,1.75)--(6.1,2.15);
 \node[anchor=west,align=left] at (6.25,2.15)
   {height exactly two:\\ \(T_2=\nu_l\)};
 \node[anchor=west,align=left] at (6.25,0.2)
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 18 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

