Vertical axis is labeled "height" with integer levels 1, 2, 3, 4. A horizontal dashed line at height 2 is labeled "$s = 2$". Column 1 has height 4 with squares numbered 1, 2, 3, 4 from bottom to top; columns 2 and 3 have height 3 with squares numbered 1, 2, 3; column 4 has height 2 with squares numbered 1, 2; column 5 has height 1 with square numbered 1. The four squares strictly above $s=2$ (two in column 1, one in column 2, one in column 3) are shaded dark teal, with an arrow pointing to the label "tail above two: $W_2 = 4\nu_l$". Column 4 is shaded orange, with an arrow pointing to the label "height exactly two: $T_2 = \nu_l$". A horizontal bracket spans columns 1, 2, and 3 below the grid, pointing to the label "full first three columns: $J_B = 10\nu_l$".


We use the slowly varying scales <a id="eq:layer-scales"></a>$$\label{eq:layer-scales}
 R=\lceil\ell^2\rceil,
 \qquad \sigma=R^{-6}.$$ They will also be used in Section <a href="#sec:paths" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:paths">6</a>.

<a id="stmt-prop-static-column"></a>
<div id="prop:static-column" class="proposition">

**Proposition 14** (Static column estimate). *For every fixed $L_0\ge1$ and $e_0>0$, there is a fixed $\zeta>0$ such that, typically for nonsingular bit matrices $B$ of size $m\in\mathcal I_N$, $$W_2(B)\le\zeta
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 |\mathcal S_{L_0}(B)|\le2^{e_0N}.$$*

</div>

The proposition leaves matrices with large $W_2$ for the path argument. Its proof separates according to $T_2$. Layers of height exactly two can force admissible columns into a few subgroups of large index; Theorem <a href="#thm:small-intersection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:small-intersection">11</a> then applies. Otherwise a coloring controls the pairing on differences of columns. An arithmetic determinant bound and the spectrum of $B$ then bound the dimension of a large subset of one color. We first develop the pairing argument that distinguishes these two cases.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Colors and subgroups of large index

The *discriminant pairing* of $B$ is <a id="eq:discriminant-pairing"></a>$$\label{eq:discriminant-pairing}
 \lambda_B(\bar x,\bar y)
   =x^{\mathsf T}B^{-1}y\bmod\mathbb Z,
 \qquad \bar x,\bar y\in G_B.$$ It is well defined because changing either representative by an integer column combination of $B$ changes the value by an integer. It is *perfect*: the map $G_B\to\operatorname{Hom}(G_B,\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z)$ defined by the pairing is an isomorphism. To see injectivity, an element pairing to zero with every coordinate class satisfies $B^{-1}x\in\mathbb Z^m$, so its class is zero; the two finite groups have equal order. Distinct primary components are orthogonal, since their mutual pairing is annihilated by coprime powers. An element is called *isotropic* if its self-pairing is zero. Every $z\in\mathcal S_{L_0}(B)$ has isotropic class, and its primary components are individually isotropic by uniqueness of primary decomposition in $\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z$.

The orthogonal block decomposition of a perfect primary pairing uses cyclic blocks and, at $l=2$, possible two-generator blocks with equal cyclic exponents. These decompositions are classical; see Miranda <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[Miranda](#ref-Miranda), Section 1]. We use the following precise consequences of the decomposition and the coloring analysis in <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[SymmetricRate](#ref-SymmetricRate), Lemmas 6.1–6.3].

<a id="stmt-lem-pairing-colors"></a>
<div id="lem:pairing-colors" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:primary-color-count"></a>
<a id="eq:primary-color-pairing"></a>
<a id="eq:restricted-pairing-map"></a>
**Lemma 15** (Coloring input). *Let $B$ be a nonsingular symmetric integer matrix. For each prime $l$, put $f_l=b_{l,3}$. The perfect pairing on $G_{B,l}$ admits an orthogonal decomposition $$G_{B,l}=U_l\oplus V_l,$$ where $U_l$ consists of the blocks with cyclic exponents strictly greater than $f_l$, and the exponent of $V_l$ divides $l^{f_l}$. There are at most two cyclic factors in $U_l$. The isotropic elements of $G_{B,l}$ can be colored with $C_l$ colors satisfying $$\label{eq:primary-color-count}
 C_l\le1+8b_{l,1},
 \qquad C_l\le2^{b_{l,2}-b_{l,3}}\quad\text{if \(l\) is odd},$$ such that two elements of one color satisfy $$\label{eq:primary-color-pairing}
 l^{f_l}\lambda_B(x_{U_l},y_{U_l})=0
 \quad\text{in }\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z.$$ If $X\subset G_B$ consists of isotropic elements with one fixed color at every prime, put $H=\langle x-y:x,y\in X\rangle$. Then the image of the *restricted* pairing map $$\label{eq:restricted-pairing-map}
 H\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(H,\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z),
 \qquad u\longmapsto\lambda_B(u,\cdot)|_H,$$ has order at most $N^{NJ_B}$.*

</div>

The cutoff in Lemma <a href="#lem:pairing-colors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pairing-colors">15</a> does not split a block, since the two exponents in each two-generator block are equal. The restriction in Equation <a href="#eq:restricted-pairing-map" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:restricted-pairing-map">[eq:restricted-pairing-map]</a> is essential: the characters are tested on $H$, not on all of $G_B$.

For our purposes the same coloring has another consequence.

<a id="stmt-lem-color-subgroup"></a>
<div id="lem:color-subgroup" class="lemma">

**Lemma 16** (A subgroup containing each color). *For every prime $l$, each primary color in Lemma <a href="#lem:pairing-colors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pairing-colors">15</a> is contained in a subgroup of $G_{B,l}$ whose index is at least $l^{b_{l,2}-b_{l,3}}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-color-subgroup"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-color-subgroup</summary>

*Proof.* Write $f=f_l$ and $U=U_l$. Multiply the perfect pairing on $U$ by $l^f$, and quotient $U$ by the radical of this new pairing. The resulting pairing on a group $\bar U$ is perfect. Its order is $$|\bar U|=l^{\sum_i(b_{l,i}-f)_+}.$$ Indeed, under the original perfect pairing the new radical is $U[l^f]=\{u\in U:l^fu=0\}$, and each cyclic factor of exponent $b>f$ leaves a quotient of order $l^{b-f}$. By Equation <a href="#eq:primary-color-pairing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:primary-color-pairing">[eq:primary-color-pairing]</a>, the images of one color generate a subgroup $A\subset\bar U$ with $A\subset A^\perp$. Perfectness gives $|A|\,|A^\perp|=|\bar U|$, so $$[\bar U:A]\ge|\bar U|^{1/2}
 =l^{\frac12\sum_i(b_{l,i}-f)_+}
 \ge l^{b_{l,2}-f}.$$ Take the preimage of $A$ in $U$, and add the unrestricted summand $V_l$. This subgroup contains the entire primary color and has the required index. The argument also covers $U=0$, when the asserted index is one. ◻

</details>

The next counting step controls the price of specifying colors. It uses both bounds in Equation <a href="#eq:primary-color-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:primary-color-count">[eq:primary-color-count]</a>: the odd-prime bound is efficient before a weight threshold is reached, and the polynomial bound controls the prime that crosses it.

<a id="stmt-lem-color-cost"></a>
<div id="lem:color-cost" class="lemma">

**Lemma 17** (Cost of specifying colors). *Uniformly over nonsingular symmetric bit matrices of size at most $N$, the following hold as $N\to\infty$.*

1.  *If $T_2(B)\ge\sigma$, there is a set of primes $\mathcal P$ such that $$\sum_{l\in\mathcal P}(b_{l,2}-b_{l,3})\nu_l\ge\sigma,
      \qquad \sum_{l\in\mathcal P}\log_2 C_l=o(N).$$*

2.  *If $T_2(B)<\sigma$, then $\sum_l\log_2 C_l=o(N)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-color-cost"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-color-cost</summary>

<a id="eq:large-prime-color-cost"></a>
*Proof.* The group-order bound gives $b_{l,1}\le(N/2)\log_2N$, so the first bound in Equation <a href="#eq:primary-color-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:primary-color-count">[eq:primary-color-count]</a> costs $O(\log N)$ bits per prime. All primes $l\le\sqrt N$ together therefore cost $O(\sqrt N\log N)=o(N)$. For larger primes, $\nu_l>1/(2N)$; they are odd for large $N$, and hence $$\label{eq:large-prime-color-cost}
 \sum_{l\in\mathcal P,\ l>\sqrt N}\log_2 C_l
 \le\sum_{l\in\mathcal P,\ l>\sqrt N}(b_{l,2}-b_{l,3})
 \le2N\sum_{l\in\mathcal P,\ l>\sqrt N}
                 (b_{l,2}-b_{l,3})\nu_l.$$ For <span class="upright">(i)</span>, first include every contributing prime at most $\sqrt N$. If their total weight is below $\sigma$, add larger contributing primes until that threshold is first reached. Before the last prime, Equation <a href="#eq:large-prime-color-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:large-prime-color-cost">[eq:large-prime-color-cost]</a> costs at most $2\sigma N$ bits. Charge the last prime only $O(\log N)$ bits using the first color bound, regardless of its weight. The total is $o(N)$, since $\sigma\to0$. For <span class="upright">(ii)</span>, Equation <a href="#eq:large-prime-color-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:large-prime-color-cost">[eq:large-prime-color-cost]</a> applies to all large primes with total cost at most $2\sigma N$; the small-prime cost is unchanged. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:height-two-columns"></a>
Fix $0<\alpha<\min\{e_0,1\}$, and impose the typical event of Theorem <a href="#thm:small-intersection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:small-intersection">11</a> with $D=20$. Since $\tau=\ell^{-20}=o(\sigma)$, the preceding lemmas settle all matrices with $T_2(B)\ge\sigma$. In fact, specify colors only at the primes selected in Lemma <a href="#lem:color-cost" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:color-cost">17</a><span class="upright">(i)</span>. There are $2^{o(N)}$ resulting parts of $\mathcal S_{L_0}(B)$. By Lemma <a href="#lem:color-subgroup" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:color-subgroup">16</a>, each part lies in the preimage $K\subset\mathbb Z^m$ of a subgroup of $G_B$ with $$B\mathbb Z^m\subset K\subset\mathbb Z^m,
 \qquad [\mathbb Z^m:K]\ge N^{\sigma N}\ge N^{\tau N}.$$ Theorem <a href="#thm:small-intersection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:small-intersection">11</a> bounds its size by $2^{\alpha N}$. Consequently $$\label{eq:height-two-columns}
 |\mathcal S_{L_0}(B)|\le2^{\alpha N+o(N)}\le2^{e_0N}$$ for sufficiently large $N$. This argument uses the theorem’s simultaneity over all lattices: the color subgroups may depend on $B$.

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### A lattice of graph differences

It remains to control the column set when $T_2(B)<\sigma$. The color cost is then subexponential. We first show how two real-matrix estimates would bound the dimension of a large part of one color; their probabilistic proofs will follow the comparison.

A nonempty finite set $\mathcal A$ is *$\rho$-robust* on its affine span $F$ if every affine subspace $E\subset F$ of codimension $t$ satisfies $$|\mathcal A\cap E|\le2^{-\rho t}|\mathcal A|.$$ We use the following two geometric inputs from <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[SymmetricRate](#ref-SymmetricRate), Lemmas 3.7 and 3.8].

<a id="stmt-lem-robust-extraction"></a>
<div id="lem:robust-extraction" class="lemma">

**Lemma 18** (Robust extraction). *If $\varnothing\ne\mathcal A\subset\{0,1\}^m$ and $\rho>0$, there is an affine subspace $E$ for which the nonempty set $\mathcal V=\mathcal A\cap E$ is $\rho$-robust on its affine span and satisfies $$\log_2|\mathcal V|\ge\log_2|\mathcal A|-\rho m.$$*

</div>

<a id="stmt-lem-robust-image"></a>
<div id="lem:robust-image" class="lemma">

**Lemma 19** (Determinant of a robust image lattice). *Fix $D,C_0>0$ and put $\rho=\ell^{-D}$. Let $\varnothing\ne\mathcal V\subset\{0,1\}^m$, $m\le N$, be $\rho$-robust on its affine span $F$, of dimension $r$. Let $\Phi:F\to\mathbb R^d$ be an injective rational affine map such that $$\lVert\Phi(v)-\Phi(w)\rVert_2\le C_0\sqrt N
 \qquad(v,w\in\mathcal V).$$ Then the integer span of its image differences is a rank-$r$ lattice of covolume at most $\exp(O_{D,C_0}(N\ell))$. Also $H(\mathop{\mathrm{span}}(\mathcal V-\mathcal V))\le\exp(O_D(N\ell))$. The covolumes in rank zero are one.*

</div>

For a symmetric matrix $C$ and a linear subspace $F\subseteq\mathbb R^m$, the notation $C|_F$ denotes the restriction of its bilinear form to $F\times F$, rather than the restriction of the associated linear map. Its radical is $$\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}(C|_F)=\{x\in F:x^{\mathsf T}Cy=0\text{ for every }y\in F\}.$$ Its dimension is the nullity $\dim F-\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(C|_F)$.

We will also use a determinant consequence of a finite pairing image. We include its short proof to make clear why the image order enters to the first power; this is <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[SymmetricRate](#ref-SymmetricRate), Lemma 6.4].

<a id="stmt-lem-pairing-determinant"></a>
<div id="lem:pairing-determinant" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:pairing-det"></a>
**Lemma 20** (Pairing image and determinant). *Let $L$ be a lattice spanning a Euclidean space $V$, and let $q$ be a symmetric bilinear form with $q(L,L)\subset\mathbb Q$. Set $$K=L\cap\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}q,
 \qquad S=V\cap(\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}q)^\perp.$$ If the image of $L\to\operatorname{Hom}(L,\mathbb Q/\mathbb Z)$, $x\mapsto q(x,\cdot)\bmod\mathbb Z$, has order $t$, then $$\label{eq:pairing-det}
 |\det_{\mathrm{orth}}(q|_S)|
 \ge\frac{(\det K)^2}{t(\det L)^2}.$$ The determinant on the left is in an orthonormal basis of $S$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-pairing-determinant"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-pairing-determinant</summary>

*Proof.* The matrix of $q$ in a lattice basis is rational. Its radical therefore has a rational basis, so $K$ spans the radical and is primitive in $L$. The orthogonal projection $\bar L$ of $L$ onto $S$ is a lattice, and $\det L=\det K\det\bar L$. The form descends to $\bar L$ with the same pairing image order, because pullback of characters along $L\to\bar L$ is injective.

Let $M$ be the nonsingular matrix of the descended form in a basis of $\bar L$. Its pairing kernel modulo $\mathbb Z$ in the coordinate lattice has index $t$. If $A$ is an integer basis matrix for that kernel, then $|\det A|=t$ and $A^{\mathsf T}M$ is integral and nonsingular. Thus $$1\le|\det(A^{\mathsf T}M)|=t|\det M|.$$ Changing from the lattice basis to an orthonormal basis divides this determinant by $(\det\bar L)^2$, which proves Equation <a href="#eq:pairing-det" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pairing-det">[eq:pairing-det]</a>. If $S$ has dimension zero, then $K=L$ and $t=1$, so both sides of Equation <a href="#eq:pairing-det" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pairing-det">[eq:pairing-det]</a> are one. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-graph-comparison"></a>
<div id="lem:graph-comparison" class="lemma">

**Lemma 21** (The graph comparison). *Fix $L_0\ge1$ and $\xi,w>0$. Suppose that $B$ is a nonsingular symmetric bit matrix of size $m\in\mathcal I_N$, with $T_2(B)<\sigma$, and that the following hold:*

1.  *for every rational $F\subseteq\mathbb R^m$ of height at most $\exp(N\ell^2)$, $\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(B^{-1}|_F)\ge\dim F-\xi N$;*

2.  *at most $\xi N$ eigenvalues of $B$ lie in $[-w\sqrt N,w\sqrt N]$.*

*Then $$|\mathcal S_{L_0}(B)|\le 2^{(2J_B+2\xi+o(1))N},$$ where the error tends to zero uniformly over these matrices, for fixed $L_0,\xi,w$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-graph-comparison"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-graph-comparison</summary>

<a id="eq:robust-column-size"></a>
<a id="eq:graph-height"></a>
*Proof.* The assertion is immediate if $\mathcal S_{L_0}(B)$ is empty. Choose a largest overall color $\mathcal A$ of the column set, and apply Lemma <a href="#lem:robust-extraction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:robust-extraction">18</a> with $\rho=\ell^{-2}$. For the resulting set $\mathcal V$, Lemma <a href="#lem:color-cost" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:color-cost">17</a><span class="upright">(ii)</span> gives $$\label{eq:robust-column-size}
 \log_2|\mathcal S_{L_0}(B)|
 \le\log_2|\mathcal V|+o(N).$$ Put $F_0=\mathop{\mathrm{span}}(\mathcal V-\mathcal V)$ and $r_0=\dim F_0$. We place each column beside its inverse image: the norm condition keeps differences short, while the first coordinates remain integral. The induced graph metric will also control the inverse form near small eigenvalues of $B$. Define the rational graph map and its difference lattice $$\gamma(x)=(x,B^{-1}x),\qquad
 L=\langle\gamma(v)-\gamma(v'):v,v'\in\mathcal V\rangle_{\mathbb Z}
       \subset\gamma(F_0).$$ The graph differences satisfy $$\lVert\gamma(v)-\gamma(v')\rVert_2^2
 \le m+4L_0^2N\le(1+4L_0^2)N.$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:robust-image" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:robust-image">19</a>, applied both to the graph map and to the identity map, consequently gives $$\label{eq:graph-height}
 \det L\le\exp(O_{L_0}(N\ell)),
 \qquad H(F_0)\le\exp(O(N\ell))\le\exp(N\ell^2)$$ for sufficiently large $N$.

<a id="eq:graph-rank"></a>
On the full graph $\Gamma=\gamma(\mathbb R^m)$, define the rational symmetric form $$q(\gamma(x),\gamma(y))=x^{\mathsf T}B^{-1}y.$$ Its restriction to $\gamma(F_0)$ has rank $s$ satisfying $$\label{eq:graph-rank}
 s\ge r_0-\xi N$$ by the first hypothesis and Equation <a href="#eq:graph-height" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:graph-height">[eq:graph-height]</a>. The hypothesis covers every subspace of this height, including $F_0$, which was selected from admissible columns for $B$.

<a id="eq:graph-det-lower"></a>
The map $L\to G_B$ taking $\gamma(u)$ to $u+B\mathbb Z^m$ has image $$H=\langle\bar v-\bar v':v,v'\in\mathcal V\rangle.$$ It is surjective onto $H$, and the form modulo $\mathbb Z$ is the pullback of the restricted pairing on $H$. Pullback of characters is injective, so the two pairing maps have equal image orders. Lemma <a href="#lem:pairing-colors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pairing-colors">15</a> bounds this order by $N^{NJ_B}$. Let $K$ be the radical lattice of the restriction to $\gamma(F_0)$, and let $S$ be its radical’s orthogonal complement in that space. The first-coordinate projection is injective on $\Gamma$ and takes $K$ to an integer lattice of the same rank. Such a lattice has covolume at least one, and orthogonal projection contracts volumes. Hence $\det K\ge1$. Lemma <a href="#lem:pairing-determinant" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:pairing-determinant">20</a> now yields $$\label{eq:graph-det-lower}
 |\det_{\mathrm{orth}}(q|_S)|
 \ge N^{-NJ_B}\exp(-O_{L_0}(N\ell)).$$

The same determinant admits an upper bound from the spectrum of $B$. If $v$ is a unit eigenvector with nonzero eigenvalue $x$, its graph vector has squared norm $1+x^{-2}$, while $q(\gamma(v),\gamma(v))=x^{-1}$. Orthogonal eigenvectors of $B$ have orthogonal graph vectors. Thus the eigenvalues of the self-adjoint operator representing $q$ on $\Gamma$ are $$\frac{x}{1+x^2}.$$ They have absolute value at most one. By the second hypothesis, all but at most $\xi N$ of them have absolute value at most $C_w/\sqrt N$, where $C_w=1/w$.

<a id="eq:graph-det-upper"></a>
The absolute determinant of the compression of a self-adjoint operator to any $s$-dimensional subspace is at most the product of its largest $s$ singular values. Indeed, it is $\langle\omega,(\bigwedge^s T)\omega\rangle$, where $\omega$ is the unit exterior product of an orthonormal basis of the subspace. Applying this to $S$, whose dimension is $s$, gives $$\label{eq:graph-det-upper}
 |\det_{\mathrm{orth}}(q|_S)|
 \le\left(\frac{C_w}{\sqrt N}\right)^{(s-\xi N)_+}.$$ This argument applies to indefinite forms and includes $s=0$. Combining Equations <a href="#eq:graph-det-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:graph-det-lower">[eq:graph-det-lower]</a> and <a href="#eq:graph-det-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:graph-det-upper">[eq:graph-det-upper]</a>, and then using Equation <a href="#eq:graph-rank" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:graph-rank">[eq:graph-rank]</a>, gives $$(s-\xi N)_+
   \left(\frac12\log N-\log C_w\right)
 \le NJ_B\log N+O_{L_0}(N\ell),
 \qquad
 \frac{r_0}{N}\le2J_B+2\xi+o(1).$$ The second bound also holds when $s<\xi N$.

Finally, projection to $r_0$ suitable coordinates is injective on the affine span of $\mathcal V$, so $|\mathcal V|\le2^{r_0}$. Equation <a href="#eq:robust-column-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:robust-column-size">[eq:robust-column-size]</a> and the preceding dimension bound imply $$\frac1N\log_2|\mathcal S_{L_0}(B)|
 \le 2J_B+2\xi+o(1).$$ The error is bounded by $O_{L_0,w}(\ell^{-2}+\sigma+N^{-1/2}\log N+\ell/\log N)$. The first three terms come from extraction and colors, and the last from the determinant comparison, using $J_B\le1/2$. This establishes the asserted uniformity. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### Uniform rank and spectral estimates

Lemma <a href="#lem:graph-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:graph-comparison">21</a> identifies the two remaining probabilistic requirements: inverse forms must retain rank on every low-height span, and most eigenvalues must stay away from zero at scale $\sqrt N$. We now verify both requirements for the biased bit model. The arguments adapt <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[SymmetricRate](#ref-SymmetricRate), Corollary 5.2 and Proposition 5.4] to the biased arrays. The rank estimate uses the atom bound $b$; the spectral estimate uses the common variance $pb$ and the rank-two bound on the mean.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
##### Inverse forms on low-height subspaces

<a id="stmt-prop-inverse-rank"></a>
<div id="prop:inverse-rank" class="proposition">

**Proposition 22** (Uniform inverse restriction rank). *For every fixed $D>0$ and $\xi>0$, typically every nonsingular bit matrix $B$ satisfies, simultaneously for all rational subspaces $F\subseteq\mathbb R^m$ with $H(F)\le\exp(N\ell^D)$, $$\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(B^{-1}|_F)\ge\dim F-\xi N.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-inverse-rank"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-inverse-rank</summary>

<a id="eq:fixed-restriction-tail"></a>
*Proof.* First fix an arbitrary real subspace $U\subseteq\mathbb R^m$ of dimension $d$. Choose a basis matrix $V$ and a coordinate set $I$ of size $d$ so that $V_I$ is the identity matrix. Conditional on all entries of $B$ outside the principal block $I\times I$, the matrix of $B|_U$ in this basis is $$V^{\mathsf T}BV=B_{I,I}+H,$$ where $H$ is a fixed symmetric matrix. The unrevealed entries of $B_{I,I}$ are independent bits, each with largest atom $b$. Equation <a href="#eq:typical-corank-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:typical-corank-tail">[eq:typical-corank-tail]</a> therefore shows that $$\label{eq:fixed-restriction-tail}
 \mathbb P\bigl(\dim\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}(B|_U)\ge\lceil\xi N/2\rceil\bigr)
 \le 2^{-c_{p,\xi}N^2}$$ for all sufficiently large $N$; the event is empty if $d<\lceil\xi N/2\rceil$.

For a fixed symmetric form, restricted nullity changes by at most $d(U,V)$ when $U$ is replaced by $V$. To check this, if $Z\subseteq U$ has codimension $t$, restriction deletes $t$ rows and $t$ columns of a representing matrix. The rank decreases by an amount between zero and $2t$, so the nullity changes by at most $t$. Apply this first to $Z=U\cap V$ inside $U$, then inside $V$.

Apply Theorem <a href="#thm:height-cover" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:height-cover">8</a> with the given $D$ and any fixed $P>0$. Use Equation <a href="#eq:fixed-restriction-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fixed-restriction-tail">[eq:fixed-restriction-tail]</a> for the orthogonal complement of every member of its list. The union of all failures has probability at most $$2^{N^2\ell^{-P}-c_{p,\xi}N^2}=2^{-\Omega_{p,\xi}(N^2)}.$$ Outside this event, approximate a given $F$ by a list member. The nullity comparison and preservation of distance under orthocomplements give $$\dim\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}(B|_{F^\perp})
 <\lceil\xi N/2\rceil+N\ell^{-P}\le\xi N$$ for large $N$. For nonsingular $B$, the two radicals satisfy $$\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}(B|_{F^\perp})=F^\perp\cap B^{-1}F,
 \qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{rad}}(B^{-1}|_F)=F\cap B(F^\perp).$$ Multiplication by $B$ is an isomorphism from the first space onto the second. Their equal dimensions prove the proposition. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
##### Few eigenvalues near zero

The preceding argument controls exact nullity on many subspaces. We also need a metric statement: only a small fraction of the eigenvalues can be much smaller than $\sqrt N$. The rank assumption on the mean enters precisely here.

<a id="stmt-prop-small-spectrum"></a>
<div id="prop:small-spectrum" class="proposition">

**Proposition 23** (Small-spectrum bound). *For every fixed $\xi>0$, there is $w_\xi>0$, depending also on $p$, such that typically a bit matrix $B$ has at most $\xi N$ eigenvalues, counted with multiplicity, in $$[-w_\xi\sqrt N,w_\xi\sqrt N].$$ Nonsingularity is not required. The exceptional probability is at most $\exp(-c_{p,\xi}N^2)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-small-spectrum"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-small-spectrum</summary>

*Proof.* Set $$X=\frac{B-\mathbb EB}{\sqrt{mpb}},\qquad
 \mu_X=\frac1m\sum_{i=1}^m\delta_{\lambda_i(X)}.$$ We first verify that the expected measures $\mathbb E\mu_X$ converge uniformly over the permitted arrays to the semicircle law $$d\mu_{\mathrm{sc}}(x)=\frac1{2\pi}\sqrt{4-x^2}\,
                       \mathbf 1_{\{|x|\le 2\}}\,dx.$$ This is the fixed-moment argument for Wigner’s law <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[Wigner](#ref-Wigner)]. The centered, variance-standardized entries $(B_{ij}-\mathbb EB_{ij})/\sqrt{pb}$ have mean zero, variance one, and uniformly bounded moments of every fixed order. Expand $m^{-1}\mathbb E\operatorname{Tr}X^s$ as a sum over closed walks of length $s$. Independence makes a summand zero whenever an unordered edge is used exactly once. Every remaining walk has at most $s/2$ distinct edges and at most $1+s/2$ vertices. For each fixed walk pattern with $v<1+s/2$ vertices, its contribution is $O_{p,s}(m^{v-1-s/2})=o(1)$. Equality is possible only when $s$ is even and the walk traverses each edge of a tree twice. Each such pattern contributes $1+o(1)$, since it uses only variances, and there are $(s/2+1)^{-1}\binom{s}{s/2}$ patterns. There are finitely many patterns for fixed $s$, so the errors are uniform. The limiting moments are those of $\mu_{\mathrm{sc}}$; its compact support makes them determinate. The method of moments proves the asserted weak convergence, uniformly by applying the same argument to any sequence of permitted arrays.

<a id="eq:typical-tent-mean"></a>
Choose a fixed $w>0$, sufficiently small in terms of $\xi$, and put $$f(x)=(1-|x|/w)_+.$$ The semicircle mass of $[-w,w]$ tends to zero with $w$, so the convergence just proved gives, for sufficiently large $N$, $$\label{eq:typical-tent-mean}
 \mathbb E\operatorname{Tr}f(X)\le\xi m/16.$$ To obtain a much stronger probability estimate than moment convergence alone provides, write $$f=1-g_1+g_2,\qquad
 g_1(x)=|x|/w,\quad g_2(x)=(|x|/w-1)_+.$$ Both functions are convex and $1/w$-Lipschitz, and their matrix traces are convex functions of the entries. If $M_+$ denotes the spectral positive part of $M$, then $\operatorname{Tr}M_+=\sup_{0\preceq P\preceq I}\operatorname{Tr}(PM)$ for symmetric $M$; expressing the two traces as sums of positive-part traces proves convexity directly.

Let $y$ denote the independent upper-triangular coordinates, and write $X(y)$ for the corresponding centered, normalized matrix. The Hoffman–Wielandt inequality <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[HoffmanWielandt](#ref-HoffmanWielandt), Theorem 1] and Cauchy–Schwarz give, for $j=1,2$, $$\left|\operatorname{Tr}g_j(X(y))-
             \operatorname{Tr}g_j(X(y'))\right|
 \le\frac{\sqrt m}{w}\lVert X(y)-X(y')\rVert_F
 \le\frac1w\sqrt{\frac2{pb}}\lVert y-y'\rVert_2.$$ Here $\lVert\cdot\rVert_F$ is the Frobenius norm, and the factor $2$ accounts for reflecting off-diagonal entries. The convex Lipschitz consequence of Talagrand’s convex-distance inequality <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[Talagrand](#ref-Talagrand), Theorem 4.1.1] applies to independent coordinates in $[0,1]$ with arbitrary distributions: if $F$ is convex and $L$-Lipschitz, then $$\mathbb P(|F-\mathbb EF|>t)\le C\exp(-ct^2/L^2).$$ The usual median formulation implies this expectation formulation by integrating the tail and adjusting the absolute constants. Applying it to the two convex traces, with deviations $\xi m/32$, and using Equation <a href="#eq:typical-tent-mean" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:typical-tent-mean">[eq:typical-tent-mean]</a>, gives $$\mathbb P\bigl(\operatorname{Tr}f(X)>\xi m/8\bigr)
 \le C\exp(-c_{p,\xi,w}m^2).$$ On the complementary event, $f\ge1/2$ on $[-w/2,w/2]$ implies that at most $\xi m/4$ eigenvalues of $X$ lie in this interval. The matrix $B/\sqrt{mpb}$ differs from $X$ by a matrix of rank at most two. Interlacing changes the count in any interval by at most four. Taking, for example, $w_\xi=w\sqrt{pb}/4$, we have $w_\xi\sqrt{N/(mpb)}\le w/2$ for large $N$, uniformly in $m\in\mathcal I_N$. The requested eigenvalue count is therefore at most $\xi m/4+4\le\xi N$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-small-spectrum-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-small-spectrum</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:static-column" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:static-column">14</a>.* Fix $L_0\ge1$ and $e_0>0$. Choose fixed $\xi,\zeta>0$ with $6\zeta+2\xi<e_0$. Intersect the typical event used for Equation <a href="#eq:height-two-columns" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-two-columns">[eq:height-two-columns]</a> with the events in Propositions <a href="#prop:inverse-rank" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:inverse-rank">22</a> and <a href="#prop:small-spectrum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:small-spectrum">23</a>, taking height exponent $D=2$ and error $\xi$. The latter proposition supplies a fixed $w_\xi>0$. This finite intersection is still typical, uniformly in the permitted models and sizes.

Let $B$ be nonsingular on this event, with $W_2(B)\le\zeta$. If $T_2(B)\ge\sigma$, Equation <a href="#eq:height-two-columns" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-two-columns">[eq:height-two-columns]</a> already gives the required bound. Otherwise apply Lemma <a href="#lem:graph-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:graph-comparison">21</a> with $w=w_\xi$. Equation <a href="#eq:pairing-high-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:pairing-high-tail">[eq:pairing-high-tail]</a> gives $$|\mathcal S_{L_0}(B)|
 \le2^{(2J_B+2\xi+o(1))N}
 \le2^{(6\zeta+2\xi+o(1))N}
 \le2^{e_0N}$$ for all sufficiently large $N$. This proves the proposition. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:paths"></a>
### Two column constraints along a path of principal minors

Proposition <a href="#prop:static-column" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:static-column">14</a> bounds the admissible columns when $W_2$ is small. We now show that a terminal matrix with large $W_2$ must encounter two rare column constraints along a short path of principal minors. Each constraint costs almost $b^N$, giving the exceptional probability required in Proposition <a href="#prop:column-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:column-bound">5</a>. The principal-minor method and its layer recursion come from <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[SymmetricRate](#ref-SymmetricRate), Sections 7 and 11]. We first state the deterministic inputs, then give the probability estimates for biased columns.

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### Principal extensions and retained layers

<a id="eq:path-extension"></a>
<a id="eq:path-gate"></a>
A principal extension of width $h\in\{1,2\}$ has the form $$\label{eq:path-extension}
 B=\begin{pmatrix}C&V\\V^{\mathsf T}&D\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad V=(v_1\ \cdots\ v_h),$$ where $C$ is nonsingular and $D$ is symmetric. At width two we impose the *gate* $$\label{eq:path-gate}
 v_1,v_2\in\mathcal S_{L_0}(C).$$ There is no gate at width one. The following elementary fact ensures that every nonsingular bit matrix can be reached by a path with these gates.

<a id="stmt-lem-path-deletion"></a>
<div id="lem:path-deletion" class="lemma">

**Lemma 24** (Gated deletion, <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[SymmetricRate](#ref-SymmetricRate), Lemma 7.1]). *Let $B$ be a nonsingular symmetric bit matrix of positive size. The empty principal minor has determinant one, and its admissible column set consists of the unique empty column. Either some one-coordinate principal deletion is nonsingular, or a two-coordinate principal deletion gives a nonsingular matrix $C$ for which both deleted cross columns satisfy $$v_i^{\mathsf T}C^{-1}v_i\in\{0,1\},
 \qquad \lVert C^{-1}v_i\rVert_\infty\le1
 \quad(i=1,2).$$ In particular, whenever the sizes belong to $\mathcal I_N$, the two-coordinate move satisfies Equation <a href="#eq:path-gate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-gate">[eq:path-gate]</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-path-deletion"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-path-deletion</summary>

*Proof.* The cofactor identity gives the first alternative if some diagonal entry of $B^{-1}$ is nonzero. Otherwise choose distinct $i,j$ maximizing the absolute value of an entry of $B^{-1}$, and write $\kappa=(B^{-1})_{ij}\ne0$. The corresponding principal block is $\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&\kappa\\\kappa&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)$. Jacobi’s complementary minor identity makes the complementary matrix $C$ nonsingular. In the block notation of Equation <a href="#eq:path-extension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-extension">[eq:path-extension]</a>, its Schur complement $T=D-V^{\mathsf T}C^{-1}V$ satisfies $$T^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\kappa\\\kappa&0\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad (B^{-1})_{I,\{i,j\}}=-C^{-1}VT^{-1},$$ where $I$ is the set of remaining indices. The diagonal of $T$ vanishes, giving the two quadratic identities. The two columns of the inverse block on the right are $-\kappa C^{-1}v_2$ and $-\kappa C^{-1}v_1$. Their entries have absolute value at most $|\kappa|$, proving the norm bound. Finally $\lVert C^{-1}v_i\rVert_2\le\sqrt{\operatorname{size}(C)}
\le\sqrt N\le L_0\sqrt N$. ◻

</details>

For an extension with nonsingular start $C$, define the successive quotients <a id="eq:path-quotients"></a>$$\label{eq:path-quotients}
 G_0=G_C,\qquad
 G_i=\mathbb Z^m/(C\mathbb Z^m+\mathbb Zv_1+\cdots+\mathbb Zv_i)
       =G_{i-1}/\langle\overline v_i\rangle
       \quad(1\le i\le h),$$ where $m=\operatorname{size}(C)$. These groups are defined even if the endpoint $B$ is singular. In a one-element quotient, each layer height drops by zero or one. A positive layer whose height does not drop is called *retained*. A *plateau* is a maximal interval of consecutive layers of equal positive height at one prime. On each plateau, the retained layers form a prefix, possibly empty. These facts are Lemma 7.3 of <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[SymmetricRate](#ref-SymmetricRate)]; for example the prefix assertion follows because quotient heights are nonincreasing and can only equal the original height or one less.

Here are the remaining group-theoretic inputs in the precise form needed below.

<a id="stmt-lem-path-algebra"></a>
<div id="lem:path-algebra" class="lemma">

**Lemma 25** (Retention lattices and tail recursion). *Let $C$ be a nonsingular symmetric bit matrix of size $m\le N$.*

<a id="eq:path-retention-lattice"></a>
1.  *Let $\pi:\mathbb Z^m\twoheadrightarrow G$ be a quotient map with $C\mathbb Z^m\subseteq\ker\pi$. For any set $\mathcal D$ of positive layers of $G$, there is a lattice $K_{\mathcal D}$, fixed by $\pi,G,\mathcal D$, such that $$\label{eq:path-retention-lattice}
     C\mathbb Z^m\subseteq K_{\mathcal D}\subseteq\mathbb Z^m,
     \qquad [\mathbb Z^m:K_{\mathcal D}]=N^{Nq(\mathcal D)},
     \quad q(\mathcal D)=\sum_{(l,j)\in\mathcal D}k_G(l,j)\nu_l.$$ If every layer in $\mathcal D$ is retained on quotienting by $\pi(v)$, then $v\in K_{\mathcal D}$.*

<a id="eq:path-tail-recursion"></a>
2.  *Suppose the extension in Equation <a href="#eq:path-extension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-extension">[eq:path-extension]</a> also has nonsingular endpoint $B$. Then $k_B(l,j)\le k_C(l,j)+h$ for every layer. Writing $\mathcal R_i$ for the retained layers in $G_{i-1}\to G_i$, for every integer $s\ge h$ we have $$\label{eq:path-tail-recursion}
     W_s(B)\le W_{s+h}(C)
       +2\sum_{i=1}^h
           \sum_{\substack{(l,j)\in\mathcal R_i\\
                            k_{G_{i-1}}(l,j)\ge s+1-h}}\nu_l.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-path-algebra"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-path-algebra</summary>

*Source and interpretation.* Part <span class="nodecor">(i)</span> is the Retention lattice lemma <a id="pm-cite-44"></a>[[SymmetricRate](#ref-SymmetricRate), Lemma 7.4], including arbitrary, nonconsecutive sets of layers. Part <span class="nodecor">(ii)</span> is the Tail recursion lemma <a id="pm-cite-45"></a>[[SymmetricRate](#ref-SymmetricRate), Lemma 11.1] together with common-quotient interlacing <a id="pm-cite-46"></a>[[SymmetricRate](#ref-SymmetricRate), Equation (7.11)]. For the latter, projection onto the old coordinates identifies $G_h$ with the quotient of $G_B$ by the $h$ new coordinate classes. Since a one-element quotient lowers each height by at most one, $k_B\le k_{G_h}+h\le k_C+h$. The stronger recursion uses symmetry through the Symmetric kernel inequality <a id="pm-cite-47"></a>[[SymmetricRate](#ref-SymmetricRate), Lemma 7.5]; it needs no assumption on the entry distribution. All of these statements therefore apply to the present biased model. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### The cost of retaining layers

<a id="eq:path-costly-definition"></a>
Fix $L_0\ge1$, a sufficiently small fixed $e_0>0$, and the corresponding $\zeta>0$ in Proposition <a href="#prop:static-column" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:static-column">14</a>. Recall $R=\lceil\ell^2\rceil$ and $\sigma=R^{-6}$. Call an extension *costly* if, in at least one of its quotient operations, $$\label{eq:path-costly-definition}
 \sum_{(l,j)\in\mathcal R_i}\nu_l\ge\sigma.$$ We use this name only for the condition in Equation <a href="#eq:path-costly-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-costly-definition">[eq:path-costly-definition]</a>.

In all column estimates, the information already exposed fixes the start $C$, and possibly a preceding column of the same width-two extension. The next column has independent bit entries with success probabilities $p$ or $b$. The quotient map is thus fixed before the next column is tested.

<a id="stmt-lem-path-retention-cost"></a>
<div id="lem:path-retention-cost" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:path-retention-cost"></a>
**Lemma 26** (Almost full cost of retention). *Typically for nonsingular $C$ with size in $\mathcal I_N$, conditional on $C$, the probability that an independent principal extension of either width is costly is at most $$\label{eq:path-retention-cost}
 p_N:=2^{-N(a-2e_0)}.$$ For one specified column, the probability that its retained weight is at least $\sigma$ obeys the same bound, also after exposing the preceding column of a width-two extension. Also, typically, when $W_2(C)\le\zeta$, the width-two gate has conditional probability at most $p_N^2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-path-retention-cost"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-path-retention-cost</summary>

*Proof.* Impose Theorem <a href="#thm:small-intersection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:small-intersection">11</a> on $C$ with $\tau=\ell^{-20}$ and some fixed $0<\alpha<e_0$. Then $\tau=o(\sigma)$. We show that the retention condition can be covered by $2^{o(N)}$ selected layer sets, each giving a lattice of index at least $N^{\sigma N}$.

<a id="eq:path-small-prime-entropy"></a>
Let $G$ be the group before one column is tested. It is a quotient of $G_C$, so its total height times weight is at most $1/2$. At a prime $l$, let $d_l$ be its number of plateaus. Distinct positive plateau heights give $d_l^2\le2\sum_j k_G(l,j)$, and the group-order bound gives $\sum_{l,j}k_G(l,j)=O(N\log N)$. Consequently the number of plateaus at primes $l\le\sqrt N$ is $O(N^{3/4}\sqrt{\log N})$, by Cauchy–Schwarz. Each prefix length has $O(N\log N)$ possible values. Thus the complete retention pattern at these primes can be specified with $$\label{eq:path-small-prime-entropy}
 O\bigl(N^{3/4}(\log N)^{3/2}\bigr)=o(N)$$ bits. Include all those retained layers in the selected set.

If their total weight is below $\sigma$, add retained layers at primes $l>\sqrt N$ until the total first reaches $\sigma$. There are at most $N$ candidate layers in this prime range, since each has $\nu_l>1/(2N)$. At most $\lceil2\sigma N\rceil+1$ additions suffice. The binomial bound therefore contributes $2^{o(N)}$ further possibilities. This constructs the claimed cover whenever the retained weight is at least $\sigma$.

For every selected set $\mathcal D$, positive heights and Equation <a href="#eq:path-retention-lattice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-retention-lattice">[eq:path-retention-lattice]</a> give $q(\mathcal D)\ge\sigma$. The simultaneous cube estimate applies to $K_{\mathcal D}$, since it contains $C\mathbb Z^m$, even when $G$ depends on an earlier column. Each cube point has probability at most $b^m$, where $m=N-o(N)$. The one-column probability is therefore at most $$2^{o(N)}2^{\alpha N}b^m
       =2^{-N(a-\alpha-o(1))}.$$ The slack $\alpha<e_0$ absorbs a union over at most two columns and yields Equation <a href="#eq:path-retention-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-retention-cost">[eq:path-retention-cost]</a>.

Finally impose Proposition <a href="#prop:static-column" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:static-column">14</a> on the start. When $W_2(C)\le\zeta$, each column belongs to $\mathcal S_{L_0}(C)$ with probability at most $2^{e_0N}b^m$. The two cross columns are independent conditional on $C$, so the gate probability is at most $(2^{e_0N}b^m)^2\le p_N^2$. ◻

</details>

We also need a weaker estimate that applies without typicality. Its role is to prevent a path from carrying substantial weight in very high layers. The elementary counting argument below is the biased version of <a id="pm-cite-48"></a>[[SymmetricRate](#ref-SymmetricRate), Lemma 11.2].

<a id="stmt-lem-path-dissociation"></a>
<div id="lem:path-dissociation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 27** (Dissociation and a lattice constraint). *Let $C$ be a nonsingular bit matrix of size $m\le N$, and let $C\mathbb Z^m\subseteq K\subseteq\mathbb Z^m$ have index $N^{qN}$. If $v$ has independent bit coordinates, each with maximum atom at most $b$, then <a id="eq:path-fixed-mask-cost"></a>$$\label{eq:path-fixed-mask-cost}
 \mathbb P(v\in K)\le b^{2qN/3}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-path-dissociation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-path-dissociation</summary>

*Proof.* Write $g_1,\ldots,g_m$ for the coordinate images in $\mathbb Z^m/K$. Choose a maximal set of $j$ of these images whose subset sums are all distinct. Such a set is called *dissociated*. Once the other bits are fixed, at most one assignment of these $j$ bits gives zero in the quotient, and that assignment has probability at most $b^j$.

Maximality says that every $g_i$ is a combination of the selected images with coefficients in $\{-1,0,1\}$: otherwise adding it would preserve distinct subset sums. Put these coefficients in a $j$-by-$m$ integer matrix $M$, using identity columns at the selected indices. Then $\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}M=\mathop{\mathrm{rank}}(MC)=j$, and evaluation of the selected images is a surjection $\theta:\mathbb Z^j\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb Z^m/K$. Every column of $MC$ lies in $\ker\theta$. Choose $j$ independent such columns; each has norm at most $N\sqrt j$. The index of their integer span bounds the quotient order, so Hadamard’s inequality gives $$N^{qN}\le(N\sqrt j)^j\le N^{3j/2}.$$ Thus $j\ge2qN/3$, proving the assertion. If $j=0$, the quotient is trivial and the same conclusion holds with empty products. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:path-heavy-weight"></a>
For one column with pre-test group $G$, write $$\label{eq:path-heavy-weight}
 Q_{\rm hi}=\sum_{\substack{(l,j)\text{ retained}\\k_G(l,j)\ge R-1}}
                     k_G(l,j)\nu_l.$$

<a id="stmt-lem-path-heavy-cost"></a>
<div id="lem:path-heavy-cost" class="lemma">

**Lemma 28** (Cost of heavy retained layers). *Fix $0<\eta<1$, and put $q_{\min}=\eta/(100\log N)$. There is a constant $c_p>0$ such that, uniformly for $q_{\min}\le q\le1/2$, <a id="eq:path-heavy-cost"></a>$$\label{eq:path-heavy-cost}
 \mathbb P\bigl(Q_{\rm hi}\in[q,2q)\mid\text{previous exposure}\bigr)
       \le2^{-c_pqN}.$$ This holds whenever the previous exposure fixes a nonsingular start $C$ of size in $\mathcal I_N$, and a quotient map to $G$ whose kernel contains $C\mathbb Z^m$, while leaving the tested bits independent with success probabilities $p$ or $b$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-path-heavy-cost"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-path-heavy-cost</summary>

*Proof.* For a fixed set $\mathcal D$ of retained layers, the retention lattice and Lemma <a href="#lem:path-dissociation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:path-dissociation">27</a> give cost $2^{-(2a/3)q(\mathcal D)N}$. Enumerate the sets consisting of all retained layers of height at least $R-1$, with their total height times weight in $[q,2q)$.

For primes at most $\sqrt N$, prefix lengths on the eligible plateaus specify the set. Equation <a href="#eq:path-small-prime-entropy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-small-prime-entropy">[eq:path-small-prime-entropy]</a> costs $$O\bigl(N^{3/4}(\log N)^{3/2}\bigr)
       =o(N/\log N)=o_\eta(qN)$$ bits uniformly over the stated range. At larger primes each eligible layer contributes at least $(R-1)/(2N)$. There are therefore $O(N/R)$ eligible layers in total, and a set of weight below $2q$ contains at most $O(qN/R)$ of them. The binomial estimate, or the all-subsets bound when $q$ is bounded below, gives logarithmic count $$O\!\left(\frac{qN}{R}\bigl(1+\log(1/q)\bigr)\right)
       =o_\eta(qN).$$ Indeed $1+\log(1/q)=O_\eta(\ell)$, $R\asymp\ell^2$, and $qN/R\to\infty$ uniformly, so rounding has no effect. Every counted set has $q(\mathcal D)\ge q$. Its probability cost absorbs both entropy bounds; for instance any fixed $0<c_p<2a/3$ works for all sufficiently large $N$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### Fixed paths and removal of high layers

<a id="eq:path-count"></a>
We now turn the column estimates into a bound on terminal matrices. Let $$H_0=2\lceil N^{3/5}\rceil,\qquad m_*=N-1.$$ Consider all fixed index paths <a id="eq:path-index-path"></a>$$\label{eq:path-index-path}
 I_0\subset I_1\subset\cdots\subset I_{H_0}=[m_*],
 \qquad h_t=|I_t\setminus I_{t-1}|\in\{1,2\}.$$ Fix an ordering of each pair of new indices. At a backward step there are at most $2N^2$ choices, so the number of paths is at most $$\label{eq:path-count}
 (2N^2)^{H_0}=2^{O(N^{3/5}\log N)}=2^{o(N)}.$$ Every node size belongs to $\mathcal I_N$, since at most $2H_0=o(N^{7/10})$ indices are deleted.

Let $\mathcal T_m$ be the set of nonsingular size-$m$ matrices satisfying Lemma <a href="#lem:prime-corank" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:prime-corank">7</a>, Proposition <a href="#prop:static-column" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:static-column">14</a>, and the simultaneous cube property used in Lemma <a href="#lem:path-retention-cost" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:path-retention-cost">26</a>. Their exceptional probabilities are uniformly $2^{-\omega(N)}$. Every fixed principal submatrix remains in the bit model: its upper-triangular entries are independent with the prescribed biases, and its mean is a principal submatrix of the original mean, hence has rank at most two. A union bound using Equation <a href="#eq:path-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-count">[eq:path-count]</a> shows that the probability of any nonsingular node on any path failing these requirements is still $2^{-\omega(N)}$.

For one fixed path, write $B_t=B[I_t]$, and let $\mathcal A$ be the event that all its nodes belong to their respective $\mathcal T_m$ and every width-two gate holds. Lemma <a href="#lem:path-deletion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:path-deletion">24</a>, applied backwards $H_0$ times, shows that every nonsingular terminal matrix outside the exceptional event belongs to $\mathcal A$ for at least one fixed path.

For the probability estimates, expose a fixed path *forwards*: first $B_0$, then the cross columns and new symmetric block of each extension. Conditional on everything already exposed, a cross column has independent entries of the original prescribed biases. The second cross column of a pair remains independent after the first has been revealed. Future typicality, nonsingularity and gate requirements enter only through the event $\mathcal A$.

Call the last $5R$ steps, together with their $5R+1$ nodes, the *terminal window*. We first remove paths with a large $W_R$ anywhere in that window.

<a id="stmt-lem-path-high-tail"></a>
<div id="lem:path-high-tail" class="lemma">

**Lemma 29** (Small high-layer tails on the window). *For every fixed $0<\eta<1$, each fixed path satisfies <a id="eq:path-high-tail"></a>$$\label{eq:path-high-tail}
 \mathbb P\!\left(\mathcal A,
       \max_{H_0-5R\le t\le H_0} W_R(B_t)>\eta\right)
       \le2^{-\Omega_{p,\eta}(NR)}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-path-high-tail"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-path-high-tail</summary>

<a id="eq:path-heavy-total-moment"></a>
*Proof.* At each tested column form $Q_{\rm hi}$ from Equation <a href="#eq:path-heavy-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-heavy-weight">[eq:path-heavy-weight]</a>, and put $$\widetilde Q=
 \begin{cases}
 Q_{\rm hi},&Q_{\rm hi}\ge q_{\min},\\
 0,&Q_{\rm hi}<q_{\min},
 \end{cases}
 \qquad q_{\min}=\frac{\eta}{100\log N}.$$ Set $\widetilde Q=0$ for every cross column of a step with singular start. This defines the quantities on the entire exposure space. At nonsingular starts, $Q_{\rm hi}\le1/2$. For dyadic values $q_j=2^jq_{\min}\le1/2$, Lemma <a href="#lem:path-heavy-cost" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:path-heavy-cost">28</a> gives, with any fixed $0<c'_p<c_p/2$, <a id="eq:path-heavy-one-moment"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E\!\left[2^{c'_pN\widetilde Q}\mid\text{previous exposure}\right]
 &\le 1+\sum_{j:q_j\le1/2}2^{2c'_pNq_j}2^{-c_pNq_j}\notag\\
 &\le1+2^{-\Omega_{p,\eta}(N/\log N)}.
 \label{eq:path-heavy-one-moment}
\end{aligned}$$ The same bound holds at singular starts. There are at most $2H_0$ tested columns. Successive conditioning, including between columns of a pair, yields $$\label{eq:path-heavy-total-moment}
 \mathbb E\,2^{c'_pN\sum\widetilde Q}\le1+o(1).$$

On $\mathcal A$, suppose a window node has $W_R>\eta$. Iterate Equation <a href="#eq:path-tail-recursion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-tail-recursion">[eq:path-tail-recursion]</a> backwards from that node, beginning with $s=R$ and increasing $s$ by the width at each step. Stop once $s>N^{3/5}$. This takes at most $\lceil N^{3/5}\rceil$ steps; even the earliest window node has $H_0-5R>\lceil N^{3/5}\rceil$ predecessors for large $N$. The final tail vanishes because Lemma <a href="#lem:prime-corank" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:prime-corank">7</a> bounds every layer height by $N^{3/5}$.

Number these backwards steps by $j=1,2,\ldots$. At step $j$, the threshold in the recursion is $R$ plus the preceding widths, so every charged layer has height at least $$s+1-h\ge R+j-2\ge R-1.$$ The contribution from one column is therefore at most $2Q_{\rm hi}/(R+j-2)$. There are at most two columns per step. All contributions with $Q_{\rm hi}<q_{\min}$ total at most $$4q_{\min}\sum_j\frac1{R+j-2}
       \le4q_{\min}(1+\log N)<\eta/2.$$ The remaining contributions must exceed $\eta/2$. Their denominators are at least $R-1$, and consequently <a id="eq:path-heavy-forced-sum"></a>$$\label{eq:path-heavy-forced-sum}
 \sum\widetilde Q\ge\frac{(R-1)\eta}{4},$$ where the sum may be extended to all tests on the fixed path. This same necessary condition holds for a violation at any window node. Markov’s inequality and Equation <a href="#eq:path-heavy-total-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-heavy-total-moment">[eq:path-heavy-total-moment]</a> prove the claim. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### The terminal window forces two constraints

Choose a fixed $0<\eta<\min\{1,\zeta/4\}$. The preceding lemma leaves only windows with $W_R\le\eta$ at every node. On such a window, the following deterministic argument is the reason that two full column costs suffice.

<a id="stmt-lem-path-window"></a>
<div id="lem:path-window" class="lemma">

**Lemma 30** (The terminal-window alternative). *Consider $5R$ consecutive nonsingular principal extensions of widths one or two, satisfying their gates. Suppose all their nodes have $W_R\le\eta$ and the terminal node has $W_2>\zeta$. For all sufficiently large $N$, either at least two distinct steps are costly, or a width-two step starts at a node with $W_2\le\zeta$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-path-window"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-path-window</summary>

<a id="eq:path-noncostly"></a>
*Proof.* At a noncostly step of width $h$, the recursion gives $$\label{eq:path-noncostly}
 W_s(B)\le W_{s+h}(C)+4\sigma
       \qquad(s\ge h).$$ Iterate this inequality from $s=2$ backwards over any block of $R$ noncostly steps. The threshold increases by at least $R$, so the endpoint satisfies $$W_2\le\delta,\qquad \delta:=\eta+4R\sigma.$$ Here and below $\delta<\zeta$ for large $N$.

Label the window nodes $X_0,\ldots,X_{5R}$. If there are no costly steps, the last $R$ steps contradict the terminal value. Suppose there is exactly one costly step, at position $t$. There must be fewer than $R$ steps after it, or the same argument again applies. Thus $t\ge4R+1$. Both $X_{t-2}$ and $X_{t-1}$ end blocks of $R$ noncostly steps inside the window, and hence both have $W_2\le\delta$.

Assume there is no width-two step whose starting $W_2$ is at most $\zeta$. The two steps at positions $t-1,t$ must then both have width one. Applying Equation <a href="#eq:path-noncostly" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-noncostly">[eq:path-noncostly]</a> with $s=1$ to the preceding step gives $$W_1(X_{t-1})\le W_2(X_{t-2})+4\sigma\le\delta+4\sigma.$$ Even though step $t$ is costly, its width is one, so the height comparison in Lemma <a href="#lem:path-algebra" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:path-algebra">25</a> gives $$W_2(X_t)\le W_1(X_{t-1})\le\delta+4\sigma.$$ The remaining steps are noncostly. For either width, Equation <a href="#eq:path-noncostly" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-noncostly">[eq:path-noncostly]</a> with $s=2$ and monotonicity of $W_s$ in $s$ show that each increases $W_2$ by at most $4\sigma$. There are fewer than $R$ such steps, whence $$W_2(X_{5R})\le\eta+8R\sigma<\zeta,$$ a contradiction. Figure <a href="#fig:terminal-window" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:terminal-window">2</a> records the two adjacent width-one steps used in this argument. ◻

</details>

<figure id="fig:terminal-window">

<figcaption>The contradiction setup in Lemma <a href="#lem:path-window" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:path-window">30</a>: suppose the window has only one costly step and no width-two step starts with $W_{2} ≤ ζ$. The costly step is within $R$ steps of the end. Each of its two preceding nodes ends an $R$-step noncostly block, and the displayed adjacent steps must have width one. The lower chain enlarges these adjacent steps; horizontal spacing is schematic. The labels are upper bounds propagated by the proof, not a plot of a matrix statistic. Here $δ = η + 4Rσ$.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-terminal-window"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:terminal-window`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:


<details id="proof-stmt-lem-path-window-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-path-window</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:column-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:column-bound">5</a>.* Outside a set of probability $2^{-\omega(N)}$, every nonsingular terminal matrix belongs to a required fixed-path event $\mathcal A$. By Lemma <a href="#lem:path-high-tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:path-high-tail">29</a> and Equation <a href="#eq:path-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-count">[eq:path-count]</a>, we may also exclude, at the same scale, all such path events having $W_R>\eta$ anywhere in their windows.

For a fixed remaining path with terminal $W_2>\zeta$, Lemma <a href="#lem:path-window" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:path-window">30</a> forces either two costly positions or a width-two gate with starting $W_2\le\zeta$. To make the conditional probability calculation explicit, let $\mathcal F_t$ contain all entries exposed through step $t$. Define $D_t$ to be the event that the starting matrix is in its typical set and step $t$ is costly. Its starting-matrix indicator is $\mathcal F_{t-1}$-measurable, and Lemma <a href="#lem:path-retention-cost" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:path-retention-cost">26</a> gives $$\mathbb E[\mathbf 1_{\{D_t\}}\mid\mathcal F_{t-1}]\le p_N.$$ For any two positions $r<t$, including consecutive ones, <a id="eq:path-two-positions"></a>$$\label{eq:path-two-positions}
 \mathbb P(D_r\cap D_t)
 =\mathbb E\!\left[\mathbf 1_{\{D_r\}}
       \mathbb E[\mathbf 1_{\{D_t\}}\mid\mathcal F_{t-1}]\right]
 \le p_N\mathbb P(D_r)\le p_N^2.$$ For a specified width-two position, the event of a typical start with $W_2\le\zeta$ and a satisfied gate likewise has probability at most $p_N^2$. These bounds require no conditioning on how a backwards path was selected or on any future path requirement: we upper-bound by the stated events after dropping all other conditions.

There are only $O(R^2)$ pairs of positions or individual gate positions in the window. Taking the union over these and all fixed paths gives $$\mathbb P\{B\text{ nonsingular},\ W_2(B)>\zeta\}
 \le2^{-\omega(N)}+2^{o(N)}p_N^2
 =2^{-\omega(N)}+2^{-2N(a-2e_0)+o(N)}.$$ If $W_2(B)\le\zeta$, Proposition <a href="#prop:static-column" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:static-column">14</a> gives $|\mathcal S_{L_0}(B)|\le2^{e_0N}$, apart from another typical exception. For all sufficiently large $N$, the displayed bound and these exceptions are at most $2^{-N(2a-10e_0)}$, as required. ◻

</details>

```json
  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "Layers for a primary group with cyclic exponents $(5,4,3,1)$. The column heights are $(4,3,3,2,1)$. $W_{2}$ and $J_{B}$ count selected squares, each of weight $ν_{l}$, while $T_{2}$ counts each height-two column once. The four dark squares above the dashed cutoff contribute $4ν_{l}$ to $W_{2}$. The orange column is the unique layer of height exactly two, contributing $ν_{l}$ to $T_{2}$. The first three columns have total weight $10ν_{l}$ and contribute that amount to $J_{B}$.",
      "figure_id": "fig:layers",
      "pdf_page": 18,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:layers",
          "exact_quote": "Layers for a primary group with cyclic exponents\n \\((5,4,3,1)\\). The column heights are \\((4,3,3,2,1)\\).\n \\(W_2\\) and \\(J_B\\) count selected squares, each of weight\n \\(\\nu_l\\), while \\(T_2\\) counts each height-two column once.\n The four dar",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=0.72cm,y=0.66cm,font=\\small]\n \\definecolor{layertail}{RGB}{37,111,122}\n \\definecolor{layertwo}{RGB}{227,166,70}\n \\foreach \\j/\\height in {1/4,2/3,3/3,4/2,5/1} {\n  \\foreach \\i in {1,...,\\height} {\n   \\fill[black!8] (\\j-1,\\i-1) rectangle (\\j,\\i);\n   \\ifnum\\i>2\n    \\fill[layertail!75] (\\j-1,\\i-1) rectangle (\\j,\\i);\n   \\fi\n   \\ifnum\\j=4\n    \\fill[layertwo!80] (\\j-1,\\i-1) rectangle (\\j,\\i);\n   \\fi\n   \\draw[white,line width=1pt] (\\j-1,\\i-1) rectangle (\\j,\\i);\n  }\n  \\node[below] at (\\j-0.5,0) {\\(\\j\\)};\n  \\node[above] at (\\j-0.5,\\height+0.05) {\\(\\height\\)};\n }\n \\draw[densely dashed,black!65] (-0.3,2)--(5.3,2);\n \\node[anchor=east] at (-0.4,2) {\\(s=2\\)};\n \\node[anchor=east] at (-0.4,4.7) {height};\n \\node at (2.5,-0.9) {layer index \\(j\\)};\n \\draw[layertail,thick] (1.5,2.6)--(6.1,4.0);\n \\node[anchor=west,align=left] at (6.25,4.0)\n   {tail above two:\\\\ \\(W_2=4\\nu_l\\)};\n \\draw[layertwo!80!black,thick] (3.6,1.75)--(6.1,2.15);\n \\node[anchor=west,align=left] at (6.25,2.15)\n   {height exactly two:\\\\ \\(T_2=\\nu_l\\)};\n \\node[anchor=west,align=left] at (6.25,0.2)\n   {full first three columns:\\\\ \\(J_B=10\\nu_l\\)};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A Young-diagram-like grid of unit squares arranged in 5 columns. Horizontal axis is labeled \"layer index $j$\" with column indices 1, 2, 3, 4, 5. Vertical axis is labeled \"height\" with integer levels 1, 2, 3, 4. A horizontal dashed line at height 2 is labeled \"$s = 2$\". Column 1 has height 4 with squares numbered 1, 2, 3, 4 from bottom to top; columns 2 and 3 have height 3 with squares numbered 1, 2, 3; column 4 has height 2 with squares numbered 1, 2; column 5 has height 1 with square numbered 1. The four squares strictly above $s=2$ (two in column 1, one in column 2, one in column 3) are shaded dark teal, with an arrow pointing to the label \"tail above two: $W_2 = 4\\nu_l$\". Column 4 is shaded orange, with an arrow pointing to the label \"height exactly two: $T_2 = \\nu_l$\". A horizontal bracket spans columns 1, 2, and 3 below the grid, pointing to the label \"full first three columns: $J_B = 10\\nu_l$\"."
    },
    {
      "caption": "The contradiction setup in Lemma 30: suppose the window has only one costly step and no width-two step starts with $W_{2} ≤ ζ$. The costly step is within $R$ steps of the end. Each of its two preceding nodes ends an $R$-step noncostly block, and the displayed adjacent steps must have width one. The lower chain enlarges these adjacent steps; horizontal spacing is schematic.           "source_object": "fig:0002"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1cm,y=1cm,>=Stealth,font=\\small]\n \\draw[gray!65] (0,1.75)--(12.8,1.75);\n \\draw (0,1.64)--(0,1.86) node[above=4pt] {$X_0$};\n \\draw (12.8,1.64)--(12.8,1.86) node[above=4pt] {$X_{5R}$};\n \\draw (11,1.64)--(11,1.86) node[above=4pt] {$X_t$};\n \\node[above] at (6.4,1.9) {terminal window: $5R$ steps};\n \\draw[<->,gray!80!black] (11,1.4)--(12.8,1.4)\n       node[midway,below] {$<R$ steps};\n \\node[draw,rounded corners=2pt,align=center,inner sep=6pt,\n       fill=blue!4] (left) at (2.0,0)\n       {$X_{t-2}$\\\\$W_2\\le\\delta$};\n \\node[draw,rounded corners=2pt,align=center,inner sep=6pt,\n       fill=blue!4] (middle) at (5.8,0)\n       {$X_{t-1}$\\\\$W_1\\le\\delta+4\\sigma$};\n \\node[draw,rounded corners=2pt,align=center,inner sep=6pt,\n       fill=orange!7] (right) at (9.6,0)\n       {$X_t$\\\\$W_2\\le\\delta+4\\sigma$};\n \\draw[->,thick] (left)--(middle)\n       node[midway,above=15pt,align=center] {noncostly\\\\width one};\n \\draw[->,thick] (middle)--(right)\n       node[midway,above=15pt,align=center] {only costly step\\\\width one};\n \\draw[densely dashed,gray!65] (11,1.65)--(right.north);\n \\node[below=8pt,align=center] at (left.south)\n       {preceding $R$ steps\\\\are noncostly};\n \\node[below=8pt,align=center] at (middle.south)\n       {also $W_2\\le\\delta$ from\\\\its preceding $R$ steps};\n \\draw[->,thick] (right.east)--(12.65,0)\n       node[pos=.58,below=5pt,align=center] {noncostly\\\\steps};\n \\node[align=center,anchor=north] at (11.4,-1.0)\n       {$W_2(X_{5R})\\le\\eta+8R\\sigma$\\\\$<\\zeta$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Schematic diagram showing two levels of representation. Top level: a horizontal segment labeled \"terminal window: $5R$ steps\" delimited by tick marks at $X_0$ (left) and $X_{5R}$ (right), with an intermediate point $X_t$ near $X_{5R}$ and a bracket spanning from $X_t$ to $X_{5R}$ labeled \"$< R$ steps\". Dashed lines connect this upper timeline to an enlarged lower chain consisting of three rounded rectangular nodes: $X_{t-2}$ containing \"$X_{t-2}$ / $W_2 \\le \\delta$\", $X_{t-1}$ containing \"$X_{t-1}$ / $W_1 \\le \\delta + 4\\sigma$\", and $X_t$ containing \"$X_t$ / $W_2 \\le \\delta + 4\\sigma$\". An incoming horizontal arrow enters $X_{t-2}$ from the left, annotated below with \"preceding $R$ steps are noncostly\". The arrow from $X_{t-2}$ to $X_{t-1}$ is annotated above with \"noncostly / width one\". Below $X_{t-1}$ is the text \"also $W_2 \\le \\delta$ from its preceding $R$ steps\". The arrow from $X_{t-1}$ to $X_t$ is annotated above with \"only costly step / width one\". An outgoing arrow leaves $X_t$ to the right, annotated with \"noncostly steps / $W_2(X_{5R}) \\le \\eta + 8R\\sigma < \\zeta$\"."
    }
```
